BOOK MANUFACTURERS RAHWAY, NEW JERSEY

BEFORE YOU READ 

IT would have been more natural for me, in a book on dude ranching, to sit down and discuss things: to describe some of the problems that will arise in getting started; to tell you about some of the details of operation; to suggest the sort of personality that will fit the life.

These things are here, but they are built into a story because, well, because the editor didn't want a drowsy monologue. If you think a story about the West should be full of holdups and stampedes and gun play, you would better pass right on to Hollywood. The West of ranching and dude ranching would disappoint you.

Some dude ranches, I believe, do try to provide excitement in the movie style. But if you ever run a dude ranch of your own, you will find that the real excitement is connected with the first good grass in spring, the rain that ends a drought, the home-bred filly that turns out better than her dam, the reappearance of some dude you thought might not have liked your place and that great day when you are finally sure that this is the life you were cut out for. 

This last is important. The ranch country has been so unscrupulously played up as a stage for adventure and "romance" that there are thousands of people (I have letters from dozens of them) who think it would be such fun to give up an every-day job and plunge into dude ranching. It would be, and it is—and there is

still plenty of room for the right person. But if you expect to go to bed every night with a new notch on your pistol butt or a new rustler strung up to the hangin' tree, you're not the right person; you're kidding yourself.

The places and people in this book are imaginary. 

Mr. and Mrs. Jap Wyman-and Cruzad—are the only exceptions. 

The stallion Cruzad, whom I had for a year after Mr. Wyman had sent him back to the Remount, is the sire of one of our best mares.

I met Mr. and Mrs. Wyman once on a mission similar to the one that takes Phil to their ranch. It is my misfortune that I never knew Mr. Wyman until his last illness. But, even under the painful circumstances, our brief meeting gave me something that will always be important in my life with horses. I am warmly grateful for Mrs. Wyman's permission to refer to her and her husband and their ranch. 

Ralston Creek Ranch, Golden, Colorado.

STEVE STRANG.

DUDE RANCHER

1.  

WHEN Snip's shuffling trot ended with his nose at the barbed-wire gate, Phil swung to the ground and fumbled with the awkward fastener. On the other side he got the lever properly hooked over the wire again and walked the remaining fifty yards to Pop Wakefield's cabin. Strange, he thought, the way every gate was a different sort of puzzle. Why didn't somebody invent a standard catch that would cost about a dollar and save all this diddling with rusty loops and clumsy sticks? 

Along the path five sad turkey poults left their languid search for grasshoppers in the sweet clover and slipped off into the dusty weeds. The side door of the cabin hung open to the sun, and a light stem of smoke poised above the chimney, but Phil saw no other sign that the place was lived in. "Hey!" he shouted and, thinking of unsociable dogs, moved around to the near side of the old horse, ready to mount.

From a long row of sheds west of the cabin came the sound of a tool being laid on a bench, and a face of the color and finish of a well kept cavalry boot popped out one of the open doors. "Are you Mr. Wakefield?" Phil asked as he led Snip toward the head, which now appeared attached to a slight little body with square shoulders and long, relaxed arms.

For several seconds the face said nothing, but looked 

Phil over: his new high-heeled boots; waist-length blue 1

DUDE RANCHER 

dungarees; open leather vest and light-blue work-shirt, the V at its throat framing a bright patch of fresh sunburn. "No, I ain't Mister Wakefield," clipped a pair of tight, thin lips that scarcely moved as they spoke; and, glaring from under heavy white brows, the man spun around toward the shed. "But I thought-" Phil began. "You don't look like you thought much, young feller. 

I said I ain't Mister Wakefield. Now you go on back down the crik just like you come up, turn right when you git to the end o' my road, and you might find Mister

Wakefield at that there fancy outfit you'll come up with after couple, three miles. Expect he ain't there, though; most likely up to Mineral." And, as if shocked with his own garrulity, he snapped around again and went quickly into the shed. Before Phil could speak, the head popped out again. "No, I'm only Pop Wakefield, an' you better get goin', er else you'll soak that bran' new 

Stetson you got on." 

Phil could see in the throbbing sunlight no threat of this, and he would not have minded having it soaked any way, since most of the hats he had seen out here were crown-wilted and weathered. "But it's you I came to see," he said hastily, before the head could disappear. "Mr. Wakefield, I mean your son, sent me over; said the sign doesn't look good for the twentieth, and you may want to see him."

Pop's eyes narrowed for just a moment; his jaw muscles tightened into little bunches, then relaxed, and his lips formed something not quite a smile and not quite a sneer. His gray eyes shot a look, first at Phil,

then in the direction of the Square Slash, and when he spoke there was in his voice a note of taut exasperation. "Can't run his own shebang. Can't do a thing fer hisself. 

Helpless dudes, 'n worthless plugs, 'n hired slobs to herd 'em! Fences down; rust on the mower; willows takin' the meadow!" He turned to Phil. "You make tracks back there; tell him I'll be there 'fore the twentieth, but he'd better keep you yawpin', screechin' halfwits out o' my way, else I might forgit my manners. 

Now get!"

In spite of warnings about what he might run into, Phil was irritated and resentful. But his comeback was cut short by a low roll of thunder over the mountains to the west and the simultaneous slamming of Pop's shed door. Absent-mindedly he mounted Snip and had to get off almost at once to fiddle with the gate again. 

Looking back once from down the road, he saw Pop darting about among the weeds in front of the cabin and guessed that he was trying to round up the turkey poults before the rain.

For a mile and a half Pop's road followed the steep course of the little creek through aspen and cottonwood bottoms down to its junction with the Rebel River. But 

Phil had not gone half that far when the largest drops of rain he had ever seen, the first he had seen in this country, began plopping heavily on the brim of his hat and smacking him coldly on the shoulders. Snip, whose normal gait was such that the boys at the ranch said you had to drive stakes along his path to tell whether he was moving, now shuffled uneasily ahead and broke into a lumbering gallop. 

When a cloudburst catches you in the mountains, you

DUDE RANCHER 

are miserable at first as each cold, monstrous drop slaps the warm skin of your back. Then, when you are pretty wet but not thoroughly soaked, your clothes stick to you and come loose with each stride of your horse. But finally they stop shifting and settle as cozily into your curves and creases as the last spring snow into the folds of the hills. Then you do not mind; you are simply wet, and you begin to enjoy the freshened color of the sage; the slick, bright green of the aspen leaves; the deepened red of earth and rock. On the slopes above you the pine and spruce, no longer standing away from blue, contrasting shadows, melt to a solid bank of the deepest opaque green. And from plant and earth come smells too tenuous to be caught when the air is dry. 

With the exercise of riding at a gallop Phil was warmed and exhilarated; only the fronts of his thighs were numb, where the breeze of his forward motion hit them full. But Snip was overdue for a shoeing job, and when a horse is shod with uncaulked plates and his feet are too long, a little rain does funny things to his footing. In wearing down, the inside heel of one front shoe had spread slightly beyond the hoof, so that in shifting his weight to catch his balance after a little slip, he caught the projecting heel with the other foot, lost his rhythm, banged one knee against the ground, and brought up sharp, shuddering with the horse's dread of a fall. 

Phil let himself down off Snip's ears to see what damage had been done. A long scratch on the knee told that the impact had been light, glancing, and probably not serious; but the half of the shoe with the projecting heel was bent down out of its plane, so that Snip could

not even stand comfortably. Phil lifted the leg and wrenched lightly at the shoe but saw that he could not get it off without danger of tearing out the clinches and damaging the hoof on the side that was still fast. Having no tools, he could not raise the clinches so as to remove the shoe properly. There was nothing to do but pick up a rock and bang the twisted side back as close to the hoof as possible. But it was still half a mile down to where Pop's road joined the state road, and then three and one-half miles along it to the ranch. Pop's cabin was about a mile behind; and so, in spite of his knowledge of the welcome he would probably receive, Phil turned back, leading Snip through the pounding rain. 

At the first crossing of the creek a sizable flow of muddy water, topped with white foam and floating twigs, churned down the stream bed, where there had been only a clear trickle the first time up. And when he reached the last crossing, a quarter of a mile from Pop's gate, Phil almost lost his footing in the current and Snip would not attempt it until Phil got on his back and kicked him through. Phil knew by the heavy smoke that rolled from the chimney, languidly down along the shingles, that Pop was inside and had recently built up the fire. 

There was no need to knock; the side door whirled in, and Pop stood there with just the expression Phil had known he would wear. Neither spoke at once, but finally Pop's middle began to shake with a convulsion that gradually spread upward to his face, and he laughed, noiselessly at first, then with a rising whinny through his gleaming, perfect teeth.

Since he knew how he must look, Phil could do noth-

DUDE RANCHER 

ing but laugh, too, rather weakly, and his laugh was broken by a shiver that started from his knees. "Here, you," Pop spoke at last, reaching his slicker down from just inside the door, "you come in an' set by the stove. I'll put that critter o' yours out in the barn, though my team'll prob❜ly die o' shame, bein' under the same roof with him." "Thank you, Mr. Wake- I mean, sir, I'd like to come in and get dry; but I want to get this shoe off first. That's why I came back; didn't want to lead him home that way. Could you just show me where your shop is?"

Pop looked quickly at Phil with a puzzled expression but said nothing. He led the way along the row of sheds, got a hammer and sickle section, then continued rapidly to the barn, which was beyond the last shed and projected southward, so as to form with the sheds a right angle open to the south and east. He and Phil and 

Snip passed through the curtain of water that was tumbling off the eaves. Once inside, Phil reached for the tools; but Pop did not appear to notice his gesture. He lifted the horse's foot, drove the edge of the sickle section under the clinches to raise them, and deftly whipped off the shoe with one hand. He straightened his back then and looked at Phil. "Them shoes been on so long they're gonna be lonesome fer the old bronc," he remarked. "Wonder you didn't kill the horse 'n break your own fool neck. Now you git back in the house 'n I'll boil you up a pot o' coffee."

Sitting naked in front of the stove, with his clothes steaming around him, Phil watched the preparations for coffee and tried to keep his long legs out of the way.

"You didn't seem to want me around a while ago," he ventured in a puzzled voice, and looked out of the corner of his eye to observe the effect. "Can't say I want you now. But, Judas Priest, you come back here cold an' wet. Can't chase you out like

I done before. Anyhow, you led the horse back to git that shoe off him. Most dudes woulda rode him on in like a automobile with a busted tire that ain't worth changin'." "Well, you see, I'm not a dude, not exactly, that is. I have a job at the ranch. I'm supposed to ride with the more inexperienced ones and see that they don't get into trouble. And I really think you're wrong about them, uh, Pop. Most of them think a lot of the horses they ride and wouldn't do anything they thought was bad for them. They just don't know." "Don't know! Don't know! What difference that make? Time I took a load o' whitefaces to Chicago, they put me in the calaboose 'cause I didn't know you wasn't supposed to shoot them cats they got loose there. Don't know! What's the sense o' them leavin' their subways 'n night-clubs 'n theayters an' messin' round out here where they're just as much help to theirselves as a hog on ice? Gates used to stay shut so's a feller'd know where his heifers was, 'fore Joe 'n May come back with all their looney notions how we should slick the place up 'n get to be John D. Rockefeller. Three dry years we'd had, an' the grass was gone, or else I wouldn'ta listened. "Here, this coffee's 'bout stewed enough. Want canned cow? I ain't got no cream, this bein', as you might say, a cattle ranch."

The downpour had stopped. In the west the slaty

DUDE RANCHER 

cloud mass swiftly curdled and broke; a lower edge appeared, rose past the declining sun; and steam began to twist up from the soaking sheds. From the creek, however, came a low roar which announced a big runoff from the hills and gullies.

Phil was not surprised, nor did he argue long when Pop explained that it would be dangerous, if not impossible, to get back to the ranch until the water had gone down. "Seen a yearlin' steer, big one, July it was, just a year ago—he was stuck ten, twelve feet up in a cottonwood, had his belly jammed into the main crotch. 

Musta been part duck, 'cause he was still alive when I found him, mornin' after the rain, bawlin' his head off." "Did you get him down?" Phil asked with interest, then noticed the creative fire that began to glow in 

Pop's eye. "Didn't need to. Fed him right there. You see, the grass come up good in the bottom after the rain, an' I rigged me a rope to a pulley 'n clumb up 'n tied it over his head. Used to throw a big loop 'round a armload o' grass (cut it right there; never had to go more'n a couple rods from the tree) an' haul her up even with him 'n tie the other end o' the rope. Tree covered his brand, if he had one; so I slapped mine on where I could see it. Well, sir, he growed better'n any steer I ever see, gettin' no chance to run the fat off. Come time to sell him in the fall, an' I didn't know how I was gonna do; thought I might have to butcher him right there. But a deefnin' crash woke me up one night, an' next mornin' I went out an' found he'd growed so big he'd split that cottonwood wide open. Woulda got away,

too, maybe, only his hoofs was so long he couldn't travel. He was treemendous, front 'n back, but his middle was all wrong, 'bout five feet up 'n down, tapered like a wedge, so's he was less'n two inches thick at the bottom. Twigs asproutin' out his sides. Packer peenalized me two cents, 'count of his funny shape. Didn't matter, though; he weighed right onto nineteen hundred." "Funny thing," observed Phil, “I think that explains a mystery that has bothered me ever since last fall. Some of us ordered a big piece of sirloin for an outdoor steakfry. Well, sir, when we came to cook it, we had to dig a trench about nine feet long. It was tender, though. A friend of mine took one of the ribs home and had it made into a billiard cue." "Musta been the same brute," Pop agreed, with no change of expression, "only the butcher seen you comin' when he passed off that piece of sirloin."

Phil knew that he had become something more than an intruder on Pop's irritable privacy.

II. MARSHALL'S REBELLION 

POP kept his supplies on a bank of shelves in the same room that he used for kitchen, dining room, boudoir, and salon. In the small battery of canned goods, pork-and-beans, tomatoes, and evaporated milk bulked largest. On the bottom shelf, a table's height from the floor, were larger cans of about two gallons' capacity, with removable tops; and Phil saw that the painted black labels, Coffee, Flour, Sugar, Salt, were done in a surprisingly perfect and sophisticated lettering, surely not Pop's, he guessed. A tin container lying empty on its side told that for the moment Pop was out of bread. 

Against the wall below the shelves were part of a sack of potatoes, a corrugated paper carton with a few withered onions whose green shoots stretched toward the window, and a five-gallon kerosene can.

From this modest commissary Pop "slung together" a supper undistinguished but for the hot biscuits plastered with comb honey-honey and comb mixed together in a large glass jar. "Found me a bee tree up the gulch," Pop explained. "Smoked 'em out just 'fore dark an' split the tree open that night. Hadda strain most of it. This was clean." "Much better with the comb," said Phil. "My dad used to keep bees, and I was always the one to take care of them. They stung my brothers and sisters; didn't bother me much. I like them."

After the dishes were done and the last of the coffee 10

MARSHALL'S REBELLION 

11 had been drained from the huge pot, Pop unlocked the door leading to the unused front room of the cabin and rummaged noisily in the dark for a folding cot, a wilted mattress, and some old blankets, to which clung the vague, acrid smell the pack-rats always leave in a mountain room that is seldom opened. 

Then the two men sat on opposite sides of the table, with the lamp between them so that each was only a voice to the other, and stared at the stove. For a time, from the little crack around the stove door and through the shaker hole, came a pulsing crimson glow that played on the black iron. But, long after the glow had gone, Phil sat listening to a story of the Rebel River country which pieced out and gave perspective to all he had heard in the two weeks since Mr. Joseph Wakefield's roadster had set him down at the Square Slash Ranch. "Judas Priest!" shouted Pop at last, looking at the alarm clock hanging by a nail on the wall. “Ain't set up so late since my old mare took sick year ago last winter; an' ain't never talked so much, I guess, 'cept maybe when Molly gets home from college 'n comes up from the ranch t' see me."

In the dark Pop almost instantly began the easy, long-drawn breath of healthy sleep; but Phil was full of coffee and a turbulent jumble of mental images, so that he lay awake for a long time and wound back the reel of memory from this memorable evening: to his childhood in the big stone house on the outskirts of Abbottsville in eastern Pennsylvania.

Philip Marshall's father was a country doctor who had elected the smaller life and easy gait of Penbrook

DUDE RANCHER 

County in preference to the uncertainties and almost inevitable specialization of a city practice; for his interest in his patients went beyond their insides, and he had a damaging indifference to the magnitude of fees. 

Though he knew it cost more and limited his range, Dr. Marshall kept no car but drove a light made-toorder buggy, whose rubber tires, gleaming wire spokes, and clear varnish over the natural wood told of the owner's eccentricity and his pride. His horses were better than he could afford: one, a big chestnut mare, standard-bred, he had picked up at the trotting track in Lancaster. The other horse, Quicksilver, was halfthoroughbred, even more of a luxury, since, in spite of a good disposition, he did not go well between the shafts and needed the freedom of pasture or a bold rider, sympathetic with his bursting power and playful spirit. 

Phil, more than his brother or either of his sisters, who resented Dad's backwardness and yearned for a family motor car, grew up in the stable and in the pasture that led back to the woodlot. And when a bad fall and Mrs. Marshall's flat command forced the doctor to give up his "silly and conspicuous" gallops to the outlying patients, it was Phil who kept Quicksilver in perfect muscle and who did all the work of caring for both horses.

Though he did not force the point, the doctor was well satisfied when all his children chose to go to college. He and Mrs. Marshall did what they could to help; but the boys regarded it as natural that they should earn most of their expenses.

Phil liked his studies, liked his friends, liked his rowing (polo cost too much to be considered) and spent

MARSHALL'S REBELLION 

13 more time than he should have, playing bridge. His mind was inquiring and active and so "open" that he considered nothing as finally settled and so put off the decision about his life's work. When he awoke a graduate one June morning, he realized that his background of study in Romance languages and English literature, selected largely on the inspiration of his roommate, Stanley Howard, led just about nowhere.

Jobs were hard to get, and so Phil followed Stanley, who had no doubts, to the teaching staff of the Stocton Academy, a small private school, precariously lodged in a one-time sanatorium among the Berkshire Hills of Massachusetts. But, to the uncalculated detriment of the coming generation, the Stocton Academy expired at the end of Phil's first year, simply because of no enrollment.

Contrary to his expectation, Phil had enjoyed teaching and would have liked to get a place in another school. Stanley was appointed almost at once to a student instructorship in a large college, but Phil did not try for anything of the kind. He spent the summer in and out of Abbottsville, tried to improve Quicksilver's form over the jumps; thought wanderingly of finding or inventing work that would combine horses and teaching; and, late in August, put pressure shamelessly on all his likely college friends to get him a job of any kind.

Through the influence of his classmate, Walter Ivins, 3rd, he was taken on by the firm of Matlock, Ivins & Ivins and began to learn the art of selling bonds. His connections were not so promising as those of most of the young men in the office, and that fall and winter

DUDE RANCHER 

were bad in the Street. But Phil had plenty of energy and an easy way of meeting people, so that his superiors began to think that he had a future. Evenings, when he had time, he read scatteringly in a monstrous variety of authors, men whose names suggested something but whose work he had only sniffed in college: Thoreau, Veblen, Whitman, William Morris, Rousseau, and many others. Most of them were after an unreal escape, he rather arrogantly decided; but from all, he drew the sense that life needed to be lived; that you made a bad bargain if you spent it enduring today for the sake of tomorrow; that the dynamos had not always whirled; that the ticker's bloodless throb was not the heartbeat of the universe. 

Before he voiced it to any of his friends, before he framed it clearly to himself, he knew inwardly that the life he led was no life for him. And he more than suspected that many others who saw no "out" felt as he did.

Some young men he knew enjoyed it all from day to day. For them, fine! Many, however, hanging anxiously to a job, drawn and nervous from the daily scramble of competition, dashed by the copious evidence that there was no room at the top, looked forward to the evenings as a chance to let down and to breathe. But when the evenings came, and the week-ends, there was no energy left for any but the most passive "recreations." Parties, yes, kept afloat on a treacherous tide of cocktails. Talk, yes, hiding doubt and worry and exhaustion under a brittle mask of indifference. Sleep, not much, and not very good when at last it came. 

Even the "go-getters," stamped already with Success,

MARSHALL'S REBELLION 

15 were volubly critical of the life, while they polished with brutal openness the right contacts in business and society. And the girls-some few had jobs, of course, and they, Phil thought, were on better terms with themselves than most of the men. But the jobless majority, unless they had the luck to be satisfied with social work or courses at Columbia or puttering with the arts, were often bored and sour and mean.

Hardly a person he knew could see anything of himself in the finished product he was helping to make or finance or sell or distribute. "Well, Phil, maybe it doesn't make sense; maybe you don't like it. What are you going to do about it? You've got a job, haven't you? That's more than a lot of birds in our class. Me? No, maybe I don't think so much of it either. But, believe me, I'm going to keep my nose just a little further in than the next guy; and, by the time I'm forty-five, I'm going to pull out and leave all this and relax and have time to do all the things I want to do. And you might as well do the same, Phil, 'cause you're never going to get anywhere teaching French to a lot of prissy little blockheads; or, what's more likely from the way you rave, putting on a fake leopard skin and raising wood-violets for the dowagers along the 

Main Line. "You've got what it takes to get by right here, and you're doing it. Why kick? Think what you want to, if it amuses you; but, for the love of Common Sense, don't be ass enough to suppose that in these days your thinking has anything to do with the way you live." 

Frank Driscoll downed the last of his beer, stretched

DUDE RANCHER 

his legs under the table, and started making with his thumb a system of radiating spokes from the wet hub where the mug had stood. "Listen, Frank," Phil began patiently, after a long pause of which neither was conscious, "you may be right, in a general way, that is. And if I'm crazy in my own small way, at least I'm not preaching 'back to the land' as social doctrine. You'll give me credit for being broad-minded enough to admit that 'The automobile is here to stay.' That as long as it does stay, and a lot of other things like it, most of us will have to hang around a factory or a bank or a ticker to breed and curry and train the thing, and clean out its stall. "But not me. "I'm big and strong and willing to work; and by 'work' I mean using my muscles, as well as my bean and my irresistible sales approach. And I'm not going to insult my muscles by turning them on for an hour three afternoons a week in Flanagan's malodorous gymnasium. No, boy, I'm leaving. I'm not sure just how nor where; but I think I've got an idea.” "Wait 'til I get my face straight. All right, now." "Dude ranching.” "Dude ranching! Dude- You've never been west of 

Pennsylvania in your life. The Indians'll scalp you. 

Marshall's last stand!" "All right. Look here, Frank. I haven't the money to buy a farm in Pennsylvania, wouldn't know what to do with one, if I had. Don't think I'd like it any way: I like cabbages all right, but I like people better, all sorts of people.

MARSHALL'S REBELLION 

17 "I've been reading up about this dude ranch thing; and it seems to combine just about all the things I like: people; work and play outdoors; some farming, probably; maybe travel; selling, of course, because you've got to put your place across; swell, big country, I'll bet, not all trimmed and clipped and polished. It would be a chance to work out the perfect vacation for people who love the life but can't have it all the time. Has a lot to do with horses, too, and, just for me personally, that means something.” "Mmmmm.-Going to step around to Morgan's tomorrow and borrow a million on your clean-cut, manly face and honest brown eyes? Or maybe you'd be generous and offer 'em a partnership, eighth interest, say?" "Well, that's an idea; but why should I let them in on it? I've written to three or four places, laid it on thick, told them I'd work for nothing until they found they couldn't get along without me. If it works, I'd like to start a place of my own after I find out what it's all about. "Lots of ranches are for sale cheap all through the 

Rocky Mountain country; not big successful cattle ranches, naturally, but smaller places, where you could run all the horses you'd need and a few cattle for your own milk and beef. Loads of scenery. And from all I can gather, most deals are carried on the cuff out there; so I don't think I'd need much capital. I heard from a chap today, Square Slash, Colorado. Seems to think it might help to have someone acquainted in the East. 

Competition there, too, I guess. Owner wants to be sure of my respectability."

DUDE RANCHER 

And that was how Phil came to be sleeping across the room from Pop Wakefield in a crumbling log cabin near the head of a little creek on the eastern slope of the Colorado Rockies.

III. THE WAKEFIELDS DEAR FRANK: 

Square Slash Ranch 

RFD, Mineral Spring, Colo. 

July 16 (Sunday evening) 

The "first impressions" I promised you are getting overdue; but, even after two weeks, it may still be too early for me to set down anything that will be comprehensible to you who have never seen this country and are at outs with my aims in coming here. Things strike me with such an impact that "impressions" is hardly a strong enough word. "Jolts" might do better. But I'm going to record what I can, and it's just your hard luck to get the longest letter I ever wrote. 

There are two things that I am sure of, which set this ranch country apart from any other I have seen; they are size and contrast.

Size first. Well, the state of Colorado covers over 

100,000 square miles. That is about what you'd get if you rolled New York, Pennsylvania, and Massachusetts into one; and the other states, north, south, and west average as large or larger. I've been peeking in the 

Atlas. The mountains-instead of a few handsome, orderly ups and downs such as I remember on the road from Chambersburg to Pittsburgh-cut a jagged strip of chaos some 200 miles wide for the full length of the continent. And they don't begin suavely with a series of hospitable inclines, as I thought mountains should. No, 19

DUDE RANCHER 

they jump as abruptly out of the prairie as New York out of the Hudson. That's contrast, I suppose, rather than size, but it is what makes the size so staggering. 

Pikes Peak, which beetles on Colorado Springs from 

14,000 feet, is about the twenty-fifth highest in the state. 

When a man buys land, he doesn't bother with acres: he talks sections. The average ranch around here is 

3,000-4,000 acres, and they tell me that is small: some, though not many in Colorado, are a hundred times as large. 

Distances are deceptive: what looks like an afternoon's stroll turns out to be a two-day trip by horse, involving a climb of 7or 8,000 feet. When I was sent to "wrangle" the horses out of the north pasture, I rode half an hour before I saw a horse. By yourself, out of sight of men and animals and buildings, you can stare things into scale; but if someone rides out from behind a hill, you want to send him back to Lilliput.

Then Contrast. The north sides of the near-by mountains are covered with pine and spruce so thick you can't possibly ride through them; nothing but needles on the ground. A rabbit would have to carry his lunch to get through. Climb over the ridge to the south slope; immediately you can see for miles, and right now the color of the land comes not from the grass or the gray-pink earth but from the wild kaleidoscope of flowers. I counted sixty-one different kinds the other day; but I don't know their names, and I imagine you are just as well pleased that I don't. If you stand in the sun you broil; when you step into the shade you may want a sweater. The other day, from the top of a hill in the north pasture, I could look out over the plains south-

THE WAKEFIELDS 

21 east of Denver and see a dust storm; in view behind me was the main range, its peaks covered with snow. Between two slopes on which the only vegetation is sage and cactus and desert grass among a few distorted pines, I've found a little "gulch" (gorge, to you) where the light is green, filtered through the leaves of aspen and cottonwood. Cold water trickles over the moss and drips from the stones. Ferns and columbines, big blue-and-white ones, grow out of the black, rubbery soil.

If this is all a jumble to you, I am not sure it is much more to me. But I couldn't leave it out, for it seems to me to be a part of what I've got to try to grasp; and I am sure it is part of what gives the life here and the people their particular character. 

Now to focus on this "outfit." Speaking from the deepest ignorance, I'd say that the setup here is far from what a dude ranch might be and ought to be. Clearly, with casual visitors coming and going, the staff has to get along with all sorts: some easy and natural and personally sympathetic; some spoiled or exacting or even pretty tough. It would be a mistake, I think, to neglect and ignore your guests; and it would be even worse to hit their stride and ape their attitudes. I've seen both neglect and aping here, by different members of the staff. The right thing, it seems to me, would be to live your own life in your own way, assume that that way was attractive to your guests, and offer them every `chance to take part in it.-You have rightly deduced that 

I don't intend to make my career at this ranch. But I do hope to learn here a good many things that I'll need to know, before I push along; and my chances may be bet-

DUDE RANCHER 

ter than in a place where I could see nothing to find fault with.

Nucleus of the present Square Slash was a homestead staked in 1866 by Paul Wakefield, grandfather of my padrone. He came from Maine in the gold-rush of '59; made a fair start in the gravel of Cherry Creek, where Denver is now; went back to fight in the Civil War and got to be a major; found his claims had been "jumped" when he got back here; started upstream to locate some new ones but stumbled into this valley and decided that the top of the ground looked better than its insides probably would, and stayed to start the homestead. One of the poker players in the barber shop at Mineral

Spring told me of an old yarn they used to tell that, after the Major had got the homestead going and had sent back to Maine for his girl and increased his holdings on both sides of the cañon, he found a rich pocket of gold right on his own property but did nothing about it and never showed anyone where it was. There is no other "pay dirt" within twenty miles, and no one nowadays puts any stock in the story. 

With his own hands and the skill of the Maine woodsman, the Major built his first cabin of logs beautifully dovetailed at the corners. Part of it is still standing in a corner of the corral. Later he sent for a steam sawmill and sold lumber to the homesteaders who were beginning to settle the country and to the merchants who were racing to complete any kind of store buildings in Denver and Central City. It was a profitable way to clear the land for grazing: he used the proceeds to buy the best cattle he could find, and sold his beef retail at high prices in the boom mining towns. His first son,

THE WAKEFIELDS 

23 now an old man whom everyone knows as Pop, stayed here; the others took advantage of the Major's moderate prosperity, went east to school and college, and have never returned.

I met Pop day before yesterday afternoon, spent the night in his cabin when I was marooned by the most incredibly theatrical downpour I have even seen. (More contrast, after weeks without a cloud.) He lives by himself in a lonely gulch five miles from the main ranch house, and I was glad of the opportunity to see him, for he has a reputation for his blistering hatred of dudes.

He ordered me off his place at first but changed when I came back soaked and showed what he thought was some sense about my horse. It is possible that he is mad, as some of the present generation think, but it strikes me that he knows more about the practical details of ranching than any man I have talked with so far, and I plan to cultivate him as long as I stay here. 

He comes nearer being a real frontiersman than anyone I ever met outside a book-must be sixty-five or more, but looks a seasoned fifty and moves like a boy. 

He never went to school, learned only a mite o' readin' an' figgerin' from the Major. There was no school close enough until his younger brothers and sisters came along, and then Pop had little difficulty convincing the Major that it was too late to do anything with him. His yarns are full of epic whoppers reeled off with perfect seriousness.

He grew up on the ranch and took over the management when the Major got too old and tired. But I don't believe Pop had his father's gift for business (nor perhaps his luck, for already the boom days were past), and

DUDE RANCHER 

he must have been a Tartar at times in his personal relations. His wife was able to tone him down and make him behave, even to the dudes his son began bringing ten or twelve years ago. She died, though, a year later, and after what must have been a Homeric scene with his son, Pop packed a few duds and went off to the little cabin in the Gulch, where he has stayed ever since. He almost never shows up at the ranch, although he and his son are reasonably civil now when they meet. He raises enough cattle to pay for the few things he needs, but I suspect that he gets his chief kick out of working with his tools and prowling in the woods. About the only company he has is his granddaughter Molly, who rides over as often as she can during the summer and tries to see that the cabin is in some sort of order. He seems indifferent or averse to most human contacts, but I could tell that he is very fond of her.

To shorten things a bit, if I can, the present owner- I guess he's the owner; he's the boss any way-is Joe 

Wakefield, Pop's only scion. He ran away when he was a kid, grew up knocking around Chicago, came back later with a wife and four-year-old daughter, broke, and after a year or so talked Pop into letting him build the place into a dude ranch. But for some reason, whether the country got on her city-bred nerves or whether Joe treated her the way he does his dudes, the wife went back to Chicago a few years ago, about the time the daughter began college. I think I gathered that Pop had something to do with the arrangements, steered the daughter to college, and probably financed her.

Mr. Wakefield, Jr., is a man of about forty-five, dark and heavy-set, nervous. Pop says he used to be quite a

THE WAKEFIELDS 

25 good amateur boxer when he was a youngster in Chicago; and I suppose boxing explains his puffy left eyebrow and drooping lid, which give him a rather fierce expression. But he is agreeable enough, I guess, and seems to have been around, though he's not exactly a cosmopolitan. I haven't seen much of him, because he is away a good deal. Most of the ranch planning and management is done, outside, by a man named Buck Snagler, a product of Mineral Spring, and, inside, by a sort of professional hostess, dietician, and housekeeper, Mrs. Hinkle. Snagler is regarded as the local expert in all things equine.

The daughter Molly got here this afternoon, after a short stop with her mother in Chicago. Molly makes four generations of Wakefields who have lived on this same place. It is a most unusual thing in this country, where almost everyone you meet over fifty years old has come from Missouri or Iowa or somewhere further east. 

She's a quiet kid with wide gray eyes and the usual Northampton look of preoccupation-with nothing, I suspect.

We have ten guests now, and there will be more during the summer. They will not fill the plant, but Mr. 

W. says it is better than he expected, considering the times. Some ranches in the big dude ranch section of 

Wyoming have folded last season and this. Our guests are a varied lot, and it is fun to talk to them and keep the ball rolling. I ride with the young children who can't go out by themselves and with some of their mas when they want to come along. I don't believe I look very authoritative on, I mean "in," a western saddle yet; the things split me so badly that I think my legs

DUDE RANCHER 

are four inches longer than when I got here.

Well, Frank, that's that for the present. If I can raise the postage, I shall send this off by the next mail and may write more about dude ranching when I feel entitled to have any ideas on the subject. 

If you are at the club and happen to think of it, will you please look in my box for any mail that may have gone there. A gal who owes me a letter left for South

America before I had any idea of taking to the wilderness. 

I hope you are holding your course to windward of the bunch. Let me know. Yours,

PHIL P.S. Looks like Pop riding up the road now. He said he would be over our way an' check his shootin' iron with me so's he wouldn't be tempted beyond his strength.

IV. NEW SHOES AT LAST 

IT was after supper when Pop appeared, riding the old black mare, whose mane, worn off in front of the withers, showed that she was used for work as well as riding. In a gunnysack tied to his saddle were his own shoeing hammer and trimming knife. “Seen the shape your horse's feet was in," he said to Phil, who had come out to meet him, " 'n I knowed most o' th' others 'd be the same way. Nothin' to me, o' course, but I can't stand the way that Snagler lets 'em go; and, bein' as I was comin' to see Joe anyhow, thought I'd look 'em over. 

What's the matter with Slim? Can't he tack on a plate no more?"

Pop referred to Slim Satterly, late aide-de-camp to the ornamental but unlaborious Buck. His duty it had been to tend to things so technical and inelegant as shoeing and keeping saddles patched up for use. Without stopping to explain that Slim had left ten days before, following a considerable ruckus with Snagler, Phil informed the old man of his granddaughter's arrival and went to play bridge with Mr. and Mrs. Wedderspoon from Cleveland and Mrs. Oakes from Boston.

Later he strolled out into the cool dark by himself and heard the voices of Pop and Molly where they sat on a bench built against one of the empty cabins. “Is that Mr. Marshall?" Molly asked as Phil was passing by. "You're very conscientious, aren't you? How was the bridge?" 27

DUDE RANCHER 

"I enjoyed it," Phil said and took Molly's question as an invitation to pause. "The Wedderspoons are keen players; but Mrs. Oakes and I made up in originality and daring what we lacked in technique. Held them to a few hundred points. She says Culbertson couldn't foresee every situation, or it wouldn't be a game. Bridge or not, what she plays is a game. Good egg, she is.” "Did you see what Connie was doing?" Molly asked with a touch of concern. "She must have been bored after working on her poem all day. I suppose I should have been entertaining her, since you were involved in bridge." "Oh, she was all right," Phil answered. "Sat between

Mrs. W. and me; quarreled in a sisterly way with Mrs. 

W. and pretended she was learning about bridge from my play. Said she supposed an old maid has to learn bridge to avoid becoming too much of an introvert. It sounded both silly and appealing.-I wonder what's on her mind." “Her mind's all right," Molly explained. "Mrs. Wedderspoon tells me it's her heart that's affected. Little 

Sister gets it at least once every spring and has to go away somewhere to read Freud and write a poem and forget about men. She's nineteen, you know." "Well, she must be on the mend," Phil chuckled. "Said she tore up most of the poem this afternoon. Perhaps I- How does everything look to you after the long winter in New England?" "Same as ever; that is, heavenly. I can never wait to get back. Grandfather tells me that you like horses." "Love 'em; always have. I don't know much about

29 your kind, though. Did all my riding and driving in Pennsylvania." "How do you like ours? Not much on looks?" "Well, perhaps they're a bit on the short and stocky side, and they're not so, uh, hard to manage as I expected of your cowhorses." 

A snort came from Pop. "Cowhorses! Sheep-toadsturtles they are; they ain't cowhorses, never could be around here! Phil (feller's name's Phil, Molly), you ain't never see a real cowhorse. A real cowhorse should be short, stocky, like you call it; but his head's up, 'n his tail's out, 'n he's full o' spunk 'n ginger; no matter if you've just brought him in off the winter range, thin 's a rail; no matter if you've just rode him a hunderd mile.

An' he's got more sense in them prickin' ears, 'n them movin' eyes, 'n them swellin' nostrils-than all the jugheads in this here bunch rolled into one!" “But, Grandfather," Molly protested, “you remember the trouble we had at first, when we tried to use the horses all of us had used. The time Mrs. Shepherd yanked Bruno's mouth and he came back and sat in her lap and we thought she was dead. And the time little 

Billy Davenport put on your spurs when we weren't looking, and went over the corral fence on Cheetah? 

People who aren't used to horses come out here from the East and want to do all the things they think cowboys do. We've got to be sure they have animals that will stand for practically anything. Just one bad accident and we might never get over it." "Hmm. S'pose you're right, if you don't want 'em all strung out 'long the barbed wire; though they'd be bet-

DUDE RANCHER 

ter there, scarin' off magpies 'n what they are straddlin' a horse." "I'd like," Phil said speculatively, "I'd like to have a dude ranch where all the horses were raised right on the place, where they'd all be handled from the time they were little fellows. They'd never know what it was to buck or get scared. Those that were just ordinary, I'd break quiet for visitors to use; the others I'd sell to the army or make into jumpers to sell back East. They'd all have to be half-thoroughbred or better, though.” 

Another snort from Pop. "First place, boy, you'd make fools of 'em, babyin' 'em along like kittens. Never get any sense into their heads. Shouldn't break a horse 'til he's four, five. Ruins 'em. Meanest horse I ever see was raised on a bottle when his mammy died. Then, you'd never get no place with them hotbloods in this country. Crazy they are; 'n their legs is s' long 'n thin they'd snap 'em right off first time you took down into a gully hard. Feller I knowed had a bunch o' racehorses used to run at the Overland track in Denver. Track closed down 'n he couldn't get nowhere near what he put into 'em; so he takes 'em out to his ranch 'n puts his cowhands on 'em to work a bunch o' cattle. They'd start down 'longside the bunch, 'n when the boys 'd come back on the bit to turn 'em they'd go plumb wild -head off into the brush like a fool antelope; maybe you'd see 'em next day, 'n maybe you wouldn't. Got to be so's the boys 'd pack a week's grub 'fore they'd git on. Feller got mad 'n chased 'em all out on the range.

I seed some of 'em years after: wild as deer they was, with scratches along their rump, where mountain lions tried to git 'em.”

NEW SHOES AT LAST 

31 "Well, they showed they could live, any way," said Phil, "and I have an idea that it all depends on how you handle a horse from the beginning. The only thing your friend's racers knew was to go as fast as they could around a track; same thing as bolting, almost; and when a horse is bolting he can't see nor think of anything else. 

I'd like to try it. And I should think if you raised your own horses it would cost a lot less." "No," Molly said, "I don't believe you could possibly raise them for what we can buy them for, ready broken, in Denver. They're a little higher now, but a few years ago, when they were rounding up mustangs in Montana and shooting them along the side of a big ditch, to keep them off the cattle range, Dad bought seven for $175. 

Pretty good ones-there was Kate, and Alec, two or three others that we're still using." She stood up. "It's late, Grandfather; we'd better all get to bed, if you insist on getting up early to shoe. You always said yourself it was no job for a man over forty, though." "Forty! Who said I was forty? I ain't but twenty-six; and you needn't think just 'cause you been to college you c'n come back here 'n talk fresh to your granddad. 

You get along to bed now, 'fore I take a notion to spank you." "Well, good night, Grandfather. I'm sorry Dad wasn't here to talk with you. When he dashed off to Denver, he said he'd be back late tonight or some time in the morning. Good night, Phil. Don't you keep him up talking. When he gets on horses-”

So Pop and Phil went to bed, not right away, because there were some loose ends in their argument, and because Phil wanted to persuade Pop to give him his first

DUDE RANCHER 

lesson in shoeing. Phil had never shod a horse; it had never occurred to him; but now he wanted to learn all there was to learn, and Pop, in spite of his set attitude on some things, seemed about the best source of practical knowledge. And, Phil pondered before he went to sleep, in this life all sorts of knowledge are going to be practical: hiring, firing, playing bridge, buying, farming, building, being charming, breaking horses-almost a jingle; wonder if I could do something with that.

Before the sun came over the ridge, Grease Beidel finished setting the tables for breakfast in the big ranch dining room; leaned out the door to boom at his helper, who was just coming across from the cooks' cabin, rubbing his forearms and shivering in the early morning cold; and went back to his chair between the stove and the kitchen table. "All cooks' helpers is bad," he announced to Pop, who was on his second cup of coffee across the table, "but this guy Pete beats 'em all. You'd think six days here'd be enough to cure his hangover, so's he could roll around 'n be some use gettin' breakfast. 'F y'ask me, I think he's ascared o' freezin' t'death 'f he shows up b'fore the stove's hot.-Hey, Pete, PETE! look alive, will you; shut that door; comb the knots out o' that lousy scalp o' yours, 'n take them buckets down to the hogs. Huh? What I care if there ain't no hogs! There oughta be! Take 'em somewhere! Shoulda took 'em last night, 'fore they stunk up the kitchen."

Grease lowered his 260 pounds over and around his chair, picked up his coffee, gulped a tepid mouthful, and spat it back into the cup. "Here, Pete, set down

33 them buckets a minute and get me a fresh cup o' Java. 

You have some more, Pop?" “No, thanks,” said Pop. "Got the old shoes offa one plug, what they was left of 'em, 'n his feet trimmed down, 'fore you was up. Like to git through with him. 

Thanks fer the coffee 'n all. You might have Pete bring me out a mite, come dinner time. I ain't gonna come in 'n eat with them dudes. 'D turn my stummick. An' say, if that young feller, what's-his-name, Marshall, shows up like he said he might, you tell him I'm out to the shop."
