Now you turn this handle, easy, 'bout like this, see? while I try the one I got here. Shoe'll get red directly 'n I'll punch the holes 'n shape her. No, you better not try-"

V. RIDING HERD 

THE rising-bell rang while Phil was "helping" Pop, then the bell for breakfast, and soon the air was filled with voices planning the day.

It was not long before a shadow fell across the anvil, and they looked up to see Buck Snagler standing in the doorway, pulling on his gloves. Buck was a tall man with heavy, sloping shoulders under a thick neck. His nose, seen from the side, was sharply hooked; from the front it was straight and handsome, though a little heavier at the bottom than he liked. He always faced you directly when he talked, even if he had to snap his neck around when you spoke to him from the side. His slight limp might have come from an accident in a rodeo arena, though he had a native coordination, muscular and mental, that usually kept him out of danger. 

From head to foot he looked the Hollywood ideal of a cowboy. When he took off his high-crowned buff sombrero, at meals or in bed, you could see that his hair, plastered straight back, almost hid the pink spot on his crown. In front of his tiny ears it ended in long, tapering sideburns which thrust prettily forward at the bottom. His voice came from his pendulous mouth in a one-sided drawl almost as Hollywood as his makeup. "Seein' you was arisin' up so spry, Marshall, it would of been better if you was to feed Sandy 'n the team 'fore chow, 'stead o' foolin' 'round here. Woulda saved couple hours. With Slim gone 'n the cows ta milk, Hap can't 34

RIDING HERD 

35 do everthing. Now I'm gonna take the team 'n Hap 'n go pull the wagon out, where she stuck last night. I want you should take Sandy-go wrangle in enough horses fer Miz Oakes 'n the Oakes kids, 'n the White kids 'n their old lady. Better ask that sad dame, Connie, would she care to go 'long. She's lookin' a might healthier last couple days. Noticed she did a pretty good job on her grub last night. Then you ride out with 'em where they wanta go, 'xcept Miz Oakes. I'm a ridin' out with her later, 'n you c'n leave in them two palominos for I and she to ride. Might saddle 'em 'n leave 'em tied in the barn 'fore you go. See everbody got their saddles on right, 'n remember it's your job ta keep 'em out o' trouble. 'F you git back time enough 'fore dinner, you go help Hap saw some firewood. Grease is ayellin' his head off he ain't got wood ta singe the feathers off a frog. Now, you think you got that all straight?” "Maybe," said Pop, after Phil had put down the pritchel and left for the barn, "if you was to get you one o' them there secretaries, have her print off your orders fer the next day 'n post 'em somewheres night b'fore, these bums 'd git things straight. Can't expect a ignorant feller like that Phil to keep up with a dingdong planner like you." "That's what I been atellin' Joe," Buck replied, "only he's so tight don't do no good. But I c'n get along all right, keepin', as you might say, a soopervizery eye on everthing.-Glad to see some shoes gettin' on; seems to me, though, you'd make more time if you was to seat 'em in hot, 'stead o' takin' the hoof down so careful with the rasp."

Pop managed to say nothing; but his fingers tight-

DUDE RANCHER 

ened around the hammer handle, and he spat a succulent brown cud in an arc through the doorway. Buck sidestepped it and went to get the load of wood out of the dip where the wagon was mired down. 

When you have thousands of pine and spruce on your north slopes, it is cheaper and probably better to use wood for fuel than to buy coal and haul it in—especially if you burn mostly the dead trees which have fallen in the spring winds and spent two or three years drying out. If you don't mind chopping down your forest, green wood is easier to get but much heavier, and just now the ground was softened from the recent soaking rain. Because the road back to the ranch buildings was down hill, yesterday's load had traveled well enough as far as the little dip where the water had run off the hillside a hundred yards back of the shop. But here a sublayer of black soil prevented normal drainage and a deposit of gravel from the hillside made a mushy covering four or five inches deep, so that the wagon had slowed down and stopped just short of the little rise out of the dip. After one or two futile heaves by the team, Buck had sensibly decided to unhitch and come back for the logs in the morning. 

Now he noticed, while Hap was hooking the tugs to the singletrees, that the wheels had settled further in during the night. It was going to be a real pull. But there was no one on hand to help Hap unload and reload, and so Buck determined to make 'em yank her on out. 

He had supreme confidence in himself as a teamster and, seated high on the top log, absolutely no fear of a horse. Hadn't he driven six mules through the spring

RIDING HERD 

37 mud of Mineral in the days of the ore wagons? But he sensed that Belle and Polly, bad to begin with and getting worse each time he worked "the sulky hides"—were likely to quit if he gave them the slightest evidence of uncertainty on the part of the driver. So he gathered the lines softly into his left glove, swung his whip back carefully to make no warning "whish,” and timed the slash along Polly's off side and flank perfectly with his roar, "Giddy-AP." Polly leaped convulsively and hit the collar ahead of Belle; but a cross-arm cut over the lines snapped Belle against the load just as Polly fell back.

The wagon did not move forward, but each mare was thrown in turn hard against the tongue, and the logs shifted a little so as to startle Buck, who slid to the ground and stood on the off side. "If I was to unload the top half—” Hap began, but a look from Buck and the crack of the whip over the mares' heads cut him short.

Out of rhythm and panicky now, they were no longer a team. They yawed awkwardly back and forth, sweat came out along their flanks and under their necks, their eyes bulged white with terror. 

Furious, Buck dropped the lines and stamped around to the horses' heads. "You-you-you!" he gasped, grabbed one inside line in each hand, drove the mares' chins against their necks; then, "Now!" he bellowed and heaved his full weight backward, as if to start both team and load with his own muscle. Something had to give: it was the stiffness in Buck's spine, so that for a moment his rear sprung out and down. 

Pop had an instinctive sense of timing. He changed neither the speed of his approach nor the rhythm of his

DUDE RANCHER 

stride; like a fullback punting out of danger, he swung his right foot through, and there was a solid thud against Buck's seat. Pop's attack had the virtue of shock and surprise, so that, after his first reflex grunt, Buck was momentarily disorganized and nearly fell forward but clasped the neck-yoke to save his balance. 

Straightening and turning slowly around, he looked at Pop; took in his eyes glinting through narrowed lids; the blacksmith's tongs in his right hand, gently agitating the hot shoe unconsciously in time to his slow and measured words, "I shoulda killed you, Snagler. D' you call yourself a teamster?" And he took a step forward.

Buck took a step backward; the rage molten in his eyes was alloyed with something else. "Like to see you drive 'em out," he said. "Listen, Snagler, I raised them mares in my pasture.

I seen 'em grow up sound and sensible. I broke 'em. 

Bad as you've abused 'em, I c'n get 'em to do anything they can do. Snagler, I can back six where you couldn't lead a dog, best day you ever see; but I'm not goin' to drive 'em out o' there with that load, nor you, nor nobody else. Now you dump them trees you killed, pronto!" He took another step forward. 

Muttering inarticulately, Buck released the chainfastener, heaved against one of the logs, and stepped back while the load came apart and rolled over the wheels to the ground. Pop lifted the lines and clucked to the old team. They moved ahead confidently, but 

Pop stopped them just beyond the dip and set the brake with his hand. "Unhook, Snagler," he said. "That's all o' you they have to stand fer today." He turned to Hap. "And you, young feller, come along to the barn and

RIDING HERD 

39 show me where you hang the harness. I'm sorry I hadda talk kinda pert-like to Handsome; but he always did make me 'most ferget my manners." 

Hap remembered this as a good day, though Buck did ride him a little harder than usual after that. 

For Buck, life on the ranch had had a new interest since the arrival one day late in June of Mrs. Oakes and her two children, Bobby and Phyllis. She was a blithe and pleasant lady. Everyone who talked with her came to feel that he was more stimulating and accomplished than his modesty had let him realize, for she listened with interest, and her dancing eyes that jumped so readily to the next point were not only warm and responsive but really intelligent. And when she spoke the music of her voice softened the mockery in almost everything she said. She was something new to Buck; no sissy, he decided, and he began to shave twice a day instead of thrice a week. He could not find out how long she intended to stay at the ranch; but he hoped for the best and for once extended himself in little offices of hospitality. 

It was probably Buck's limited acquaintance as much as his male conceit that had encouraged him. The girls he knew at Mineral and on the ranches through the county did not do much talking, and the list of subjects "fittin'" for social converse was tightly fenced off in a high corral. Another corral held those you talked of with the girls you saw in Denver, once in a while. So here was something different, and Buck's visits to the neighbors stopped abruptly.

At first bridge had been his only worry, but he had promptly offered to take on one more card game. "No,

DUDE RANCHER 

Buck," Mrs. Oakes had said, "it's dear of you; but you're perfect now. At bridge you'd only be a sweet bell jangled out of tune and harsh. Let me dream of you as you are.-You double, Mrs. White? Redouble!"

Buck had blushed with pleasure and dropped the idea of playing bridge. He only made a helpful suggestion now and then as he sat and watched the game. 

But, in spite of all Buck's maneuvers, Phil was a worry to him; so that his expression was not kind when he learned from the smirking Grease, on his way past the house, that Mrs. Oakes, instead of waiting for him, had gone out riding with Phil and the others. 

He went to the stable; threw his silver-mounted saddle onto the cream-colored Prince of Cimarron; reached for his hammer behind the oat bin; tied it and a gunnysack to the saddle horn; and went forth on one of his solitary field trips. Buck's interest in local geology may have been uncharacteristic, but it was strong and dated from the time of Joe's return, when Buck had left his boarding house at Mineral and come down to take a job on the ranch. In the bottom of his trunk were many little labeled bags of rock chips. It exasperated him that only one had ever shown a decent assay, an amazing $280 to the ton. And that was one of the bunch he had forgotten to label, until after it came back from the office in Denver and he could not remember from which ridge he had chipped the sample. 

Before his group had ridden far, Phil found himself at the side of Mrs. Oakes, behind the others. This was good, for it gave him a chance to cool off after the

RIDING HERD 

41 ordeal of saddling for five small children and Mrs. White.

Day after day it was the same story. Sammy and Judie and Betty White ranged in age from eleven to five; 

Bobby and Phyllis Oakes were nine and five. None of the children could do anything about saddling or bridling; so it took a long time to get them started. They did not waste the time, however; they fought. Betty and 

Phyllis always claimed Cinder: he had such a nice color and such a pretty face, and he would eat biscuits out of your hand. Neither of them was ever permitted to ride him, except around the corral, since he did not merely follow the crowd like a good dude horse but expected signals from his rider; and there are always many things more important and interesting to a five-year-old than a pair of reins.

It was Phyllis who discovered one day during the saddling that corral mud dries out enough on top so that it may be cleanly used as a missile, while the under side retains a happy viscosity. On the day of the discovery Mrs. White, instead of riding, took Betty to the bath; and for three days thereafter Phil had had to ride with the Oakeses and the Whites in separate parties. But in a long talk with Mrs. White he had straightened things out and convinced her that the uninhibited Oakes children were not a threat of contamination to her own. Now she only smiled while Bobby

Oakes led her plump Sam along the picket line and explained the terms used to denote gender in horses. 

The squabbles, the dodging in and out among the horses, the task of adjusting stirrups to the proper

DUDE RANCHER 

length, all took time.

At last Phil freed the hen that Judie was hugging in front of her saddle, got his shoulder under Sam and heaved him to a temporary balance, and they were off. "You're improving, Phil," said Mrs. Oakes. "At first, when I'd see the clouds around your Jovian brow and catch the scent of sulphur in your mutterings, I feared you lacked the maternal instinct. I expected you to kill one of them and hoped it would be Sammy. But your smile, just now, when you pretended it was by accident that you stepped on my Phyllis's toe, will be an inspiration and an example when I am tried." The path led past the edge of the last oat field into the cool shadows of the young spruce and jack pine that crowded the narrowing creek bottom. "Gosh, Mrs. Oakes," Phil exclaimed, “you don't miss a trick! Any way, she stopped screaming. I'm glad you're not mad." "Mad, Phil? The only thing that makes me mad is your beastly habit of calling me Mrs. Oakes. When a woman who admits thirty-two and hopes you don't believe her, starts calling you by your first name, you might as well hand her a cap and footstool as go on calling her 'Mrs.'

The path crossed and recrossed the little stream. 

Maples and birches, which are large bushes rather than trees in the Rockies, hung close on either side and brushed the riders. "O.K., Eleanor. Everything you say makes- Hey, wait a minute, Mrs. White. I forgot about that gate.

RIDING HERD 

43 It's a tricky fastener. Our neighbor, Mr. Sandburg, likes his fences tight and uses this extra lever. First time I tried this one I had to give up and tie the thing shut with a piece of barbed wire." “Oh, thank you, Mr. Marshall. I'm sure I could have done it. I hated to disturb your conversation. Now, Sammy boy, you see why Mother doesn't want you to eat so much cake and candy. If you didn't eat so much, you'd be able to hop off and open gates." "Aw, Mother, we pay to come here, don't we? Why shouldn't he open 'em?" "Oh, Sammy!-Please don't mind him, Mr. Marshall.

I'm sure I don't know where he gets his undemocratic ideas. Certainly not from Mr. White or I. Mr. White is one of the biggest men in Passaic; but everyone looks up to him because he is so simple and natural and you'd never suspect he is one of the biggest men in Passaic."

So up and over and home in time for lunch. 

Molly finished some letters and came down just before the bell. Her father was not back from Denver yet, and Pop was eating off the anvil. Everyone else was there, though, including Connie, who, at the sound of the gong, had bounced like a mountain goat, down from her meditation on the crag, in order to be sure of lunch and a place next to Phil. During the meal it was arranged that Phil should play tennis with Mr. and Mrs. Wedderspoon and Mrs. Christie, while Molly went riding with another group.

Grease came in from the kitchen and volunteered to

DUDE RANCHER 

take the children to the swimming-hole. "Can't swim,' he explained, "but I can't sink neither. If one of 'em should get out o' wind, I c'd jump 'most to the middle o' the pond, and they could climb aboard."

VI. PROFESSOR MOLLY 

THE last game ended 21-6 in Hap's favor, the scoring pretty slow in the gathering dusk; so Phil laid his horseshoes beside the peg. “Guess I'll hit the hay," said Hap. "Won't be long 'fore you'll be goin' after Buck, way you're catchin' holt."

This was just talk, for no one in the country had a chance against Buck: he threw ringers with either hand, from any position, often over the back of some youngster, whom he would get to kneel so as to block his view of the stake. Lacking real competition at the ranch, he didn't play in games much. Mostly he gave exhibitions of his tricks. Hap was pretty good, though; good enough so that Phil saw what a fine game it was and spent odd minutes practicing. "Yes," said Phil, "maybe I'd take on Buck if I had two or three lives to devote to nothing else. After that, I might work on his trick of putting six revolver shots into a tin can before it hits the ground.-Well, good night, Hap. I'll step into the living room and see how things are going. I'd like to help you push that wood into the saw tomorrow, if I don't have to do something else."

Against the long south face of the main and newer wing of the house ran an open porch, looking over the croquet court, then the horseshoe pit, then down the eighth of a mile of ranch road, lined with cottonwoods, 45

DUDE RANCHER 

to the river and across the bridge to the state road, which followed the river's west-east course opposite the ranch buildings. A quarter of a mile to the left, though, where the cliffs came down to the water's edge, the road had to leave the stream bed and go snaking up over the Hump. 

Before they went in, Phil and Connie stood for a few minutes, watching the lights of a truck traveling from Denver to Mineral, as it came twisting down around the switchbacks, its low gear whining against the steep grade. "That's the only bad stretch left between Denver and 

Mineral," Phil said slowly. "I should think Mr. Wakefield would be afraid the highway engineers would get the idea of switching the road over here on the level side and spoil his front yard with dust and noise.” "Do you think that would irreparably wound his fine sensibilities?" Connie asked as they turned and went into the living room.

The kerosene lamps, used whenever the electric light plant broke down, and kept handy at all times, were shedding their warm yellow glow on the ivory paint with which Molly had freshened the walls. It was "mail day"; most of the grownups were deep in their copies of The New Yorker or the Sunday issues of their 

New York and Chicago papers. For the sake of adult ears and nerves, Molly had herded the children into the dining room, where they could be heard still battling over the comic sections. 

Mr. Wedderspoon looked up from the chess game in which he was being worsted by his wife. "Hey, Phil," he moaned, "come over here and help. Pat snatches the

PROFESSOR MOLLY 

47 financial section away from me because she says it gives me the jitters, gets me into this thing to take my mind off it, and now look!" "You don't need help, Jack," Phil said, looking over the board, "you need the coroner-unless you can sneak an extra rook out of your pocket. Nice work, Pat."

He was passing on in the direction of Eleanor Oakes, but Molly, who had been studying a score at the piano, turned and called softly, "Oh, Phil, here's that Geological Survey map you spoke of day before yesterday. I was sure I had given the last copy we had to Buck; but he didn't have it, and so I asked Dad to bring one back from Denver. Would you like to see it now?" "I certainly would. I never have been able to untangle this country. Thought I had most of the paths straight by a system of memorizing and self-hypnosis; but this morning I got lost again up in Sandburg's. 

Sammy White was the only one who caught on at first. 

But he wasted no time before he broadcast my plight, and Mrs. W. spent the next ten minutes telling us all about the perfectly marvelous guide they have at their place up in Ontario." “Well, if you can digest this map, all you'll need to be a native is our local lingo and one more cloudburst on that hat of yours." She raised her voice. "Wouldn't you like to see, too, Mrs. Oakes?" "Why, yes, Molly, thank you-and please call me 

Eleanor. But I'm afraid the map won't help me. I've about decided to tie a silken cord about my waist whenever I ride or walk out alone." "That's a fine idea," Molly beamed, "unless the wrong person happened to pick up the spool.-Now,

DUDE RANCHER 

Phil, if you'll put the table cover and these magazines over on the window sill, I'll set the lamp on the corner of the piano and we can spread the map out here." 

A little group began to gather around the table as Molly started her lecture. "This is called the Wakefield 

Quadrangle because, though it covers a district about twelve miles by sixteen, it contains no town by which it could be named, and at the time of the survey, 1870 -no, this one is 1904, see?-ours was the largest and the oldest settlement. The scale is about one mile to an inch, I think." "That's right," said Phil, “it says 1:62500 here at the bottom." "Well," Molly went on, "it's large enough so that you can trace most of the paths and old wagon roads, and these tiny black squares you see are the buildings of the homesteaders who were here in the seventies, at the time of the first survey. Look! It says 'Masterson Ranch': all you can find there now is a little square of piled stones with raspberry bushes and thimbleberries growing up through them. You must have ridden by there this morning." "Yes, we did," said Phil. "I noticed a pretty husky clump of rhubarb growing beside the old wall, and Judie had to get off and pick up a dented enamel potlid." 

Molly went on, "There hasn't been anyone there for years, thirty or forty, I guess. You ought to get Grandfather to tell you about the Mastersons. Now here is our township, just about in the middle of the map." "Township," objected Connie. "There's no town. Do you suppose the name is a symbol of the surge of the

PROFESSOR MOLLY 

49 collective ego among the early settlers?" But she took a thick brown volume and curled up in a chair off at the corner of the room before Molly could explain. “Oh, no, township is just a geographical expression.

The whole country is laid off in townships, six miles square. Each square mile is a section, 640 acres; and the sections are numbered one to thirty-six, beginning in the upper right-hand corner, going across to six, with seven just below it, then counting back, and so on. Before we started selling land, when this was just a cattle ranch, we had ten sections. I'll draw a line around what we had. Here is the Rebel River, running through 36. 

It was named after a Confederate sergeant whose unreconstructed feelings led him to turn outlaw and hold up the stages that used to run from Denver to Mineral and back, carrying gold dust on the way down. Grandfather saw them hang him. These are our ranch buildings in the upper part of 35. "You see these heavy brown lines? They're called contours and run on a level, the only thing around here that can. Here's the one marked 8000 feet, running just back of our house." "Hmm, I see," said Phil, “a hundred feet apart in elevation, and every fifth one is heavier. Look, they all run together there on the north front of the Hump." "Yes, and along the cliffs where the cañon narrows below. In fact, you see they are pretty close together on both sides of the river all through here. Not like Beaver 

Creek up here, just north of us. The valley widens there and the contours with it, so that the land is flat enough for meadows. We leased it to Sandburg when 

Grandfather and Dad sold the cattle and went in for a

DUDE RANCHER 

dude ranch and horses. Mr. Sandburg bought it later; let's see, it makes four sections. How many acres, Phil?" “Uh-h-h, about 2500; yes, 2560." "Then the Jacksons got this long half-mile strip above the ridge in 13, 14, and 15. It's not so level; but there's lots of timber and, you see, two and a half miles of creek bottom. The Jacksons didn't do well, let it go for taxes and moved away. I don't know who owns it now. "Down here at the bottom, in 35 and 36, the road runs along the south side of the river, except for these big loops over the Hump. Long ago, before I was born, Grandfather traded everything south of the road to old Nils Nilssen. That means our ranch takes in almost nothing south of the river, except the Hump and the ridge back from it, where the road twists around to the south. There's a cave among the rocks on the Hump, and we think the outlaw used to hide there. It would be a good place, anyway, because from the entrance he could see way up the road and spot the dust of the stagecoach coming down from Mineral. And when they searched it after he was hanged, they found a lot of little canvas bags, all empty. "Up here in the northeast corner is the 480 acres Grandfather fenced off and kept for himself when he moved up to his cabin. It is a lovely spot, but we don't ride over there much, because he's funny about strangers, and it's a long way, unless you know how to follow the trail over the two ridges and through or around this corner of Sandburg's.' 

"It's around for us, isn't it, Phil, until we get Sammy trained down to open gates?" asked Eleanor Oakes. "Yes, but I'm starting on him tomorrow. He doesn't

PROFESSOR MOLLY 

51 know it yet, but he's going to pile firewood while Hap and I run the saw-unless his ma comes to his rescue, and I hope she has sense enough not to.” "How much land is there in your ranch now?" asked

Jack Wedderspoon, running his finger around the line 

Molly had drawn. "Something over 1500 acres, I believe. We don't know exactly because the south line follows the curve of the road and has never been surveyed. We're small, as ranches go, even around here; but we don't run cattle any more, so we've plenty of pasture for the horses. And we can grow what little oat hay we need for winter in patches here and there." "Oat hay?" said Phil. "You mean you feed the straw after thrashing?" "No, no, we never thrash any grain. No one does up here. At this altitude the season is usually too short. 

Even if it weren't, each rancher would have to have his own outfit, because the country is too rough to move a thrashing machine around from ranch to ranch; it wouldn't be worth while. What we do is cut the oats just before they are ripe; the stems and leaves are still green, the heads, too. The whole thing, plant and grain, makes wonderful horse hay." "But surely," Jack Wedderspoon protested, "the oat hay you raise in the patches I've seen around here isn't enough to feed all your thirty horses, plus the milk cows, through the winter?” "Yes, usually. We don't have to feed much, except in storms." "You mean the horses stay out all winter?” "Surely. Ours do, and everybody else's around here.

DUDE RANCHER 

You ought to see them in winter: they grow long, woolly coats like bears. But the grama grass on the south slopes keeps them in good condition, even though it's short and dry. Over on the Western Slope, of course, and wherever there is real hay country, they raise lots of hay and they feed all winter. They have to, because their uplands grow sagebrush instead of grass; and the bottom lands are all in meadows, which get covered with snow by November and stay covered until April." "How about the cows?" asked Mrs. Christie. "Do they look out for themselves, too?" "No, cattle are pretty helpless when there is snow on the ground. They'll stand in good pasture covered a few inches deep and starve to death. A horse will scrape and nose the snow away and do very well until the wind and the hot sun have cleared the slopes for him. Sometimes you can see steers that have been left out, following behind the horses, nibbling at the tufts that have been uncovered. They don't get much, though, because a horse crops closer than cattle do, and the boldest bull on the range is afraid to crowd the littlest horse." "I always thought I'd rather raise horses than meat,” said Phil. "Now I know it, in spite of the very good reasons you and Pop give for not raising them.” "Well," Molly said in conclusion, "that is the end of the lesson. I'll pin the map up on the wall, where everybody can see it, and if you look at those little black lines before you ride out, you will know how to get almost anywhere. The dotted lines are for trails, and the solid ones show where there were wagon roads at the time of the survey. They're mostly grown up with aspens or young pines now, except the ones we use to

PROFESSOR MOLLY 

53 haul in oats and firewood." "That was swell, Molly," Phil declared when the group had broken up. "It seems strange that in a country so new as this, hardly discovered seventy years ago, one man's lifetime, you have before your eyes, in the clearings and the trails; in the foundations grown full of young timber; in the outdated stage road over there, still being used for heavy trucks and fast cars-you have the story of the beginnings of a country and a people, a record of all the changes they went through, the false starts they made, the changes that ought to have come but haven't yet. Why, I thought of this country as primitive; and it is, in a way; but in another way it's as full of history as the Forum or the Valley of the Nile. "Wouldn't it be fun to get a big square of paper and make a much larger copy of this part of the map? We could show all the fence lines of the different pastures, and the gates, and the parts that are in timber and those that are clear. All the old homestead sites, and the plowed fields. You'll have to do the lettering: I saw those cans you labeled, over at Pop's." "You like people, don't you, Phil? Alive or dead, and whether you've seen them or not. Well, I do, too, most of them. Next time I get a chance- Why don't I ask 

Dad to let you do the shopping in Denver this Friday or Saturday? It will be a change for Mrs. Hinkle and 

Hap. He usually drives her in. I'll go with you in the truck and show you the places where we buy; and if there's time when we're through, we'll go to the museum. You'll love it. There you have the story before your eyes: weapons and tools and ornaments and gadgets of every kind, from the Indians right on down

DUDE RANCHER 

through the trappers, the first lonely prospectors, the settlers and farmers and big scale miners, to the larger ranchers that finally took over the country for good. "Dad is through there, in the old house, talking with 

Grandfather. I can hear him walking up and down. 

I'll wait until they have finished and ask about the shopping trip." "Fine! There's nothing I'd like better," Phil replied enthusiastically. "And I'll be glad to see how the buying is done, too. It always puzzles me how Grease manages to turn up with fresh vegetables and meat and bread for a small army, never seems to run out of anything. But, say, if it's Saturday, we'll be back in time to go to the square dance at Mineral Spring, won't we?" "Probably. Are you going?" "The Wedderspoons and Eleanor want to go, anduh-Connie said she thought it would be a good chance to observe-" "Oh. You will find it very enjoyable, I'm sure. Good night." 66 'Night, Molly. You'll be there, too, I hope." "Oh, perhaps. Depends on someone who's coming Saturday."

VII.  

WITH his thumbs in the armholes of his vest and an extinct cigar in one corner of his mouth, Joe Wakefield trod up and down the full length of his office, up and down the long floor of what Molly and everyone with a few years' memory of the ranch called "the old house." 

On each trip southward from his big roll-top desk he crossed the line into what had been the "sitting room" when he was a boy. Northward bound from the door at the front, he entered the old family kitchen and dining room. Molly said it was a shame they had torn the partition out, enabling him to travel twice as far and worry twice as hard.

In the "new house," separated from the old only by the log wall and the closed door that used to lead out to the storeroom, Joe could hear the mumble of voices. 

But he was not thinking of them. His sporty blue collar drooped under the weight of his lowered chin and the heavy jowls that curved over its edge. His dark brows were down, too, so that his left eye was closed entirely and the right one took in only the path worn in the carpet ahead of him.

Pop sat relaxed in a big leather chair with his back to the room, his feet on the sill of the west window, through which, because the dim kerosene lamp on Joe's desk was out of line with the panes, he could see the glow of moonlight on the big rocks rising above the house. His elbows were on the arms of the chair, and 55

DUDE RANCHER 

his hands, with finger-tips juxtaposed, made a peaked, bony bridge across his middle. His posture suggested healthy fatigue after a well-known muscular job, but, as he spoke, there was in his voice a tiredness of a different sort: "Trouble is you never was cut out to be a rancher, Joe, not even a dude rancher. Way I figger is you gotta grow up on a ranch, get to know the ways o' the land 'n the critters 'n all the jobs that gotta be done all the time. Or else, and maybe this might be better, some ways, you gotta have born inside you the feel fer the life 'n what it's all about-like, like, take fer an example, that young feller Marshall. He don't know nothin', can't do nothin'; he's full o' looney notions 'll have to get knocked out of him, an' he'll likely get kilt 'fore he learns. But, inside, he's got a feel fer places 'n critters 'n men; and he likes to do things with his hands. 

Whether he knows how or not don't make no difference: his hands is right, an' they tell him things his head don't know. Now you ain't like that, Joe. You don't do things with your hands. You skipped out when you was a shaver and never did learn the ways-o'-doin' a rancher's got to git inside him. And, sure as shootin', you ain't got that other thing, the feel fer it, I mean. "You're strong 'n husky 'n all; your ma and me was mighty proud when you got the boxing champeenship fer your club. But there ain't no give nor no patience in your muscles, Joe. I mind the time when you was a little tyke, nine, ten, I guess: you was down to the shed, tryin' to teach a calf t'drink out a bucket. Well, I guess you got astraddle her neck 'n backed her into the corner, like you knowed how, 'n shoved her nose down in

the bucket. But she wouldn't drink 'n wouldn't drink.

You got madder 'n madder 'n pushed harder 'n harder, 'n so'd the calf. 'N she throwed her head around 'n splashed milk all over 'n knocked you agin the wall. I come out the barn where I was milkin', to see what was goin' on. There was the two of you on the ground, the calf abellerin' an' acursin' s'loud like to make a feller deef. You had your legs atwisted 'round her 'n your teeth in her ear; but you still had the bucket in both hands, an' you was a tryin' to pour the milk, what they was left, down her throat. Like to drowned her."

At the mention of his "champeenship" Joe had swung out of his course and paused before the shelves built into the space once occupied by the outside kitchen door. Here, near the top, were a few books, leaning sideways because they failed to fill their shelf; an irregular lump of quartz the size of a man's head, its cracks and hollows black with dust; an open cigar box containing odd screws and nails. But what drew Joe's attention was the tarnished imitation-silver mug. He took it in his hands and read the inscription, "J. S. W., Winner 

Middleweight Class, Interclub Meet, Chicago." He put the mug back and turned to his father. "I remember that calf," he spoke slowly, with a deep, scratchy voice, coming slightly from one side of his mouth, “but what it has to do with the jam we're in now, I can't see, unless-" 66 9 'Scuse me, Joe," Pop interrupted, “bein' here in this room takes me back; gettin' old, I guess. Why, under that wall paper with its fancy mornin' glories, under the plaster May slapped on so's things wouldn't look so plain, I c'n see the smooth pine logs with the mortar

DUDE RANCHER 

chinked in, 'n the rifles in their racks on the walls, up high, so's you couldn't reach 'em. But all I aim to show is, there's some things you don't savvy, things that can't be taught-like gittin' a cow-brute to drink when it ain't a mind to. "I ain't blamin' you, Joe. Your ma was plenty mad about that calf, you losin' your temper 'n all; said I'd oughta whop you. But I knowed how you felt; been that way a little myself sometimes; and calves is ornery. 

Only I shoulda seed how things was. When you come back, all you talked about was the money we could make runnin' a dude ranch. I didn't care nothin' 'bout that, but I thought maybe the country had got holt of you, at last, like it has me, like it done your granddad, 'n Molly, too, I think. Thinks I, Joe's come back where he belongs; Joe can run the place the way he wants. 

Made more sense 'cause the grass was bad, follerin' them dry years. So I sell all the good meadow we got, 'n the whitefaces. And when that don't make enough to cover all the buildin' 'n the promotin' you think you gotta do, I slap a mortgage on what's left. "Then, when I get out, 'cause I can't stand your dudes, er they can't stand me, I make the place over to you, lock, stock, 'n barrel-keepin' only the 480 I'm livin' on now. "I ain't kickin' 'bout that; I'm havin' the time o' my life. But how 'bout you, Joe? How long you been runnin' this outfit?" "Eleven-no, thirteen years." "Thirteen years, and you're back two years on taxes; you ain't got the cash fer the payment that's due the twentieth this month; an' you say you ain't sent May

nothin' in six months." "Thank you for not mentioning that I've never paid you anything on the second mortgage you hold." "That? Forget it. I did, long ago. But seems to me you've had long enough to see they ain't no mountain o' gold in runnin' a dude ranch. Leastways you ain't the one to find it." "Listen, Dad," Joe started pacing again, "you're right. I don't care anything about ranching; the dudes aren't my kind. I know that now. I should've stayed in

Chicago. Always could get some kind of job. Men in the club wouldn't let their middleweight starve. Why, I used to pick up quite a bit of change, fighting preliminaries out of town, usin' different names, 'til I got this eye. Too dangerous after that. I'd 've been spotted and lost my amachoor standing. But I did all right, one thing and another-'til the army got me. Fighting 

Dutchmen was O.K., too, game o' skill; but when I got out the lid was off in Chi; too fast for me. So I come out here with May and the kid; hoped I could talk you into doin' something about that gold. You stepped on that idea quick. But I've never got it out o' my head. 

And this time, I thought maybe you'd be sensible. 

There's no other way to save the place."

At the word "gold" Pop stiffened for a moment, then settled back. "Joe," he said, "I thought we got that all straight fer once and all. But, seein' you don't think so, I'll go over it again. "First place, you got no call to think they is any gold. 

Story 'bout your granddad findin' it might be true; might not. 'F he did, you c'n bet your last plugged nickel he never told me nor nobody else where it was.

DUDE RANCHER 

"Second place, supposin' it is there an' supposin' I know where it is, the which ain't likely, what chance you think you got of its bein's on your place, with three-quarters of it sold off?" “Oh, Dad, you make me tired. One thing I'm sure of: even you wouldn't have let Sandburg have that land if it had been there." "Wouldn't hey? That shows you don't know nothin' about it. My father says to me, he says, 'Son, they's a heap more been put into a hole in the ground than what's ever been took out. It don't do no good,' he says, 'to most the fellers goes in fer it, an' what it does to the country is nothin' short o' murder. I'd hate fer to have you ever get the bug,' he says. "No need to tell me! I growed up seein' them stripped hillsides above Mineral; them big gullies washin' out under the houses; the dead-faced miners 'n their paper-faced kids. An' I knowed one, two the fellers owned the mines, too. Didn't hear them singin' no hymns o' joy. "But I guess you got no way o' knowin' I'd a heap rather see Sandburg ownin' my land an' treatin' it right than to own it myself an' see its innerds tore out. "Anyhow, Joe, supposin' they is gold an' supposin' I know where it is, an' supposin' it ain't sold: on the strickly business side, as the feller says, it's a hunderd to one it ain't worth workin'. Wouldn't be only a little pocket; 'nough to get you all steamed up, rustlin' 'round fer machinery 'n capital; then it'd peter out 'fore you could pay fer printin' your stock. Remember what happened to that feller Holcomb, few miles this side Mineral?"

"All right, Dad, all right! You know I think your attitude about the whole thing is just a little cockeyed, and I'm not the only one. So we'll probably get nowhere hashing that over. But what I don't get is your treatin' me like a kid the week before Christmas. I don't get it. 

You could at least tell me, yes or no, is there any ore you know of."

Pop closed his eyes and spoke very slowly. “Joe, when you come back and started pesterin' me about that gold, made no diff'rence was it yes or no-I had the same reason fer not tellin' you: I hoped you'd hang around 'til the other thing got you, the ranchin', I mean. I can see now I was wrong. But if you'll jest set down, by yourself, do a little figgerin', you'll see the reasons I got now fer not sayin' is twice as good as what they was then. 

Think it over. I ain't gonna say no more about it, ever."

He opened his eyes, stood, and turned around. “Now, 'bout that payment you got comin' due the twentieth. 

Maybe I can help. What's today?" "Seventeenth, Monday." "Seventeenth. Twentieth, that's Thursday. If you'll send Molly fer me tomorrow, she can take me up to 

Mineral. Bank don't want to foreclose; they got too many ranches a'ready. Can't sell 'em. An' s' long 's they got loans out on 'em, they look like more on the books than what they do if they're foreclosed. Dan Thompson'll listen to me. Don't think he'll make me sign nothin', either. Taxes you can pay when you collect from your tenderfeet. Hope you can help May out by then, too."

Joe sighed deeply, shook his head, and sat in his desk chair. "Well," he said, "I'd rather you'd see Thompson

DUDE RANCHER 

than do it myself. He don't like me. I'll take you to 

Mineral, though; goin' up myself in the morning. You'll stay here tonight, won't you?" "I will not! Got a little place o' my own, you know, an' a few critters 'll be expectin' me back. Old Blackie'd most likely bust out o' pasture or die o' lonesomeness if I was to keep Bess away from he any longer 'n what 

I have now. "Pair o' ponies I raised once, twenty years ago, I guess it was. One was a real smart-lookin' pinto, fat 'n slick, 'n stepped out nice. Everybody wanted to buy him. Other was th'ugliest little runt I ever see: long hair, comin' out in spots, dirt color, head like a coal bucket, tail 'bout a foot long. Crazy he was, too. But them two stuck together like they was David 'n Goliath; couldn't part 'em. If I'd ride out somewheres on the pinto, th'other one, The Burr we called him, 'ld foller along inside the fence 's far 's he could, humpin' his roach back an' awheezin' an' amoanin' fer his chum, 'til he come to the corner 'n couldn't go no further. Then he'd stand there hollerin', 'n the pinto'd holler back, far 's they could hear one another. "Never took The Burr out alone, 'cause the pinto was a jumper. He'd go over, or sometimes through, any fence was in his way. "Well, sir, I sold that pinto three, four times. He'd come back home; 'n then the feller'd come after him 'n take him away again, 'n he'd come home again. I'd always buy him back after a bit, fer 'bout half what I sold him for. He was gettin' to be a good line o' business, 'til all the neighbors begin to git onto his ways. "Then one day one o' them stylish ladies from Den-

ver comes by in her automobile an' sees me ridin' the pinto 'long the road. She gets the colored feller to whoa 'n back up, 'n she says, 'My, but that's a purty horse you got there, Mister.' An' she asks me is he gentle. "'Ma'am,' I says, 'this here pony is so plumb gentle 'n so lovin' he'll cry like a baby if ever I fergit to kiss him g'night 'n g'mornin'.' (You see, I hadn't had no luck sellin' him fer 'most a year.) So nothin' 'll do but she must buy the horse fer her kids; 'n I let her have him fer seventy-five dollars, 'cause she was a nice lady, 'n I could easy get forty for him any place they didn't know him. "Well, 'bout a week after she sent fer him, I get a telegram says have I seen the horse. 'No, Ma'am,' I wires back, 'but I will.' An' sure enough, here he comes up the road coupla days later, proud as a rooster, with scratches all over his chest 'n legs where some o' the barb' wire had been too high for him. "The lady brings her automobile up with the truck to take him back. Truck starts off ahead of her, an' she sees The Burr crowdin' 'long the fence, 'n she hears the pinto 'n him hollerin' back 'n forth. So she comes back to where I'm standin', 'n she says, 'I don't believe it was your kisses he was ahankerin' for, after all,' she says. 'What are we gonna do?' "I draws myself up, an' I says, 'If you mean to insinuate, Ma'am, that that there horse ain't preecisely like he was repersented, here's twenty dollars I'll give you fer him right now and hold no hard feelin's.' "So she gets kinda mad 'n says no, she couldn't sleep nights if she thought she'd handed the pore horse back to a man was so slick 'n prob❜ly so cruel to him; so she

DUDE RANCHER 

guessed she'd jest shut him up in the barn. "If you do that, Ma'am,' I says, 'you prob'ly won't sleep anyhow.' So she slams the door o' the automobile 'n tells the colored feller to giddap 'n starts down the road like a bronc out the chute. Course I knowed she'd be back, an' sure enough, here she comes one day, smilin' as purty as you please, but her eyes wasn't smilin'. "She says she's been thinkin' it over an' maybe it was kinda cruel to separate two horse growed up together, an' would I take ten dollars fer that other awful little monster. “'Don't, Ma'am,' I says, 'he's got sharp ears 'n tender feelin's. There ain't no amount o' money,' I says, 'would buy that horse. He's all the family me an' the missis has got any more.' So we finally settled on a hunderd dollars, 'cause she was a nice lady, an' I figgered it'd spruce the place up to have The Burr away. "Gosh, Joe, you shoulda stopped me! All I aimed to show was why I gotta get Bess home to old Blackie. G'night."

Pop left by the outside door to avoid passing through the living room, where there might still be dudes, and Joe resumed his pacing. His father's "no" was hardly a surprise; and his own alternative plan was more promising. Of this he had told Pop nothing, sensing that his disapproval would be vehement.

The State Highway Department and the County Commissioners had for some time been considering the possibility of shifting the road to this side of the river and running it right past the main house. This would cut out all the bad curves and steep hill between Denver

and Mineral and bring nearer the goal of a fine paved road all the way. The next logical step would be to get the transcontinental highway routed through. The project had been long held up by lack of funds; but now that the government seemed disposed to help, the only objection was the cost of condemning a right of way through the Wakefields's front yard. While Joe had no intention of lowering the valuation he had set, he felt sure that the Commissioners were reasonable men and could be counted upon to coöperate in supporting an improvement that would mean something tangible to 

Mineral Spring, to Hammond County, and to themselves. He was to meet the state engineer at their office the next day.

After he had said good night to Molly, he returned to his desk and tried to sketch the first plans of a roadside hotel or lodge. There would be a restaurant with a fine big floor for dancing. The orchestra at this end. The bar over there. High class. The people of Denver were entitled to something better in the way of entertainment, and once the road was finished, there would be plenty of tourists looking for the first attractive place to stop in the mountains.

But his pencil would not do what he wanted; so Joe 

Wakefield tore up the paper and went to bed.

VIII. "DON'T FORGET MY ASPIRIN!" 

FRIDAY morning Molly came to breakfast wearing a blue and gray print dress instead of the usual dungarees. "It's going to be hot in town," she called to Phil at the next table, as she sat down by Mrs. Hinkle. "You'd better leave that jacket and necktie at home.”

She put Mrs. Hinkle's list under a fresh sheet of paper and started taking down the errands that were called out to her: a new pair of sneakers for Pat Wedderspoon, "about your size, I guess"; cartons of cigarettes; ten 

Hershey bars for Sammy, "No, only four, Sammy Boy"; suits and dresses from the cleaner's; a clevis and two ten-inch files for Hap; cartridges and a bottle of shaving lotion for Buck; various monthly and weekly magazines; more and more and more, until the list ran down to the bottom of the sheet and she had to begin a new one. Between bites she semaphored understanding of an order for toothpaste; recorded the brand and size; settled an argument between Phyllis and Betty about Cinder's age; nodded and mumbled comprehension of 

Mrs. Hinkle's suggestions buzzing in her left ear. "It will be ever so much simpler for you, Honey, if you go to Goff's first and get my aspirin and little things like that. You can 'phone your grocery order from there and let Mr. Marshall drive the truck around to Klein's and pick them up. Sometimes I 'phone Barney's from there, too, and let them take the meat over to Klein's.

Phil finished tying the kerosene drum into the corner of the truck, wedged the folded tarpaulin between it and the side-board, and climbed in beside Molly. "Whew!" he whistled as she swung out into the driveway. "Is it always like that?" "Here, Phil, hold onto these letters. If we leave them on the seat, they'll slide behind the cushion. What do you mean 'like that"?" "I mean that hideous list they piled on you. Is there always that much to do?" "It's about average, I guess. But we sha'n't be able to go to the museum. I ought to have known better than to think of it. We'll probably have to plan a special trip, September, when the crowd leaves." "Don't you want me to drive, Molly?" "Oh, no, thank you. I love to drive the truck. It's so heavy and gives me a feeling of power and authority, like a railroad engineer. I like it best coming back from town with a heavy load, at night, up hill. The cars whiz past you and toot their horns, but you just stay over on your side of the road and don't have to do anything about them, and they know it. Then, when you get a few miles out from town, if it's late, you have the world to yourself, except for the rabbits and a coyote now and then. Your lights push the darkness back, and your engine roars when you go up the grades in low. It's much more fun than driving a car. I'm going to town again

"DON'T FORGET MY ASPIRIN!” 

69 tomorrow morning, to meet the Browns and Mr. Saltell. 

That's why we're shopping today, instead of Saturday.” "Who's Mr. Saltell?" "Oh, just a man, my roommate's brother. He's on his way to California, and he's stopping off for the weekend to see the ranch."

When the truck started down off the Hump and the plains came into view, Phil found he was glad to be the passenger. Molly was driving well, and he enjoyed counting the reflections of the morning sun in the little lakes and reservoirs of the flat, thirty, forty miles to the east. He had a good view, too, of Molly's profile; and, perhaps because she was wearing girl's clothes, he felt no regret for that part of the scenery which she blocked out. From the side, he noticed, her face was less softly harmonious than from the front, and more interesting. High, firmly rounded cheeks and rather prominent brows gave a depth and straightness to her gaze that reminded him of Pop. Sitting beside her, he realized for the first time that she was neither small nor slight. Conformation, he decided, must work the same way in a woman as in a horse: the more smoothly he's built, the smaller he looks. 

All day, running from store to store, hunting for places to park, rearranging the load, backing up Molly while she fought with the vegetable man about the quality of his potatoes and cabbage, walking eight blocks in the sun because there would be no place for the truck near the only camera shop that stocked a particular kind of film-it all seemed grotesquely inefficient and irritating, but Phil could think of no other way to manage and still accomplish everything.

DUDE RANCHER 

When they started for home, too late for supper at the ranch, he was limp and weak and beaten. “I think 

I understand la Hinkle's headaches," he said to Molly. "Doesn't it get you down?" "Yes, I'm tired," Molly replied, "but it's fun, sort of.

And I don't do it very often. It will be cool at the ranch, and you'll recover after a shower. It wasn't any worse than New York, was it?" "Oh, no! Not the same thing. At least I could see today why we were doing everything, what it was all about. But if I should ever have a dude ranch, I believe I'd leave all the shopping trips to someone else and keep for myself the light work like digging postholes and pitching hay." "You couldn't, Phil. You'd be gypped all the time unless you learned that part, too. And no one you can hire will take as much interest in the little things your people want done, nor even in the ranch buying. Dad thinks I'm too young to take much responsibility, but someone's got to do it. He never has, really, and I tell him that's one of the worst things about our organization. Mrs. Hinkle tries hard, of course, on her good days; and Hap is wide awake, though he hates town as much as you do." "He's a good kid-wide awake, as you say. Strange, though, his world seems to stop with the horses and cattle and machinery. I've been trying to get him to go to the dance tomorrow, but he just smiles. Think you'll go?" "It depends, Phil. Bob Saltell is my guest. I'll have to see whether he would like to go."

Work was not over, even when they got back to the

"DON'T FORGET MY ASPIRIN!" 71 

ranch. The truck had to be unloaded because the meat must be got into the ice-box, and early morning piñon jays might peck at the fruit and vegetables. By lantern light Hap helped Phil with the unloading, while Molly and Mrs. Hinkle attacked the laundry. After that and a warm plate of dinner, which Grease had left under inverted pots on the stove, Molly hurried through her tub and came back to the living room. 

Feeling dull and a little depressed, Phil begged off the usual bridge game, walked past Connie's chair with a mumbled "Good night," and went to bed. On the way there, he knocked on Mrs. Hinkle's door and borrowed her aspirin.

IX. SQUARE DANCE 

IT turned out that Bob would like to go, to the dance or anywhere else that Molly suggested. They came in late for the party, and he stood beside her, leaning against the wall, among the little children and the old ladies fanning themselves. He turned a broad smile of simple delight on the strange scene. 

The groggy piano reeled under the punishment but never quite fell apart; little rivers ran down the fiddler's face and flooded the red bandana thrust into his collar; interminably the maple floor swayed up and down as 

Pike Moore called the figures. "Dip for the oyster, dip for the clam-" The youths of Hammond County threw off their moroseness, swung and grinned and strutted; the icicles thawed in the ranch girls' spines, and their bodies came alive as their tongues never could. Rawboned bachelors with twinkling eyes whirled the sturdy matrons and made them giggle with hints of waggish boldness.

Along the wall, the youngest babies slept in the laps of their mothers. The four-year-olds swayed against their fathers' knees, sucking the end of a finger, absorbing the talk of hay and grass and heifers. 

Near the door an alien group, seven or eight sophisticates from Denver, stood close together, smiling in tolerant derision at the antics of the "yokels." They were clothed in English riding outfits, apparently undefiled by any contact with a horse, and they mimicked the an- 72

SQUARE DANCE 

73 cient rhythms in stamping little gyrations, or gulped furtively from a flat bottle.

Every Saturday during the summer, less often in the long winter, Hammond County turned out for a dance in the Town Hall at Mineral Spring. The building was a massive, rectangular block made of large, rounded stones collected from the river bottom and held together with concrete mortar. Its malignant ugliness was a monument to the imagination and the civic pride of old General Thompson, who, in the first days of the century, had sought to assert the greatness and the permanence of his city by building the most startling contrast he could think of with the flimsy board structures that made up most of the town. 

On the ground floor of the Town Hall were the post office, an assayers' office, and the plant of the Hammond County Weekly Republican. Above, since there was no longer any trade for a hotel, the partitions had been ripped out, leaving space for a broad dance-floor of excellent maple. It was reached from the outside by a timber staircase that began in the middle of one side of the building, ran diagonally across one of the downstairs windows, turned the corner, and ended aloft in a broad landing at the rear and only door.

The dances began as soon as people could get to town after chores, and they ended just in time to get home for milking; so that the ranchers' rule of early to bed was not broken. You simply did not go to bed on Saturday night.

At last the fiddler paused, the squares broke up, and 

Eleanor Oakes took Phil's arm. "Am I seeing things,

DUDE RANCHER 

Phil?" she asked. "Or is that Bob Saltell over there with Molly?" "Bob Saltell is right. Why? Do you know him?” "Know him! Everybody knows him. His is the face mothers see in their dreams the night before daughter's coming-out. I'm not sure of the details, but I think his family owns half of New England by an original grant from James I. The other half they've acquired more gradually. He and his sister have unaccountably turned out human beings, and it has almost wrecked the family. 

Come with me." She led Phil the length of the big hall and swooped on Bob Saltell, just as he saw her and exclaimed in surprise and pleasure. "Waal, if it ain't the ol' sloth himself," she drawled. "What brings you to these here diggin's, pardner? D'you walk out on a hangin' party in Boston Common? You sure look plenty pert, you ol' horse-thief!-And look, Bob, I've got bowlegged, too!" "You always were, Eleanor," Bob said, as he squeezed both her hands in his strong, chubby fists, while his grin pushed his apple cheeks so high they closed his eyes. "This is Phil Marshall, Bob," and she took Phil's arm. "He's my favorite cow-hand. Found him under a shock of hay." "Pleased ta meetcha, stranger," Phil said, as he smiled and shook hands. "I'm glad you came, Molly." He turned back to Bob. "Molly wasn't sure you'd want to come, but I bet you'll like it."

Mrs. Scholz at the piano began "chording" for another square dance. Ab Jordan unfolded his six-feet-six, wedged the fiddle between the two promontories of his Adam's apple and his chin, and started in pursuit of the

SQUARE DANCE 

75 tune. Pike Moore went on talking to his neighbor in a whisper that could be heard the length of town; but he kept his eyes on the young people coming out from the corners and forming little groups. When there were enough for three squares, he heaved himself off the folding chair, hitched his trousers up over his bulging equator, thrust his red beard at the center of the room, and, in a voice that submerged piano and fiddle, called the squares to order. "You'd better stay out of this one and watch, Bob," said Eleanor, then turned to Phil and Molly. "You two go on and dance. I'm sure you can do a better job with his ubiquitous feet than I was doing- Mine are big, too."

When they had obediently joined the beginners' square forming in the corner, she led Bob to the row of chairs against the wall. "Tell me about Howard," she said. “Have you seen him?”

Bob sat, with a sigh of relief. “Sure, see him almost every day at the club." "Oh. Go on; tell me." "No, he's cold sober. Beat me at squash the day before I left. Says he hasn't touched a drop since-for over a month." "Thank God!" she said, closed her eyes for a moment, and bit her upper lip. "I ought to write to him; tell him where we are." "Good girl! Don't go on my say-so, Eleanor; but I think he means it this time. And a letter from you-" "But you, Bob, what brings you to our little frontier outpost? Or can I guess?"

DUDE RANCHER 

"You can and you have-right as usual, Old X-ray eyes." "Hmmm. Better work fast, Bob." "So I gathered. What's he like?” "Real, I think. Don't believe he's looked at her yet, the fool!" "I don't believe he's had a chance, has he?" "Shut up. Pay attention to this dance. How can I sponsor your debut if you won't even try to learn?-Oh, Connie, there you are. Put away your notebook for a minute. Come over here and meet the laziest man in the world. His laziness made him the star of Hasty Pudding, and now Hollywood is after him.-No, I'm not asking you to dance with him, though I think his immobility would make him a safer risk than Phil."

It was one o'clock or later when Buck Snagler got to the dance. He could walk quite steadily, and his tongue was not unduly thick, for it had been hot and dull in his usual haunts in Denver, and he had left early for the cooler and headier atmosphere of the ranch. But he found no one there who interested him, and so he drove his roadster on to the dance. Now he stood in the doorway and blinked at the waltzing couples, surging by under the bright lights. Seeking a steadier object, his eyes came to rest on the smallest of the party from Denver, standing close beside him. "That's sure a cute outfit you got on, Shorty," he said to the little man. The little man made no reply. "You ain't mad, are you, Shorty?" asked Buck and put a long arm affectionately around the gabardine shoulder. "Take your dirty hands off me," snarled the little

SQUARE DANCE 

77 man, struggling as he was drawn closer into the curve of Buck's arm. "Why, Shorty, that ain't no way to talk. Maybe you'd better step out inna fresh air an' cool off. I'll take care the lady 'til you get back.” "Oh, yeah?" gasped the little man, wrenched himself away, and swung a puny left into Buck's chest. 

There was a stir at the end of the hall and a confusion of voices as the people from Denver turned to see what was the matter, and the local stags shifted over to watch the fun. "Ernie, you behave now!" shrilled the little man's girl, who had turned only in time to see the rashly belligerent gesture of her escort. "Nemmine, sister, I'll cool him off," Buck said, grinning broadly, and he seized Ernie by the collar of his coat and the seat of his breeches. The door was too close for Ernie's friends to be of any help. Buck's local admirers drew back, tittering nervously, while Buck swung quickly around through the door to the landing outside. Before anyone could interfere, he had lifted

Ernie over the wooden railing and was holding him, grunting and wildly waving his arms and legs, out over fifteen feet of empty air. Now no one dared intervene. 

Buck's laughter rolled out into the night, as he swung against the creaky railing and Ernie's breeches slid gradually through his grip.

From the middle of the dance floor Phil noticed the commotion, turned and recognized Buck's laugh and his broad back framed in the doorway. "Better see what's up, Molly," he said, and started for the door. Before he got there, Ernie's flounderings had worked his

DUDE RANCHER 

seat out of Buck's fist, and he would have fallen but for a lucky snatch at the rail. Buck leaned over, pulled him up, and dropped him flat on the landing. 

Just as Phil came out, the other men of the Denver party were closing in on Buck with doubled fists. Buck stood with his back to the rail, undecided whether to jump or to face it out. Phil had enough momentum behind the point of his shoulder to carry him through the bunched spectators. "Wait a minute, boys," he said. "What's the matter?"

His entry from behind checked the assault momentarily, but the blur of voices explained nothing. "What's it all about, Buck?"

Buck did not reply, but by this time Phil was close enough partly to sense the cause of the trouble, and he turned to the riding clothes. “He didn't mean any harm, boys. You go back and dance. I'll take care of him.- 

Come on, Buck, let's go downstairs and walk around."

The tension seemed to be relieved a little. The other men drew aside, and one of them got hold of Ernie, who was holding his thumb in a big tear across the knee of his breeches, spluttering, and shaking an implacable fist. 

But this was not the end.

Buck paused a moment uncertainly, looked into the faces of the mollified enemy, then over their heads at his friends in the doorway, and when he turned to Phil there was no trace left of his former good nature. He seemed to feel that he had let someone down. "Who ast you ta s'ick ya long nose inta this?" he demanded abruptly. "Why, nobody, Buck; but you don't want to start a

SQUARE DANCE 

79 fight here, spoil the party. Let's go downstairs and talk it over." "Listen, you stuckup little boy scout, I've took all o' you I c'n stand. I hate your pretty hide, see? An' now 

I'm gonna take you apart." He charged.

His haymaker missed, but his momentum carried him and Phil against the rail, which sprung outward and cracked. Animal dread tied a little knot in Phil's diaphragm, and a salty taste came in the back of his throat, as he reeled away from the edge and got his back to the stone wall. This time he was ready: a straight left to the jaw inside Buck's right swing.

Phil supported the limp weight under the arms and let it gently to the floor. "Sorry, old man," he said, when 

Buck's eyes began to clear. "If that's all you've got on the program for this evening, I'll drive you home and we'll get something for that head."

X.  

THE first weeks in a new place pass slowly, while you become adjusted to strange people, a different program, new ways of doing things. Time moves like a stiff old horse leaving the stable for his first workout after a layoff. He sniffs and paws, limps a step or two, jigs, hesitates, and stretches. But then he finds his stride and settles into a smooth gallop.

July had fled mysteriously for Phil at Square Slash and August was slipping away. For almost a month now, there had been a heavy shower in the middle of every afternoon. The oats pushed ahead and the pastures freshened; but the tennis court was apt to be slick on top, and long afternoon rides fell out of fashion because of the almost certain drenching. 

The group changed as some of the dudes left because their vacations were over, some because they had scheduled stops at other ranches in Montana or Wyoming. 

Connie made a few passes at her nose with her handkerchief when the Wedderspoons left and took her with them. She had burned all of her poems again, exiled 

Freud to the bottom of her trunk, and gained two pounds. Now she would have liked to stay at the ranch and improve her bridge; but Sister Pat confided to

Eleanor that there was just about time to get safely away. Phil missed the three and even felt a little moony, answering Connie's letters. Eleanor Oakes stayed on, the Whites were still there, and a few others. All the 80

children were having a good time. They loved the horses, and Phil invented horseback games to improve their riding and to fill their hours. 

Once he took them on an overnight ride back to the peaks, and they camped at the edge of a tiny lake, with a snowbank a few feet from their fire. 

Before starting home the next day, Phil took his little crowd up the ravine that led from their campsite back to the ridge of the Divide. He was awed by the jumbled throng of rock giants that came suddenly into view, extending endlessly north and west and south. The youngsters felt something of the effect, too, but they were more excited at the thought of being on the ridge between Atlantic and Pacific. For a while, Phil let them ride north along the crest, until it was time to think of home. Then he turned down and eastward along a gently sloping shoulder covered with short grass and alpine flowers. Its direction seemed right, and the going was easier than it would have been down one of the sharp glacial cuts leading more quickly to the forest and the valley floor.

But presently Phil began to be baffled by the pathless expanse of grassy slopes between timberline and the granite ramparts. He tried to conceal his anxiety and led the way back and up once more, hoping to spot from the height something he could recognize. No doubt of general directions: there were the plains off to the east, rising like the ocean away from a distant coast. But it was all too far off to make out any details, and, close at hand, each knoll looked like all the others -flanked by forbidding chasms that plunged into the abyss of dark pine.

DUDE RANCHER 

While he was still undecided whether to risk attempting a descent to the unknown lower end of one of these, a mass of molten blackness raced across from the west and hid the sun. There was no pause at all between the first bright glare of lightning and the explosion of thunder all around them. Everybody jumped, the horses snorted and squatted suddenly on their haunches, a child whimpered, and Phil and Sam headed the crowd downhill fast. Never mind about directions now. Down is the place you want to go if there is going to be a show like that! 

For the next ten minutes the storm banged at them from all sides, and sleet and rain drove over in the whistling wind. Then the clouds were gone as quickly as they had come, and steam began to rise from the sunny sides of the boulders.

Though he was as badly lost as before, Phil did not try the peak again but looked carefully out eastward. 

A tiny flash caught his eye and disappeared. Too small and bright, he thought, to be a lake, and a lake would have stayed put longer than that. Another flash close to where the first had been-then a third a little to the left. "Look out there," Phil said and pointed. "See the sparkles? What do you suppose they are?" Once or twice a minute, now, there was a bright gleam, and Phil noticed that they were not all in exactly the same spot. "Cars, I bet," Sam announced, after he had watched for a while. "Windshields or headlights reflecting the sun. Couldn't be windows of houses, 'cause they don't show longer, and there aren't any houses. Must be cars moving along a road." "You have it, Sam," Phil spoke with relief. "And it's

a main road because we see the flashes so often. Must be twenty miles away!" Then he remembered the state highway that followed the valley ten miles north of the one they had come up yesterday, and he knew where he was, at least well enough to head down the likeliestlooking gulch to his left. It was a painful course they had to follow, through a growth of scratchy spruce so thick that directions were almost lost again, over sharp skeletons of fallen trees, around the lower end of a beaver dam which kept a mile-long swamp above it. But here was an abandoned mine, with a path leading from it down the creek, and at the end of an hour the path emerged in the larger cañon that Phil recognized as the one they had come up the day before.

I was a fool, Phil thought, to bring these babies here without knowing the country myself-never again until I've ridden all over it-couldn't get seriously lost, I suppose-just keep going down hill until you hit a streamthen a ranch or a town-still, I was a fool. 

Though they were soaked twice more with sleet and rain, the children suffered no damage from the outing. 

Sammy's chief worry, Phil observed with pleasure, was not the physical discomfort but the thought that Grease might go through his wood-pile while he was away. But when they got back Sam made Bobby Oakes help, and in two days the boys had enough split and piled for a week ahead.

The highway surveyors came early in August, and all the youngsters tagged after them as they ran their line through the fields near the house. Phil, who did not know the owner's plans, was disturbed to see the stakes outlining the new road so close to the buildings; but

DUDE RANCHER 

it was none of his business, he decided, and made no comments.

Joe Wakefield spent most of his time on trips away from the ranch. Mrs. Hinkle and her aspirin left on an indefinite vacation. Buck found little to do when Joe 

Wakefield was gone and, ever since the night of the dance, had self-consciously avoided Phil and given only the necessary minimum of orders. Hap was useful and energetic but would not take responsibility. And so, much of the planning and operation fell to Molly and Phil, who rarely saw each other except for brief conferences, since they were so busy and their jobs were usually in different places.

Things could not have worked out better for Phil. 

He put in seventeen hours a day learning the little elementary jobs connected with dude ranching and convincing the eight or ten remaining guests that they were having the time of their lives. In the latter he was quite successful. He always invited them to take part in any job he and Hap were doing, and he found them flattered and eager. He decided this was worth while, even if it did slow up the performance sometimes, so that he had to take Hap aside evenings and try to persuade him that the real object of the ranch was to please the dudes, rather than to get the most done in the least time. 

The first shoeing job that Phil did took the better part of a day and the horse moved away from the forge with what Phil analyzed as a slight list to port and a lowering astern. But a re-examination of the shoes revealed nothing critically wrong. "It will be hard to tell which way he's traveling, from those hoofprints," Phil thought. But he was not discouraged, for he remem-

bered Pop's remark that no amount of ambition could substitute for years of experience in shoeing. 

Early in August Mr. White came out from New Jersey for his vacation. He spent a day humbly and proudly learning to split firewood under Sam's direction, then sat on the porch for two days with a crick in his back and made Grease play checkers with him, to the detriment of the menu. After his muscles loosened a bit, he joined 

Phil and Hap in laying a new pipe-line from the spring and, to Mrs. White's acute distress, boasted and proved an astonishing skill with a pipe-wrench. He bossed everything in which he took part. Phil winked at Hap, and Hap let it go at that when he found that any amount of back talk was jovially received and that the work was going faster and more smoothly than it had before.

Bob Saltell dropped in on his way back East. In his pocket was a movie contract, more liberal than he had expected from the great producer's telegram, since a personal interview had convinced the great producer that to the unquestioned value of Bob's name in neon must be added some real possibilities for character parts of a type rather difficult to fill. Bob was bent on exploding the contract in the bosom of his family but doubted whether he would ever bring himself to sign it. His stay at the ranch was even briefer this time than the last, for he found so much work going on that he telephoned Denver in terror and commissioned a taxi to rush him to the airport.

Every week or so Pop would ride in by the back way to see Molly and to leave with her a tiny order for gro-

DUDE RANCHER 

ceries, which she or Phil would deliver by car, the day after shopping. "Guess I'm gittin' spoiled," he said to Phil one day as he put the supplies away on his shelf. "All this fresh meat 'n fancy green fodder. Used to be we'd lay in coffee 'n sugar 'n beans 'n flour, so's we wouldn't have to be always drivin' up to Mineral. Took two days in the wagon, there an' back. Two, three hogs we'd salt down in October, soon's it got cold enough; fresh beef we'd have all winter; an', o' course, deer 'n elk 'n grouse a plenty. "You'd never see all this canned stuff, 'xcept maybe if the wife was the gardenin' sort. Then she'd put down pickles 'n relish 'n maybe peas in glass jars. Most the women got over that, though; they couldn't stir up no help from the men. We thought ranchers was too almighty big to be foolin' 'round with cabbages 'n milk cows. 'N then, what gardens they was was likely et by the rabbits 'n chipmunks, or killed by the frosts in June. 

Coyotes 'd get the chickens 'n turkeys, like they done mine last time I spent the night up to your freak show. 

I guess the women didn't have much fun. That's why I said Molly hadda go to college, git to know somethin' different."

He put the last can away, threw the paper carton toward the stove, and turned to Phil. "I hear tell what you done up to Mineral, 't the dance. Ain't you ashamed, now?" "N-n-o," said Phil, “I really think I had to hit him.

That railing was practically gone after we fell against it, and there would have been real trouble, the way he was charging around the landing. I'm sorry it happened,

because he doesn't seem to forget. But I didn't hurt him much." "That's jest what I mean. Egzackly! You'd oughta be ashamed you didn't scalp him, the big, ornery, fourflushin', perfumed lizard. In th' old days theyda give a bounty fer his hide 'n buried it in quick-lime. Why

Joe keeps him, year in year out, is more'n I c'n see. 

Used to think maybe he had somethin' on Joe; but Joe says not. It just don't make sense. He ain't no use, not even 'round a dude ranch.” "I think I have an idea why it is," Phil said slowly. "Buck looks the part and talks the part; you know, the 

Wild West stuff. That's what Easterners look for on a dude ranch, and Joe thinks they'd miss it. I've been reading one of the dude ranch booklets issued by the railroad. It's full of pictures showing pretty girls in tengallon hats and rodeo shirts, sitting on the corral rails admiring a lean, bronze specimen of the glorious American cowboy, who's spinning a rope and smirking. It seems to be a necessary part of the stage-setting." "Don' know 'bout that," returned Pop. "But I c'n tell you one thing: you take a feller's done nothin' much but punch cows 'n wear chaps 'n tote a big gun fer a few years; he ain't most generally no good at that. An' if you take his cows 'n his rope away from him, he ain't worth the powder to blow his hat off at nothin' else. 'Stage-settin'' you said, an' I guess you're right.
