Real cowboyin', ever' place I ever see, was mostly totin' hay 'n mendin' fence 'n shovelin' manure out the corrals. 

If they want the Wild West stuff, why don't they stick to the movies?—I suppose you're expectin' a ambush by night, now, after what you done."

DUDE RANCHER 

"No, I don't think so," said Phil. "Buck probably won't miss a chance to do me a bad turn, if it ever comes. But any of the movie tricks, like planting an ace in my pocket or putting a rifle-ball through me as I ride out on some lonely vigil, would be too apt to backfire." "That's right," Pop said. "Still, I don't look to see you two in the same outfit the rest o' your lives." "Maybe not," Phil replied absently. "Well, I'll have to get back. I promised Hap I'd help him grind sickles and go over the machinery. We start haying Monday, if the weather's right. The oats have come along fast since the rain, and the kernels are going into the dough. 

Good-by, Pop. Hope we'll see you next week." 

On Monday Hap drove the clattering mower around the receding stand of pale-green oats. Little ripples swept from west to east across the nodding heads, and, whenever the mower stopped for oiling, you could hear a soft rustle in the swaying stems and pendent seed-tops. 

A fine day for curing. 

Phil hitched the "other" team and started behind

Hap with an even older mower; but the team moved so slowly that the stalks jammed the blades. Phil was inexperienced, the machine was palsied. Hap soon gained a lap on him, and he had such bad luck breaking guards and blunting sections on the rocks that finally he took Hap's advice and went back to the shop to grind more sickles.

With Mr. White he spent the rest of the morning knocking out cracked and blunted sections, riveting in new ones, and grinding, grinding, grinding. You could

never have too many sharp sickles, with fields as full of rocks as these. Hap needed a new one before the morning was gone. 

At lunch Phil asked him whether it would be possible to start raking in the afternoon. "Sure," Hap replied. "Weather like this, hay's 'most dry, time it hits the ground. You c'n rake what I cut this mornin'; even start bunchin' it, I believe."

So Mr. White and Phil took turns learning to drive the dump-rake. The boys wanted to try it, too; but Phil had heard of the horrid, mangling death that followed if the driver ever fell off the seat ahead of the teeth and the team did not stop, and so he chased them off to the creek until there should be some bunching to do. At raking, old Brutus and Kate, who were primarily “saddle-plugs" for the youngest children, and probably spent ten hours a year in harness, were perfect for beginners like Phil. In an attitude of tolerance rather than cooperation, they barely kept the traces taut. The rake crawled easily over the ground; there was plenty of time for the teeth to settle into every little hollow and pick up every stem.

When the rake is full, you press your foot on the catch. Clank, and the teeth swing up behind you, dumping a handsome ridge of hay. Clank, and they fall to the ground again, just beyond. You dump every forty or fifty feet, as soon as the teeth are full. The next time past you try to time your kick so that the new loads will line up with those you dropped before, making the windrows straight and true. 

Phil's windrows looked like dizzy mountain chains at first, but near the other edge of the field they were

DUDE RANCHER 

straightening out and taking on a uniform height.

Even at the sleep-walking pace of Brutus and Kate, all Hap had mowed in the morning was raked by the middle of the afternoon, for a rake does its work much faster than a mower. Hap squeezed a handful of hay and shook his head. Bunching would not be wise this afternoon, after all, because too much of the recent rain had been drawn up into the fat stems. "Might do all right," he drawled, "'n then it might spoil. If it was to rain 'safternoon or tonight, you'd wish you had bunched it. If we do bunch it an' it don't rain, you may wish you hadn't, 'cause it might probly sweat. Best thing 'd be if you didn't bunch it 'n it didn't rain. I dunno-leave it up to you." "I wish Joe or Buck were here," Phil said. "What do you think, Mr. White?" "My boy, I'm here to get out of thinking. I wouldn't know anyhow. You'll have to settle it yourself." "We'll leave it," said Phil. "That's just my bet that it won't rain. I've never been much of a gambler; but it looks as if a rancher has to be." Having made the decision, he stopped thinking about it. "I'll round up some horses and take the bunch out for a ride, while you finish the raking, Hap," he went on. "We'll go over to Sandburg's. He tells me they're haying now, and it ought to be fun to see how they do it in the big meadows."

Oscar Sandburg paused in his work on top of the stack to wave at the group riding across the meadow. "How do, Miz Oakes," he called down, as he put two hands like dictionaries on the end of the fork handle and rested his red, glistening chin upon them. "Hel-l-lo,

Phil-1-1. Mr. Vite? Gl-lad to see you. You been Sammy's papa? Dat boy make great hay-hand soamday." He looked across to where the youngsters had begun urging their horses down one of the irrigation ditches that ran across the meadow. “Hye, you kids," he called in a mellow, singsong voice, "better you keep out dat ditch. 

You break da horse l-leg; you spoil da bank. Which horse you got da fastest?" "Mine, mine!" came at once from several throats. “Vel-l-l, you see dat nex' stack? I gif ten cents to vinner 'round dat stack to here back. . . . Vun, two, tree,

GO!" 

The finish line was indeterminate, but it was not difficult to place the winner. Cinder, Bob Oakes up, had stood in the barn all day grinding hay and so made fewer stops to graze. He was first by some eighty lengths -came in at a smart canter and pulled a good mouthful from the corner of the stack. 

Mr. Sandburg let the loaded bull-rakes wait at the stacker while he explained this method of haying. The working part of a bull-rake was a rigid frame of huge, bluntly tapering wooden fingers, pushed ahead of the team and driver. It lifted the bunches or windrows off the ground and pushed them up to the stacker, where it ran the hay up over a similar frame of fingers. This was on the end of a long wooden arm, connected to a cable running through a pulley at the top of a high framework against the stack. When the rake backed away, leaving its load, a team hitched to the other end of the stacker-cable would move away from the stack and swing the long arm up off the ground until the new load was over one end of the stack. From here it fell

DUDE RANCHER 

over of its own weight and was spread out evenly by the man working there. This was a taxing job, with three teams on the bull-rakes, and Mr. Sandburg was glad to have a moment's rest. "Why don't we use bull-rakes, Phil?" Sam asked. "Because we have no real meadows. Our oat hay is grown on land too dry for meadow hay and too rough and hilly for a bull-rake. Any way, we'll load ours onto the wagon and take it to the barn to feed horses, during storms; and milk cows. Mr. Sandburg stacks his in the meadow because he has hundreds of beef cattle to feed there. They graze on the stubble and new growth until the snow covers the ground or the grass is gone. Then they're fed right from the stacks. He'll build a fence later around the stack, of course, so that the cattle can't get at it all at once and tear it to pieces." 

Next morning the light dew rose quickly from the windrows, driven by the brazen sun into the thirsty air.

Again Hap squeezed a handful, and this time he nodded. 

To save time Buck, whenever he was caught for haying, would drive the rake down the length of the windrow, rolling the hay into big, tumbled piles. This was indeed a rather common way of bunching; but it broke the stems and tore loose some of the leaves and heads; and it was an invitation to any sudden shower to soak right through. 

Hap hated it. 

But this morning Buck had elected to "ride the

Prince out to have a look't the grass in the North Pasture," so that it was Hap who led the way and started

bunching in the first field. "Ain't a breath o' wind," he said, looking across to the aspens, whose leaves hung quiet, "nor a sign o' cloud. Hot'n still. Might mean rain. Better not knock down no more 'til we get this in. You sure tied knots in them windrows, Phil. Make a feller cross-eyed."

He started down the row, lifting a good forkful out to the side and laying it flat, placing another flat on top of it, until he had a neat pile up to the middle of his thigh, smoothly rounded on top to shed rain. Then on to start another.

Phil and Mr. White each took a windrow, and even 

Bob and Sam managed to make a few bunches, though there were a good many arguments about "who started this shock?" and some side excursions planned in the naive hope of catching an unseen gopher that kept pushing up a little mound of moist, dark earth from his tunnel.

The smaller children were there, too, running and jumping into the bunches until Hap made them stop and Phil explained that playing in the hay broke and damaged it-that this was a game found mostly in storybooks.

The work of bunching was easy, rhythmic, pleasant; didn't interfere with thinking. At the other end of the field Mr. White was singing an old song in a warm, unruly bass, and now and then he was supported by a wavering treble chorus.

All summer Phil had found little time for his old habit of pondering on the "whys" and "whithers." It was not that he had hit on even the glimmering of an answer to the old questions. Apparently you needed the friction

DUDE RANCHER 

of contact with other minds similarly puzzled and interested in their puzzlement, or similarly intoxicated with the polysyllabic music of abstractions, or similarly bold to risk sanity in a moonlit chase after the Idea. Here you were too busy to ponder much; here the human contacts were too casual and unselected; here you went to bed and fell asleep, instead of looking into the dark. Here was no Dad, no Stanley, no Frank.

Eleanor Oakes had it, all right, the sensitive nose for a vagrant thought and the love of hounding it back to the end of its burrow; but Phil knew that she lacked years of the day when a conversation with her could be impersonal. Pop had it, too, but his speculations always ended in a yarn which sidetracked the thought that had prompted it. The others had it not at all. Not even 

Molly. She hit life from a different side. Her intuitions were mostly about people, and Phil realized that they usually got there ahead of his.

But, if Phil had not spent much time thinking this summer, at least his eyes and ears had been open, and his nerve-ends had been waving receptively. Now, as he paused near the end of the first field and looked out over the ranks of little rounded piles, gray-green on top, each with a puddle of blue shadow beside it, he felt that things were right. Here was the document of man's truce with nature. "I've found it," he said to himself-work that means something; a life out of doors; plenty of people; health; friends, maybe, in time. What else is there?

Then he thought of the dudes who had not joined the hay-crew, and he wondered how the morning was going for them. He called across the field, "Hey, Hap, you'll

have this bunched before long. I'm going back to the house, see what everybody is doing. I'll harness and bring the wagon out. Perhaps we can get in a load before lunch."

At the tennis court a doubles match with a few spectators relieved his mind about most of the ranch guests. 

As he was about to go in the front door, an upstairs curtain was drawn aside, and Eleanor Oakes called down, "Howdy, farmer. Riding this afternoon?"

As usual, the husky voice did something to the back of Phil's neck. "No," he answered, "can't. Gotta stick with these oats. What are you staying in for on a fine day like this? Sulking? Or afraid we might put you to work?" "Neither, Phil. Packing." "Packing!" Phil spoke with surprise and unconscious boyish dismay. "Certainly, stupid. Did you think I was a colonist?" "No, but-oh, Eleanor, be serious. You don't have to go. Any way, you can pack tonight." "For myself, yes. I've got a system, takes fifteen minutes. I open my trunk and bags, dump the closet and bureau drawers in the middle of the room, stand like Primavera beside the pile, and throw things until the trunk and bags are full. Then I put on whatever is left, and I'm ready to go. Always comes out just right, and I never forget anything. But it's different packing for the kids. That's what I'm doing now." "Well," said Phil, "I consider it all an idle pretext and a red herring. I was just coming for you to go out to the oats. It's wonderful out there. We're going to get in the first load before lunch."

DUDE RANCHER 

“All right, Phil. It's a funny time for a serenade, and you look too silly with that grimy face and your shirt tail out. But 'no' is a word I never learned, and you make those oats sound like a moon-soaked bower of magnolias. I'll come out and watch." "You will not," said Phil. "You'll drive the team while we load. You put on your overalls. I'll go get the wagon from the barn and meet you on the way through." 

Eleanor had not come down yet when he came by, and he went to the kitchen for a drink of water and a pail to take to the field.

XI. WHILE THE BUCKET FILLS 

IN the kitchen Phil discovered Molly struggling with

Grease about the plans for lunch. "She's a old bully, Phil," Grease spluttered, and one merry eye disappeared completely. "Makes out a meenyou 'ud poison a blast furnace 'n then expects me to sign my name to it. Parmesan cheese she calls this! Jest heft it once! Petrified, 'f y'ask me. Be worseʼn ground glass. Have me in for willful mur-" "What he means, Phil," Molly interrupted, "is that he's the laziest cook in the world and doesn't know anything but dishing out salt pork and wormy beans for a crew of Mexican track laborers who can't tell him what they think of his vile cooking. But I'm going to stand right here with this cleaver and see that he doesn't put that rat cheese into the macaroni-not after I paid a dollar a pound for the right kind! Come on, Grease, start grating." 

Grease regarded her affectionately, picked up the cheese with mock reverence, pretended to cut his finger on its edge, and put it to the grater. “Dollar a pound!" he groaned. "How many carats? Mind if I take a little, teeny crumb 'n have it set in this here ring? All I got there now 's a old ruby!"

While the water was running into the pail, Phil paused between his first and second dipper and looked around the big clean kitchen. "You're unfair to Grease, Molly," he said. "He's too modest to say so, but I hap- 97

DUDE RANCHER 

pen to know that, before he sacrificed his career to come to us, he cooked in some of the swellest ten-cent lunch joints on Larimer Street."

He turned off the water and added seriously, "But he's lucky to be here-and so am I. The moment I arrived I fell in love with this country. Since then it has grown on me-it and the life. "I had no idea, at first, of all the possibilities; but there's no end to them. You could have a separate group for kids who'd known nothing but the usual summer camp. And it would offer the best setup in the world for tutoring any who needed it. You could start polo for a tenth of what it would cost back East. I have a hundred big ideas for next summer-hope your father thinks they're all right and lets me work on them this winter." 

He took the pail and went out to the wagon. 

As Molly looked after him, the smile left her face.

Though, Joe, as usual, had told her nothing of his plans, she felt pretty sure that Phil was in for a shock. And this enthusiasm, greater than she had guessed, would only make it worse. "Those potatoes are a lot better, Pete," she said. "See what you save by not slicing off so much when you pare them. But look, Pete, I'm afraid you'll have to go over these again and take out all those eyes. See? like this."

XII.  DEAR FRANK: 

Square Slash Ranch 

RFD, Mineral Spring, Colo. 

September 10

I was glad to hear from you and to learn how well things are going. At that rate, the boys at the top better rub the sand out of their eyes. Sorry you're not important enough yet to rate a real vacation, because if you were I'd make you come out here. Next time, maybe, huh? 

And I'm overwhelmed with gratitude at your generous concession that what I'm doing sounds "interesting." Since I wrote you in July, it has grown to be more than that.

This ranch, frankly, is not my idea of the perfect setup; but what I consider its defects have worked out to give me more of the experience I want than would have come in years at a ranch with a big gang of dudes and clockwork operation. Since I'm going to keep a carbon of this, as I did the last, for a sort of record, I might as well give details. It will be all right with me if you skip most of it.

In the first place, my boss, Joe Wakefield, is temperamentally neither a rancher nor a "dude wrangler." He's a queer, uncommunicative cuss, doesn't seem to care much about the ranch or the ranch life, and spends most of his time going and coming. That's one of the 99

DUDE RANCHER 

things I think is bad for the ranch. As in any other work I know of, I think you ought to like it for itself, and you ought to stick pretty close to your job. But the fact that Joe doesn't and hasn't has meant much more opportunity for me to try out some of my own ideas with the dudes and to learn something about ranching. 

Second, the group-staff and customers-has been so small that we have all taken a hand at everything. I have come to know everybody pretty well; and I have learned much more than if I had had a definitely limited rôle, like riding instructor, or buyer, or counselor for the kids. 

Third, the nominal foreman, or manager, is pretty much of a washout with everyone except the boss, who is taken in by his gaudy looks and cowboy getup. He's lazy and secretive, is hardly seen around the crowd all day, with the result that I have had to take charge of lots of the things that he should have planned. The other one, Hap, the foreman's "assistant," is a real doer but young and has left to me many decisions that he was better qualified to make. And the old girl I mentioned as the housekeeper, Mrs. Hinkle, pulled out over a month ago for a week's vacation. She hasn't been heard from since, and this has meant that Molly Wakefield has had to take over. She has done a ten times better job, incidentally, which is in line with my conviction that a dude ranch needs a woman who is emotionally identified with the place and loves it. Well, that hasn't much to do with me, except that if she had been outdoors more, I should naturally have waited for her to take the initiative about some things that I have had to settle.

I sound like quite the executive, don't I? Well, I'm just trying to show that the smallness and creakiness of this place have been an advantage, so far as I am concerned.

We had a rainy spell in July, but since then, except for an occasional shower, the days have been warm and clear and dry. The nights are frosty now. There is a bluish haze over all the sunny peaks that brings them closer and, say the natives, always comes between summer and fall. We had a snowfall, about an inch, a week ago. It cleared and got very cold that night; but next morning the sun came out strong and thawed the south slopes in an hour. There's still snow left, though, under the pines and spruce on the north slopes, and I'm told it will stay there until May, piling up with each new storm. (Ought to mean good skiing, if someone made a trail in the woods.) The flowers, mostly a different set from the ones we had in June and July, are still blooming, just as if they had all year to show off. Pop offered to bet me that he could find wildflowers blooming any month of the year but January. It's a pity the dude season doesn't include the fall.

Our crowd began trickling away toward the end of 

August, and the last were gone a day or so after Labor 

Day. The place seems strange without them, so quiet it's ominous. I've had moments when I simply didn't think of anything to do, a letdown, I suppose, after the bustle of summer. Some of the crowd were fine, and I mean to keep in touch with them. A Mr. White, for instance, whose barony is just across the river from youhis wife had given him such a build-up that I expected a pompous fool. But he turned out to be a regular guy

DUDE RANCHER 

and a ball of fire; took over the whole place, did lots of things better than anyone else could do them, and kept everyone in a good humor. And then there was 

Mrs. Oakes from Boston. You've got to meet her some time. Between you and me, Frank, I think I may have felt a little foolish about her at one time; but I never let her know, of course, and didn't mistake her kindness and charm for anything else. I'm to visit her and her husband and kids if I ever go East, and I have a hunch I might get the kids out here again some summer. 

And then there- Wait a minute. Joe just sent word he wants to see me in his office. I think I'll tackle him right now for his OK on some ideas that I think would go a long way toward building this place up to its possibilities. I'll finish this later. 

Later. Yes, Frank, fifteen minutes later. And I'm going to let all that drivel stand up there, just as I wrote it- Because, Frank, it looks so funny, now that I read it over. Funny? Yes, Frank, funny-because I'm fired!

I didn't even get a chance to mention the things that I thought I'd work on this winter. Joe was there behind his desk, and the foreman, Snagler, was standing with his back to the room, looking out the window. He didn't turn around when I came in nor all the time I was there. But Joe seemed a little uncomfortable, kept looking over at him and then back to a pencil he was rolling between his fingers.

First he said there just wasn't any business for the winter and he supposed I'd want to be leaving soon. 

When I pointed out that I had always known it was only a summer place but had never had a hint from him that the job wasn't a permanent one, he said the

new road was going to enable him to run the ranch under a rather different schedule, one into which he did not think I would fit so well. 

Then I asked him whether I had failed in any of my responsibilities. He began to get a bit short with me and said something about my not getting on well with the staff and my being inclined to take too much responsibility on matters that should have been referred to him or Snagler.

I think I understand what he means about my not fitting into the sort of place he is going to run here; and he may be right, though I should have liked to give it a try. But on the matter of my taking it upon myself to make decisions, he's wrong, and I'm pretty sure he wasn't speaking his own mind. Anyway, I got out so as not to say something.

This may be just sour grapes, but I really think it is a good thing for me to get away from here now and for good. I've probably got all this place can give me, and it's time I was having a look at some others. 

Molly is going to drive me in to Denver tonight, after she takes me over to say good-by to Pop. I ran into her on my way back here. She knew before I told her, and she was swell. 

I'm going to stay in Denver two or three days and brood. I don't know any address to give you, but I'll let you know when I decide what to do. Yours,

PHIL. 

P.S. No, don't try to find anything for me in New 

York. I may disguise myself as a desert rat, get a burro and a pick, and slip off into the great silences.

DUDE RANCHER 

DEAR FRANK: 

Denver, Colorado 

September 25

Still no address. I've been sulking in the cheap hotel whose blazonry adorns this sheet. Wrote to a lot of dude ranches; but most of them didn't answer. Those that did say they are closed up for the winter and have nothing but cows to entertain. I drew my last $180 from the bank at home, spent part of it on an old roadster whose mudguards wave up and down like the wings on a buzzard trying to take off; and tomorrow the buzzard and I are going to begin a tour of the dude ranches, to see whether a personal appearance will do any more than a letter. Yours,

PHIL. 

Victor, Idaho, Oct. 20 

Dear F: No luck up here. Lots of swell ranches, swell country, swell people. Cold, though, now, and no jobs. 

Some leads for next summer. Have to hibernate, I guess.

My picture on other side this card, one with long ears. Phil. 

DEAR FRANK: 

Phoenix, Arizona 

December 12

I had luck. I got here with assets of $3.62 and the buzzard. Hadn't been in town two hours when I sold it to a man and his wife and two kids migrating from southern Kansas to the fruit ranches in California.

Their car had gone to pieces just outside town, and they

were at the garage dickering when I drove up. Garage man was pretty wrathy with me for horning in on his prey, but I couldn't let him get away with what he was doing to that family. Now I have a week's lodging paid for at a tourist camp, a few dollars in my pocket, and a stock of spare parts. The people left their car for me; so I rushed back along the road, stopped a truck, and got the driver to tow the wreck to the tourist camp.

The car was an antique, and I haven't sold any parts yet; but I found a man who is going to give me $2 for the frame after I take off everything I can. 

So I'm in business, after all, and you may hear next that I am an accessories tycoon. But what I really intend to do is stay around here until my week is up, looking for a dude-ranch job. Then if nothing turns up, I suppose the only thing to do will be to join the parade and try to hitch-hike to California and the fruit industry. 

I can wait there until I see whether there is any opening in the spring at one of the ranches I visited. 

After I sent you that card from Victor, a blizzard came along and left two feet of snow and a temperature eighteen degrees below zero. Right then I decided that the idea of hibernating was not so good, and just as soon as the main roads were plowed I hit for a warmer climate to starve in. 

As a matter of fact, I needn't have starved at all. 

When the dude ranchers in Wyoming and Idaho heard my story, most of them opened right up, treated me as an honored visitor, and showed me all they could about their ways of doing things. I'm sure I could have spent the winter there, living off their hospitality and doing

DUDE RANCHER 

odd jobs; but I could see that there was no way for me to be of any real use, and I hated to camp on them. 

Beyond sending you an address, if I ever have one again, I'm not going to write any more until I am either reinstated as a dude rancher or else settled in something of which you would be more apt to approve. I'm seeing the world, you old mud turtle. 

DEAR FRANK: Yours,

PHIL. 

Mineral Spring, Colo. 

February 8 

Yes, that's the address that will reach me for a while. 

Didn't I write you from Phoenix that I would send no more than the address until I had some idea whether I was standing on my head or my feet? Well, I still don't know- Certainly I'm not a dude rancher-but the fact of my being here is so improbable that I have to explain it. 

I came straight from Phoenix, got in last night; and now I'm using my second evening to write a lot of people who must have given up looking for me in the obituary column.

In Phoenix I was fairly on the way to going native, not as a dude rancher, but at something not very far from it, perhaps. I was night superintendent of a tourist camp. The man who ran the place where I stopped when I got there was mighty decent to me; let me sell some old car parts I had and lent me his jalopy to go out looking for a job. His son, who was supposed to get out

of bed and tend to late arrivals, had just gone away to take a place that paid wages. The father was out of the habit of getting up nights, and so, when some people came and rang the bell late, he didn't hear. I did, though, went out and showed them to a cabin. That happened twice in five days, and the old man and his wife offered to let me eat with them and stay at the camp as long as I wanted to in return for acting as night clerk.

In about a week I had tried every single possibility in the neighborhood and could see that there was no ranch job to be had. When I told the owner of the tourist camp, all he said was, "I been wonderin' when you'd stop this goose-chasin', stay home, git some sleep." 

Since I didn't much like the outlook of pushing on to California for the fruit harvest, I settled right down at the tourist camp with Mr. and Mrs. Kruger. But waiting on one or two cars, or perhaps none, a night wasn't much of a day's work; and I persuaded Mr. 

Kruger to start a lunch stand alongside his little grocery and filling station. He and Mrs. Kruger took care of it during the day, and I kept it open all night. Truck drivers and others started dropping in, and I found myself with a real job. 

You wouldn't believe how little ingenuity is required to assemble a hamburger. In fact they are so standardized that any touch of individuality is frowned upon. 

I had a sugar bowl shied at my head one night by a truck driver who was sure I had poisoned him after he followed my recommendation to use a little Worcester-

DUDE RANCHER 

shire sauce on a particularly insipid sandwich. I even got so that I could scramble an egg and replenish the coffee pot. But my waffles stayed so bad that I hid the iron under the shelf every night and got it out just before Ma Kruger came on duty in the morning. 

Well, one day Mrs. Kruger took a snapshot of me in my cap and apron, and, for a joke, I sent it on to Molly Wakefield at Northampton, suggesting that if she and her father couldn't stand me as a dude-wrangler, they might want an accomplished cook's helper. (The one they had last summer was sub-zero.) 

The answer that came back by air mail was as illogical and feminine as a tornado. She was mad! Said she had been trying for three months to get hold of me and did I think it was very decent to run out on people and not let them know, not even my parents, what had become of me. Wanted to know whether it had ever occurred to me that people might need to know where I was. And, what was more, she had never suspected that I was so selfish and inconsiderate. When she finally simmered down to the point, I learned that her grandfather, the "Pop" whom I wrote you about, had fallen through the rotten floor of his loft and broken his ankle. 

That was late October.

It would not have been so bad, but he was alone; had to crawl through the corral rails dragging a halter, out into the pasture, and wait for a horse to come to where he was sitting. (He couldn't go up to the old mare, because it terrified her to see him hitching himself along the ground.) He got aboard somehow, through the gates, and down to the state road. A motorist picked

him up and took him to the hospital in Denver.

He was out, on crutches, in a few days and back at his ranch. When Molly heard about it, she sent word that he would have to get someone to come and stay with him. He wrote back suggesting that she "go climb a rope. 

Then she said that if he didn't get someone, she would leave college and come out herself. After that 

I guess the sparks flew from both sides. Any way, it occurred to her that Pop and I had got along well last summer and that, if he could be persuaded it was for my sake, he might be willing to have me there. He consented to this, and that was when she began hunting for me. Meanwhile, she talked him into letting Sandburg's kid ride over once a day to do the chores. 

But she likes to have it all her way, and she felt that as things stood it was nearer a victory for Pop. So the main point of her letter was that if I was capable of any decent feeling at all, I would lose no time in getting back to Mineral Spring. A scrawl from Pop in the same mail that brought Molly's orders confirmed the arrangement and enclosed railroad fare, which I did not need.

I waited only until the Krugers had got their son back (I think they were delighted at the chance) and came on here.

So far Pop has only expressed himself to the effect that anyone else who had been wished on him would have been more useful, but that the satisfaction he will get when Joe learns I am back will make up for some of the trouble I shall be to him. 

Now, if I am to stay on the track that brought me

DUDE RANCHER 

here, all I have to do is to make Pop into a dude rancher again.

Write soon, factually. Save the editorial comment for about five years longer. Yours,

PHIL.

XIII. LUMBER 

WHEN Phil got off the train in Denver that afternoon, the air was cold. It had snowed hard all the previous day, then cleared during the night; and, as always when the wind fails to rise after a snow, the temperature went down and down. Phil was eager to get out to the ranch and took the only simple way he could think of: he rode the trolley car to the edge of town, carried his bag over to the highway, and started hitch-hiking. 

Almost at once he realized that his battered fedora, oxfords, light silk socks, and summer topcoat, all of which had served well enough in Phoenix, were not the most sensible costume for a jaunt into the mountains in midwinter. And, as the snow squeaked under his numbing feet, it came to him that he ought to buy some winter clothes before holing in with Pop. But, hating the idea of retreat, he folded up the little collar of his coat, pulled his neck down in as far as he could, and turned his back on town. 

Before he got his first lift, the pearly sun slipped into the clear below that hanging ice-mist, then dropped undemonstratively into the purple crags of the main range. He kept on, carrying his bag in his left hand most of the time, so as not to bump it into the two-foot ridge that the snowplow had thrown up. Lights began to come on in the filling stations and in the houses of city workers whose five-acre tracts bordered the highway. A few cars passed him without stopping, because 111

DUDE RANCHER 

he looked like anyone walking home from the end of the trolley line. 

But at last a diminuendo of chains rattling against mudguards told him that he was to have a lift, and he climbed in beside a plumber whose cottage was three miles beyond. He was a little warmer when he started walking again; but there were fewer houses now, and, looking back along the straight white ribbon, he could see no cars at all.

As daylight waned, the high moon, penetrating the crystal curtain, began to shed its pale, shadowless glow over the flat fields; and the grim range of peaks in the distance ahead seemed to be giving off its own unearthly light. Thermometer must be down around fifteen or twenty, Phil estimated, and set down his bag to rub his ears and stamp. 

Later a wavering yellow light on the snow ahead announced a car coming from behind. Better luck this time. "You sure picked a cool one to go walkin',” the salesman said. "How far you going?" "About twenty-five miles, I think," Phil replied and spread his fingers in front of the heater. "You?” "Me? I'm stoppin' at Mineral tonight. I'll see the hardware man first thing in the morning, and maybe after that they'll have the pass cleared so I can get on over to Eagle and Glenwood. Sure is tough making my schedule after a snow like yesterday.-You got more nerve 'n me, walkin' out into the country on a night like this. Keepin' right along this road?" "Yes, I want to get out at a little side-road about four miles this side of where you start up over the Hump." "Hump? You musta been away. Road don't go up

LUMBER 

113 over the Hump any more, not since the middle last month. Sticks right to the bottom. It's awful rough for a few miles, but it'll be a lot easier than the old way, soon as they finish working on it in the spring. A guy's putting in a big filling station and a lot of other stuff along the new road. I hear he's gonna open up next summer with some kind o' resort or lodge or something. Dancing and all.” "A pretty good location," Phil muttered absently and sat thinking, until it was time to get out at Pop's road. The salesman looked at the little unbroken trail starting up through the snow-bent spruce. "Listen, buddy," he said, "you can't kick up through there tonight, not in those shoes. Must be a foot and a half o' new snow. Looks like it hadn't been broke all winter. Better come on in to Mineral and spend the night." "No, thank you,” Phil said. “I'm very grateful for the lift; don't know what I'd have done. But I'll be all right from here. Only a step. Good night."

A mile and a half is "only a step" on a fine day in summer; but on this night Phil knew that he was in for something, even before he had rounded the first curve. Up hill. The new snow knee-deep or worse. The old crust under it giving just enough to catch his feet with every step. His bag plowing its own furrow and trying to turn him sideways. He began to pant and had to open his mouth to breathe. But his lungs could not stand the air rushing in direct, and he held his mouth against the lapel of his coat, sucking in through the wool. This made breathing harder; so he gave it up and tried to keep his face entirely inside the coat.

DUDE RANCHER 

After half a mile he stopped, put the bag down, and sat on it to catch his breath. He wondered whether it would be better to go back down the trail to the main road and wait for another car. But that was a gamble, too, and he decided against it. He did not feel cold any more, and something inside him kept arguing that he could go ahead much better if he sat and got a good rest. None of that, said another voice, severely; so he got up, leaned in the right direction, and fell forward, step by step. Further on, he wasn't quite sure where, he dropped the bag when his fingers refused to stay curled around the handle. He left it where it fell. Once he stumbled over a root and went flat on his face, and this time it was a real struggle to leave the comfortable mattress of snow. Once he found himself feeling his way through thick woods, then remembered he had a road to follow, and stepped out into the clear. 

Later, when he had forgotten that he was going anywhere in particular and was floundering on with a certain detached interest in seeing how far this fool could keep it up, something pressed against his waist and stopped him. Without taking his hands from his pockets, he pushed forward two or three times with his middle but was only shoved gently back each time. A wave of anger swept through him: he slapped roughly at the thing that was holding him, and a little pain stabbed his wrist.

That made him conscious that he was against Pop's barbed-wire gate. His hands refused to do anything with the fastener. Lying down in the snow, he rolled under the bottom wire, got up and went on to the house, around to the side, and across the stripe of yel-

LUMBER 

115 low light below the porch window.

His knock was followed by a grunt, a scraping noise inside, and an irregular thumping across the floor; and then the door swung in.

Pop stood there in his long woolen underwear, leaning on his crutches. Without speaking, Phil slumped across the sill and dropped onto the nearest chair. It was only then that Pop recognized him under the city clothes. "Phil!" he whispered, and then his words came fast, "Phil Marshall! Didn't look for you tonight. How'd you git here? You're froze, boy! D' you leave your horse out to the barn?” "No, I walked, Pop," Phil's speech jolted out through a shiver that made the chair squeak, and he drew in a deep breath that became a tremolo sigh with the little convulsions in his diaphragm. He managed a sheepish smile. "Walked? Walked! Well, of all the Judas Priest, I never see such a— Here, you git them duds off quick while I fetch a bucket o' snow." "All right, Pop, but just let me sit here and rest a minute." "You c'n rest later. Take off them clothes. You're froze." "No, I'm not even cold, Pop, honestly." "Not cold! That is bad. Prob'ly worse'n I thought."

For two and a half hours Pop and Phil rubbed snow on feet, hands, and ears. The pain did not begin until thawing was complete; then it stabbed again and again, and a fierce itching spread over his insteps and up his shins.

DUDE RANCHER 

In the end there were only a few small white patches that had no feeling and responded to no amount of rubbing. Pop looked very pleased as he set the snow bucket aside, threw more wood on the fire, and prepared to make coffee. "I ain't agoin' to tell you what I think o' you tonight," he said, "'cause you're too spent 'n stupid to listen. Do you more good in the mornin', anyhow. Take your mind off what them toes'll be doin' to you."

Phil slept, in spite of the itching; but he was roused early by the pressure of the huge blisters that were rising over the white spots. Putting his hand down to feel his toes, he was horrified by the drum-tight balls, as large as eggs. As soon as he moved his feet, he became aware of his pulse, pounding unmercifully under each hot blister. There was no chance of further sleep. 

He lit a match and looked at his watch. Quarter past five. He got up to start the fire and had a sort of savage pleasure in pushing his screeching feet against the floor and feeling the taut membranes stretch.

At breakfast Pop did take Phil's thoughts away from his toes. "Phil," he began, "you're luckier than any such an idiot had ever ought to be. You must have a constetootion like a steam engine to've got here at all; an' you've got the cleanest set o' tubes I ever see, er you'd of lost both feet 'n maybe your hands. I wouldn'ta give them feet one chance in a dozen when I first seen 'em last night. .. How cold you think it was?" • • "It was cold, all right. Ten, twelve degrees?" "No, not that bad. Cold, though. Five it was. Five degrees below zero, and that's the night you have to pick to go fer a stroll, alone, up a mile an' a half o'

LUMBER 

117 cañon half full o' snow. I s'pose you thought that there moonlight was jest elegant, didn't you?" "Well, it was, for a while, anyway. Below, over the plains as I came across, there was a high mist, not quite cloud. But in the hills the moon shone bright as day, and the pines were all bent under a twinkling load of jewels." "Mmmm. I thought so. Know why they was bent? 

Oh, 'scuse me, course you don't, 'cause you don't know practically nothin'. So I'll tell you. Hear that wind jest comin' up in the pines? See what it's adoin'? Blowin' the snow off in big chunks, ain't it? What you s'pose it's adoin' to all the snow on the ground 'tween here n' the state road? It's apilin' up drifts, four, five, ten feet high. "Wind most always comes up right after a snow, maybe couple hours, maybe eight or ten. Once in a while it waits more'n a whole day, like now; 'n then it's stronger, piles quicker. "Now I ain't even gonna mention them shoes 'n pants you wore, nor you havin' no gloves, nor thatthat ol' penwiper you had on top your head. Even you know how dumb that was. "But you listen an' try t' git this straight: don't you never start no place alone, in this country, long as you c'n see the snow still on the trees. Don't matter if somebody's adyin' er even if you got a girl waitin'. You stay put 'til the snow's off the trees. That wind coulda come up in five minutes last night, any time. You'd no way o' knowin'. Even with no wind, you was lucky to get here. But if it had of rose while you was on that road, you'da been dug out jest about the time we'll git your

DUDE RANCHER 

bag, maybe a month. An' I wouldn'ta hadda set up with you, neither, like I done."

Phil listened attentively and accepted Pop's verdict with humility. And he wisely refrained from countering with any remarks about a man of sixty-five who fights to be left for the winter in a lonely cabin, after he has been laid up with a broken ankle. 

Pop's storm of words went on and on, ranging through lyric, epic, didactic, and satire, until Phil longed for a letup. Then he made an explanatory guess: Pop was playing squid. He felt guilty, himself, feared that Phil would show disapproval or sympathy at his condition, and wanted to be sure to beat him to the offensive.-Besides, he'd had no one to talk to for months. So Phil quietly let the flood run its course and subside, then, in a casual way, began to talk about building a new floor in the old barn loft. 

The old skin sloughed off Phil's toes in a few days, though the itching kept up for weeks; and all the rest of the winter his feet and ears were abnormally sensitive to cold. But he was able to work and did, mostly at getting out logs. He had a natural swing and rhythm, perhaps connected with years of rowing in college, that were very helpful in learning to use an ax. 

Pop was astonished at the speed with which he caught the trick of keeping his bite small and even and close to the ground, and of dropping the tree where he wanted it to go. Often, to check his control, he would place a rock or a stake where the tree should fall, then try to drive it into the ground with the main trunk. 

Though Pop threw his crutches into the front room a few days after Phil's arrival, he still used a stick to

LUMBER 

119 get out to the woods. To his speechless rage, he found that he could not use an ax at all: the twisting of his body and the shock of impact carried too much pain down into the bad ankle. So he would sit on a log until 

Phil had dropped the tree; then both men would work with the big crosscut saw, cutting the log into lengths, twelve- or sixteen-foot "saw-logs" from the main trunk; short, knotty pieces and crooked branches for firewood. "Got more'n a plenty fer that loft floor," Pop announced one noon. "Better hitch Blackie 'n start snakin' 'em down. Lot easier while they's snow." "How do we make 'em into boards," Phil asked. "We? We don't. Ain't got no saw ner nothin' to drive it with if we did. Left the outfit with Joe when 

I come over here, 'n he jest let it rot. What we do is snake 'em to the house, load 'em on the wagon, three, four to a load, haul 'em down t' the highway, 'n stack 'em. Then I'll git Tom Nelson to come over when he can, load 'em on his truck 'n saw 'em for us up to his mill. He'll deliver the boards down 't the road again, or even bring 'em up here, if our wagon tracks don't drift in too bad." "Sounds wrong to me," Phil said. "Oh, it does, hey? So you're a expert now? Well, bein''s you savvy everything so quick, s'pose you tell me what's wrong, what else we c'n do."

Phil knew from Pop's comparative mildness and the fact that he did not threaten to lose his manners, that he must be impressed, a little at least, and he went on with confidence. "Well, in the first place, if you saw that green timber into boards without letting it season, it'll warp and crack, and split out around any nail big

DUDE RANCHER 

enough to hold it down on the joists. Isn't that right?

I sent for a bulletin on lumbering, and that's what they say." "Don't matter s' much fer a loft floor," Pop said rather uneasily. "'Sides, you was the one was ahollerin' we gotta git a floor down." "I can watch out for the holes in the old one while we wait for this to season," Phil spoke authoritatively. "In the next place, the program you've outlined calls for a lot of hauling back and forth and hiring of work done that seems to me wasteful. Why don't we buy a secondhand saw outfit, set it up right here, and saw our own lumber? Of course, I'm no sawyer, wouldn't have any idea how to run a mill. But I thought maybe you'd watched enough to-" "You-you thought maybe I'd watched! Why, dang your skin! I cut my teeth on old sawblades my pappy couldn't use no more! Time I was eight years old, I c'd slap a log agin the blocks 'n peel off a board twenty feet long 'n a quarter inch thick. Boy, I was weaned on sawdust; an' if they's any sawyer in this state knows everthing they is to know 'bout runnin' a mill, you're atalkin' to him, an' don't you fergit it." 

Phil went on, "That's fine, then. Why don't you and

I go into town and hunt up a secondhand outfit and an old automobile engine-or, better, buy an old truck and get a power pulley for the rear hub? Then we'd have power and transportation, too." He could see that 

Pop was inwardly excited about the idea but not convinced. "I ain't never used nothin' only steam," he protested.

LUMBER 

121 "An' a steam rig'll cost plenty, or it's no good. Don't know nothin' 'bout these here gas engines.-You're goin' crazy 'n startin' me off, too. What's the sense o' all that machinery fer jest a few boards? Now you fergit it 'n make a fire in that there stove while I open the cans." 

During dinner Phil pegged away. “You never can tell when you might want to build something, and if you did, a big pile of seasoned timber and planks and slabs would be pretty handy. If we had that truck, I'll bet we could haul our scrap to Denver and sell firewood to pay for all our fuel. “I'm no maniac about gas engines, but I've doctored 'em a little, and if you tend to the saw, I'll keep the power flowing. "What we ought to do is keep getting out logs as long as the snow lasts, then next spring, after plowing or whenever you say they're ready, start making lumber.

We ought to get a truck or an old car, anyway, whether we ever run the saw or not. I lost two days the time I had to hitch-hike into Denver to get my work clothes." "I ain't alistenin'," insisted Pop, “an' you c'n jest save all your big ideas to put yourself to sleep with.”

So the next day they both hitch-hiked into Denver and made the rounds of the secondhand machinery lots. Pop's eyes gleamed as he picked out the tracks and clutch and pulleys and belts. "Stole 'em!" he exulted, limping beside Phil to the used-car dealers. "Practically stole 'em! Don't know how they could be so cheap. 

Must be nobody's asawin' these days. Know what I'm gonna do? We saved s' much on that there stuff, I'm

DUDE RANCHER 

gonna git us a bran' new saw. Yes, sir, a bran' new saw. 

Ain't nothin' purtier 'n the shine on a bran' new blade. 'Bout forty inches diameter'll be big enough, I guess.' 

Phil selected an old "pickup," a passenger car chassis with a small truck body behind. It had space enough for the supplies and parts, with three trips; and there were overload springs which made it possible to carry a ton in a pinch. But there was a question whether the power was sufficient or the gear ratio right to run a fortyinch "rip," and so they bought for almost nothing the wreck of a huge touring car whose engine was in good shape.

For the next week everything else was neglected while Phil tore the old engine out and bolted it to a heavy frame, and Pop worked at assembling and leveling the mill. They were both so excited that they did not realize this could all be done just as well after the logging was over. But it was a pleasant change of occupation and wasted only the week, plus a day they spent running the mill "to see if she'll go right," plus the green log they sawed up to prove she would. And the oozing slabs and boards were not entirely wasted, for they served well enough as a temporary roof over the engine-shed.

The new activity seemed to accelerate Pop's recovery. 

He broke his stick over the stove and put it into the fire late one evening when it was very cold and he felt too lazy to step out to the woodpile. And, though he never lost his limp, he became just as spry as Phil had ever seen him.

Phil spent his evening studying the farm bulletins

LUMBER 

123 published by the Department of Agriculture, or writing letters, or when he was sure Pop was asleep-drawing strange architectural sketches which he kept folded in a copy of Vergil's Georgics.

XIV. SPRING THAW 

BETWEEN February and June Phil had to revise several times his idea of the word "spring." There were a few blustery winter days after his arrival: cold wind, broken masses of cloud rolling over from the west; a pale and timid sun for a few moments; then gray again, the wind louder and stronger, rattling the ridge-boards on the old barn and sighing loudly through the pines; flurries of sharp, granular snow, driving horizontally and stinging your face, until you tried to think of some work you could be doing in the barn or in the house. 

But then came such a horrible tumult of wind one night that Phil was sure the house could not stand. 

We'll freeze, he thought, in spite of any fire. Prepared to set down a record temperature, he took his flashlight, drew a deep breath, and ran out to look at the thermometer. His glance ran inches up the glass and finally hit the top of the red column at forty-one degrees. Pop had a good laugh.

The next morning there was no wind, no sign of cloud; and the fat yellow sun went right to work. By noon little red rivers were running in all the dips, and for the first time Phil caught a smell in the air that made him tingle. After dinner, coats were left in the house. The next day was the same; and the next; and still others. Pop interrupted logging to plow and harrow the first field. "I expected a long winter," Phil wrote to his mother, 124

SPRING THAW 

125 "at this altitude and so close to the Divide, but I was badly fooled. Spring is here already. Today, March second, I found clumps of little light-purple flowers, like daisies, on a dry south slope. The whole plant stuck less than an inch above ground, but the flowers were as big as wild asters. And around the old dry spikes of the mountain sage, right at the base, are little fuzzy tufts of new gray-green." 

A day or so later he understood why the flowers grew so low. After a sunny morning that duped even a few chipmunks, dark clouds rolled over again, and in the afternoon it began to snow, big wet flakes that soaked into the ground for hours, melting everywhere except on the sawn timber and the waiting logs. Then finer flakes that came faster, so thick in the searching wind that you couldn't see from the barn to the house. It was still snowing next morning and kept it up for two more days and nights, piling up to three feet on the level. This time, after it had cleared but before the wind came, the thermometer read fifteen below zero. 

The cattle had to be coaxed out of the hollows where they would have starved. It took two days to get the last of them gathered around the stack in the meadow. The first calf was born and froze to death before it dried. 

Elk and deer slipped down from the woods to nibble the willow shoots along the creek and dispute the hay with the hungry cattle. The corral was full of juncos, working over the manure, flying in and out of the loft. 

But the wind rose again, reached its hysterical climax about ten o'clock, and Phil looked somewhere near the right place on the thermometer: forty-eight degrees.

During the next hot spell his letters recorded anem-

DUDE RANCHER 

ones and sand lilies, and the first violets in the moss beside the creek. He was not entirely fooled, however, nor much surprised at the blizzard that followed.

The grand rhythm of storms worked so that the frost never got back into the ground again after that first plowing; for the intense and killing cold came always after a protective fall of snow. By the middle of April the new grass was tempting the cattle back to the hillsides, and little calves went crazy with delight, bucking on stiff legs around the haggard cows. One moonlit evening when Phil had gone back to the woods to get his ax, which he remembered needed grinding, he almost walked into a cantankerous old bear, rooting for grubs at the base of a rotten stump. 

But the snows still fell, even oftener in May, and the wind was just as strong but always warm and fragrant now. Some of the biggest pines, their roots insecure in the softened, shallow topsoil, crashed and tore up great chunks of bedrock.

As early as April the spring work, plowing, seeding, mending fence, vaccinating calves, repairing harness and machinery, began to press so hard that lumbering was much neglected, and the loft floor had to wait. 

About this time Phil got two letters that made him pause and think. The first was from his old roommate, Stanley, who had just received a three-years' renewal of his appointment. This meant that he would be assured an assistant-professorship and an academic future as soon as he had completed the work for his doctorate. "Your hermit's dell sounds fascinating," Stanley wrote, "and I often almost envy you. You are not the type that would ever really rot. But what of your intel-

SPRING THAW 

127 lectual life? You liked teaching, once, Phil. Why don't you come back here and use your training? I am sure you could get letters from the Department that would get you started, right here, for example, and you could work on your Ph.D. I can't see you spending the rest of your life as a farm-hand."

Phil tilted his chair back on two legs and looked out the window for five minutes, nibbling the inside of his lip. Then he drew a deep breath, chuckled, and showed the letter to Pop. "Ambitious fer you, ain't he?” Pop said. "What's this here intellectual life?" "I'm not sure," Phil replied slowly, "but I think the intellectual life means hiding in a pretty college town and trying to feel sorry for the rest of the world." "Be right nice, seems to me," Pop said demurely. "Uh-uh. Where are the chains for the truck? I'm going down to the corral and get a load of uh-fertilizer for the four-acre triangle. Should have done it a month ago." "Bobby's going to flunk his French," said the other letter, which was from Eleanor Oakes. "He has the fatuous notion that he will pass his arithmetic, but that is because I have concealed from him his maternal taint in matters mathematical. Howard and I are going to take the summer off and go somewhere, Alaska, perhaps, or the Laurentians. It's the first chance we've had to be away together in aeons. I'm going to deposit 

Phyllis in a small camp in Vermont. And now here is where you come in: you are going to interrupt your idyll to the extent of bullying Bobby for two hours a day in French and arithmetic. He has a similarly doltish

DUDE RANCHER 

schoolmate whose parents I have talked with, and I think he'll be coming, too. Their studies will have to be taken seriously, for there is to be a day of judgment in the fall; but the rest of the time you can let them graze with the cattle, or break and lose your tools. You will want to get them their own ponies and saddles, of course. . . . I know what you will say is the objection; but I saw enough of you to know that you can handle that old war horse, if you really try." "Nothing doing," Phil wrote back promptly. "You needn't think you can palm off your wretched offspring on me. And you are wrong in your opinion that I could handle Pop: I'd sooner walk into a buzz saw than try. 

Furthermore (try to pay attention and grasp this), there is no plumbing on this place, and that means no hot and cold showers. No bathrooms. No cook. No doctor within miles. Are you listening? No telephone. No dietician. None of the niceties that have always stood between dear Bobby and the annihilation he deserves.

Now, you run along and enter him in any of the dozens of places you know of that are organized for the sort of self-destruction you would like to wish on me.”

Phil completed the letter and put it into the envelope but did not seal it. He turned to Pop. "Do you like kids, Pop?" "Fried or roast?" "Well, either one. But what I mean is do you like them the way they are before that? You know, the way you like the yearling heifers rough-housing in pasture?" "Oh, that way. I dunno if I like 'em or not. Sight o' trouble, I expect. 'N you can't never tell how they'll grow out. Why?”

SPRING THAW 

129 "U-h-h-h- Some of those big cattle ranches I visited in Wyoming last fall took a few Eastern kids every summer. Showed 'em how to ride and handle a horse." "So that's it, hey? That's what's itchin' you! First Joe; now you! You must be more of a jug-head 'n I ever guessed. . . . An' that's puttin' it purty strong. ... "I want you to get this straight, Marshall, if you can get things straight: I like you, see? I can make a good rancher out o' you, an' a good sawyer, an' maybe one, two other things a man c'n be without blushin'. Least, I thought I could 'til you let fly jest now. But if you don't know no better'n to try to make a dude roost out o' my ranch, you jest better throw your duds together 'n drive in to the train tonight. Jest leave the truck at the depot. I'll git somebody to pick it up and sell it."

Pop hitched his big chair around so that his back was toward Phil and sat panting with rage and excitement. 

Then he stood and started walking up and down the cabin floor. "I ain't wantin' you to go, Phil," he said at last, rather sadly, "but you'd oughta have more sense, boy, honest." "Sorry, Pop," Phil said, then turned back to the table, took his letter from the envelope, and added a postscript: "Just walked into the buzz saw. Must go now and lick my wounds."

A few evenings later Phil strolled down through the dusk, heavy with the scent of young blooms, to the mail box at the main road. Back at the cabin he read his telegram, dated May first:

SPLENDID JUST WHAT BOYS NEED YOU ARE GREAT SALESMAN STOP BOYS LEAVE HERE DAY AFTER SCHOOL

DUDE RANCHER IS OVER JUNE EIGHTH OTHER BOY LAWRENCE BAYLOR HAS HAYFEVER SERUM STOP I AM TELEGRAPHING SO YOU WILL HAVE PLENTY OF TIME TO LINE THEIR HUTCH STOP BLANKETS AND CLOTHES COMING TO YOU BY EXPRESS DENVER EVER SO GLAD YOU CAN TAKE THE BOYS THEY WILL LOVE IT ELEANOR 

Phil worked fast. "You remember Mrs. Oakes, Pop, the one that was at the ranch last summer?" "Neat little woman? Awful purty? Laughed in her eyes?" 

Phil nodded. "Sure I remember. Met her out ridin' once. She was lost. Had quite a visit. Nice woman. She write to you?" "Yes. Funny thing. She wanted to send her boy out here, and a friend of his, for me to tutor. She must be crazy. Of course I wrote her right away and told her how you hated children. And I explained how bad it would be for the boys, too, in a rundown old place like this, without any of the nice things and the conveniences they've always had. I never thought she was such a fool." "Fool, hey? She might know a sight more 'bout that 'n what you do. Be the best thing c'd happen to 'em." "That's what she says; but you don't understand,

Pop. These boys probably have their own private bathroom and a butler to serve their meals and a chauffeur to drive them to school. They've been well brought up." "Phil, you make me tired. If that's what you think is the proper bringin' up, I'm supprised at you. You'd

SPRING THAW 

131 oughta git them kids out here jest's quick's you can 'n start makin' men out of 'em. Bathrooms 'n butlers, yah!" "But I'm trying to learn ranching, Pop. I can't take the time off to teach a couple of kids." "Wouldn't take much time. Couple months. Besides, kids'd prob❜ly like to help out, save you more time 'n their schoolin' would take." "Well, I don't agree with you, Pop. Anyway, it doesn't matter, because I told her flatly nothing doing." "Phil, sometimes I c'd scalp you! Here you got a chance to do somethin' you can do. Few hours work with a couple fine boys. Help out a nice lady like Miz 

Oakes. . . . Ain't you got no gumption? If you don't git right in that truck now, drive up to Mineral, send her a wire, 'Come ahead,' why, why, s' help me, I'm agonna git me a book, tune up m' learnin' 'n teach 'em myself. Now you git goin'."

Phil got going and so did Pop. First they yanked the rusty nails that held the front door shut, threw out the accumulation of old junk, and burned most of it. Pop decided it would be better if he and Phil added on a room to sleep in. With the main room just for cooking and eating, the boys would feel more at home, and they could have a quiet place to study. The logs were not seasoned properly, but Pop selected some that he thought would do, and the saw screamed, slicing off two-by-fours for studs; two-by-sixes for joists and rafters; sheathing and slabs, for the walls. "What have you got against bathrooms, Pop?" Phil asked, as he maneuvered a big log onto the saw carriage.

DUDE RANCHER 

"Ain't got nothin' agin 'em. Prospector I knowed struck it rich one day. First thing he does is build him a great big bathroom, bigger 'n his shack, right slap 'longside it. All shiny tiles inside, it was, 'n the tub 'bout 's big 's a coal car. Trouble was he couldn't git no water to it, bein' winter 'n everthing froze up. Come spring, he was too broke. Raised some dandy chickens in it, though. Bill Miles his name-son Bill jest like him-always in over his head, 'til he married Molly's chum, Ethel Blake, that was. Settled down purty sensible, accordin' to what I hear now. Why? Was you thinkin' we'd oughta build us a bathroom?" "I wish we could. But we couldn't get water, either, could we? Not without an expensive pumping system?" "Don't see why not. See that spring north the house? 

Never been dry, that I know of. Plenty drop. Jest run four, five hunderd feet o' pipe." "And then we could have water in the kitchen, too, with a sink and hot water tank. But how about winter?" "Cut her off at the spring and drain the works. We done all right last winter, didn't we? Sink'd be handy to keep onions in. 'Nother thing, we gotta git us a milk cow, 'n some hens. Kids has got to have real milk 'n eggs."

So far Phil had seen his way through. The material for most of the additions and alterations could be made at home. Window sash and shingles and flooring and hardware could probably be paid for. But now things were beginning to look different. Bathroom fixtures and hundreds of feet of pipe and cows and chickens would all have to be bought, and they would run into real money. And water coming into the house, he sud-

SPRING THAW 

133 denly realized, would mean a sewage system going from it. What do you do about sewage? And, oh, yes, more chairs and beds and tables. And horses. And how about a bigger stove? And a cook? Saddles and bridles? Insurance? What's it going to do to Pop's taxes? And, that's right, dishes and silverware. If we get a cook, where's he going to sleep? And we'll have to hire labor to get all these things done in time.

Phil sat up later than usual, chewing a pencil and scowling at the figures. He feared Pop hadn't thought of all this. "Look, Pop," he said one night, "I'm afraid we're getting in too deep-or, rather, I'm getting you in too deep. Do you know how much all this is going to cost?" "Good deal, prob❜ly. Why?” "Well, I have no money, you know. Do you want to pay for it? Can you pay for it?" "Mmmmm, I dunno. I could sell the steers I was gonna sell in the fall. The boys'll pay their board, won't they?" "Yes, of course; but if you sell the steers to pay for this, what do we live on next winter? Where does my princely wage come from?" "Mmmmm. Gosh, I'll have to think. I could-no, I ain't gonna. I'll have to think.”

As I feared, Phil thought, Pop is just a little unrealistic. 

The work dragged behind schedule, and it was clear that things would not be nearly done on time. 

The extra carpenter helping Pop, the mason who was making the septic tank were paid up to date, and there were plenty of groceries on the shelf. Sookey, the cow,

DUDE RANCHER 

did her part and had no maintenance cost. The hens ate table scraps, mostly, and there would be more when the boys came. But all the building materials had been charged to Pop's account in Mineral Spring. And, though there would be no pressure on the bills for some time, he, too, seemed to catch some of Phil's worry.

Phil had written his father only a sketchy outline of what he was up to. Now he sent off a thick air-mail letter, enclosing some figures and asking whether it would not be wiser to call off the whole thing, or as much of it as could be called off. "NO YOU ARE TIRED

THAT IS ALL TAKE IT EASY," came the answer by telegraph, and then an air-mail letter, "I like your idea, Phil, and I wish I could help. As you know, I have overlooked the matter of building an estate. I suggest, however, that you pay your workmen religiously, from week to week or however it is arranged, but that you do not pay at once the whole of any big bill for materials. Paying a little at a time, you may be able to keep your accounts open until things clear up. Most dealers in building supplies have accepted the necessity of being bankers as well, and I have never known of litigation against an account on which something, even something small, was being paid regularly. From what you say, I imagine Pop's credit rating is pretty good. And if ever you run into a real emergency, I can probably put the screws on some of my more perfunctory patients for two or three hundred dollars. Most important, remember there will be other summers."

Then Phil had a bright idea but was sorry it had come so late. "If we had ten boys," he said to Pop,

SPRING THAW 

135 "instead of two, we shouldn't have to have more bathrooms and septic tanks and cows in proportion. We might break even or possibly make something. Do you mind if I try?"

Pop spat a mouthful of nails into one hand, put a foot up on the sawhorse, and looked sharply at Phil. 

Then he laughed. "Phil," he said at last, “if I didn't know you was just plain dumb, I'd begin to think you was pretty smooth. No, boy, go ahead; turn the place into a nursery. You'll have to do their washin', though, 'cause I'll be too busy mendin' an' darnin'. "But you won't git nobody, late's it is," he added.

Phil sat down that night and composed a modest little sheet, describing the country in one paragraph and the new tutoring school in the other. Early in the morning he drove to Mineral and left it with the printer at The Republican. And when it was ready two days later, he mailed out two hundred copies to the names on the list he had been making. It all seemed pretty simple. Ten more customers, he figured, that's only five per cent response, and we'll be on easy street.

A week later the answers began trickling in, a good many as the days went by, but not nearly so many as 

Phil had expected. His old friends were glad to hear from him; had been wondering what he was doing; wished they had children to send for such a worthwhile experience (It must be wonderful country!); would be glad to keep the idea in mind and talk it up to anyone who seemed interested. Some gave advice: said he should come East to see prospects, should advertise in magazines, should go to Cape Cod to start such a thing, should get capital and start on a scale that

DUDE RANCHER 

would inspire confidence. A good many facetiously applied for jobs on the staff; a few not so facetiously. He had some offers of life insurance at the lowest rates. No matter how silly the letters were, Phil made a point of sitting up to answer every one and so began a practice which, he learned, was of the greatest importance. 

But among the replies to his circular were half a dozen that could be considered leads and one or two that seemed really promising. One woman whose party 

Phil had attended on the occasion of her daughter's debut, telegraphed that it sounded like just what she wanted, that she was bringing her son West on the train, anyway, at the end of the week, and would stop in at the ranch. Phil met her and the boy and his trunks at the station. She took one look at the old truck and declined Phil's invitation to come out to the ranch. "No, you just tell me about it, Mr. Marshall," she protested. "I'm sure it's simply divine. I won't go out with you, I mean, because I'm going to catch the train this afternoon for Cody." 

Phil told her about it, and she said "Mmm!" and "Ah" and "But I mean, really!" and was very enthusiastic. But he made the mistake of talking too well, so that she changed her reservation for Cody and went out to see the ranch, truck or no truck. She thought it was all fine, "so primitive, I mean; just wonderful for 

Harold." Though Pop and Phil urged her to spend the night among the shavings in the front room, she decided she had better do some shopping in Denver, I mean, if Phil wouldn't mind awfully driving her to town again in that adorable old truck, and didn't he

SPRING THAW 

137 think Harold would get settled better anyway, if 

Mummy were not there? 

So Phil made the second round trip to Denver that day and got back feeling beaten but successful. Harold had bloomed during his absence and was helping Pop wash the supper dishes. His eyes gleamed with excitement and a new freedom, and he begged for just one more outlaw story before he went to bed. 

The next morning a taxicab drew up beside the house, and the driver, cursing the road and swearing to collect for his springs, handed Phil a note. It was from 

Mummy. She had been thinking it over and hated to be a fussy old thing, I mean, but after a telephone conversation with Harold's father it had been decided that

Harold was just a little too young for such an experience, and his doctor would never think of letting him have milk that was not pasteurized. So, if it wouldn't be too awfully much trouble, would they please put Harold in the cab and send his trunk on later, collect, of course. "Best thing coulda happened," Pop said to console 

Phil. "She'd of been down here every week, I mean, from Cody, to see was we takin' Harold's temperature right. Tough on the boy, though." "Poor kid. I suppose she would have. Well, the circular and postage cost only eighteen dollars, I mean."

XV.  

PHIL handed Molly's letter to Pop and sat down to read his own mail. There was not much. Another application for a job as riding instructor from a youth who had heard of Phil's enterprise through a common friend. A letter from the mother of an eighteen-year-old who might have to be tutored but would not know until he learned the results of "College Boards." A second reply from the employment agency in Denver. "These people don't sound much better than the ones I saw the last time," Phil said. "I wish Molly would hurry up and get out here. She could pick the right cook for us. When is she coming?" "Ain't comin'," Pop replied. "Got a job in New 

York soon's she graduates." "But I-you mean she isn't coming out at all? All summer?" "I said she has a job, didn't I? You think maybe she's got nothin' better to do than traipse across America jest 'cause couple o' sourdoughs don't know how t' hire a cook?" "But, Pop, I can't think of this country in the summer without her. You'll miss her!" "What you know 'bout it? Nothin'; that's what! She writes me two three months back; says she's got a chance fer this here job, but wouldn't I rather she'd come out? 'Come out?' I says, 'jest when we got so's we c'n find things! We don't want no blamed women,' 138

I tells her.-Course I knowed she wanted the job, an' this ain't no place fer a girl's got her life ahead of her; go to seed, like." "But how about the ranch? How about Joe?" "Hm. Maybe Joe ain't the most dootiful son, always; but I guess he cares more fer his hide 'n to git her 'round the show he's startin' there now.-Hear he's gonna open up middle next month."

Phil tore May off the calendar and frantically watched the count on June. At last he swept Pop and the carpenter out of the new room with the chips and sawdust and got the beds and tables placed. The septic tank was in and the sewer pipe laid. But the bathroom fixtures were still in their crates, and the new faucets were the wrong style for the sink. And sheets and towels and pillowcases! What a thing to forget! Wonder what became of Mrs. Hinkle? Have to get 'em when I go for the groceries. See the employment people, too.

Phil was so harried by details that he lived in a sort of dream and did not actively worry any more about the thickening file of unpaid bills. But it must have been at the back of his mind, for he slept raggedly and grew thin. The morning of June eleventh he found himself at the station with the truck but had no memory of the drive to town. He had forgotten his shopping list and tried to reconstruct it while he waited for the boys' train.

When they had actually arrived, the tension snapped somehow, and the world began to make sense again. In the first place, the rush would be over now. Of course, things were not ready; but all you could do about that was plug along-probably good for the boys to take

DUDE RANCHER 

part and learn a few things. 

Second, Bobby Oakes handed Phil an envelope containing a letter and a check, the first proceeds from the new idea. The letter concluded, "And I hope this is all clear, so that you will not have to write. We're going to Alaska-I'll tell you where when I find out, but I'm tempted not to, just to let you do all the worrying for the summer. We'll come through in September and pick up both boys on our way home. The enclosed sordid item is intended to cover the cost for both boys for three months, including their tutoring. Some camps would have charged more, but you are only a beginner, with no social standing and questionable abilities. 

Howard says that you will have had to spend something on housing and so on and that we should at least lend you that amount and buy the boys' horses; but I told him you'd rather scratch for it yourself. I have collected Larry's half from his father, who will write you about his tutoring schedule."

The check was for $1400. Phil had been too busy even to estimate the probable revenue, but now conflicting emotions swept over him in waves. First elation, as he mentally balanced the check against those bills. 

Next irritation, the thought that Eleanor was unconsciously snubbing him by a gross overpayment. And then he did some hasty figuring: summer camp, $350 or more for a good one, that's $700; but these lads are here for three months instead of seven or eight weeks, makes it about $1100; tutoring? two courses, two boys, four hun- Why, she's gypped me! And so he felt happy and a little proud, and grateful to Eleanor for not taking advantage in the way he had feared.

Larry Baylor himself was the third boost to Phil's spirit. Bob Oakes, of course, he knew and liked already; but taking on for three months a boy you have never seen is like closing your eyes and biting into the first apple in the basket. It seemed to Phil that he had made a lucky bite. Larry was a year older than Bob, larger and a little more mature. He had a straight, confident eye that was both shrewd and friendly. Phil could see that Bob was trying desperately to hold the momentary advantage of being the old-timer. "How's the old ranch, Phil?" he said, when the suitcases and duffel bags had been thrown into the back of the truck and Larry had been jammed into the center of the seat. "Gosh, this truck isn't so good as the one we had last summer, is it?-Larry, he's never been out 

West before. Seen any more coyotes since I left? ... Have you got a good, gentle horse for Larry?" 

Larry was not greatly impressed. "Aw, quit showing off, Oakes," he said, and stabbed with his right elbow. 

Bob grunted, tried unsuccessfully to stab back, then snatched Larry's hat and held it out the window. "Stop it, you two," snapped Phil. "You almost made me bump that car. No, we haven't had time to get horses for any of us yet. We'll have to do that in a few days. Do you think we can find one wild enough for you, Bob?" "I don't believe you can, sir," said Larry.
