"He hasn't stopped bragging since we left Boston." "Aw, I have not!" Bob protested. "I just have to teach him, that's all. Honest, Phil, he can't tell the difference between brown and bay."

When they got to the ranch, the boys immediately

DUDE RANCHER 

began a scrambling exploration. "Hey, Bob, Larry," 

Phil called. "Go and take off those good suits. And remember, do your running at a walk for the first few days. You're 8000 feet up in the air, and you may land in the hospital if you don't take it easy until you get used to it."

He took Pop by the arm, led him into a corner and waved the check under his nose. Pop's face showed the same series of reactions that Phil had gone through, but Phil had to help him over the hurdle of irritation by explaining that it was not more than other places would have charged. "I'da took a mask 'n a gun to go after that much," Pop muttered and turned back to his work. 

Phil almost made the break of asking how many steers would be required to produce a similar amount; but he checked himself, counting on Pop's asking himself the same question in time. "Oh, Phil," Pop called. "Come help me set this sink in place. Hadda let in a big timber 'cross the studs to take the weight; brackets wasn't spaced right fer the studs anyhow- "I been thinkin' while you was down to Denver. You figgerin' to make it again today about that cook?" "I thought I'd have to, Pop." "Well, jest wait a minute. Why do we fool 'round with these here men cooks you got lined up 't the agency? Mostly bums anyhow. I know a old girl 't Mineral; Minnie Brush, name is; her old man Hank got squashed in a cave-in years ago, 'fore you was born. She run a boardin' house after that, 'til the bottom dropped out o' minin'. Been takin' in wash since then, I b'lieve.

Regular Apache, she is; scare you to death. But she sure knows how to tease up a hunk o' old bull so it eats like prime yearlin'. Be good fer the boys, too, havin' a woman around.” "She sounds fine," said Phil. "Let's all drive up to

Mineral this afternoon and see her. It will keep the boys quieter, and we've simply got to get someone here right now. I've lived through your cooking, but I must be copper-lined." It appeared that Minnie Brush was not available. She would thank Mr. Wakefield to remember that he and she were not on terms of sufficient intimacy to justify his use of her Christian name. Particularly before strangers. She supposed that he and this young man could appreciate that she had her responsibilities to her clients. She could hardly be expected to bury herself in the wilderness with two bachelors whose principles of right and wrong might be open to question but of whose habits of domestic neatness she felt there could be no uncertainty. Her geraniums and morning glories had grown accustomed to depend on her for protection against pests and drought. 

What! The young man meant to stand there and say that these two little boys were doomed to live in a place like that all summer? With no mothers? Wicked, wicked! Well, in that case there was only one thing a body could do. The morning glories would have to be abandoned, and the geraniums must undergo the hazards of transplanting. . . . Why, yes, it would be very nice if Mr. Marshall would drive her around to see her customers and arrange with Mrs. Plunkett to take over her responsibilities, not that the customers were any too

DUDE RANCHER 

apt to be satisfied with the change! In fact, if Mr. Wakefield and Mr. Marshall could find something to do with their exceedingly valuable time until evening, she would go right back with them rather than endure the thought of having those boys spend one single night in an atmosphere so dangerous to their health and morals. 

That night Mrs. Brush was placed "temporarily” in the new room, while Pop and Phil bunked in the end of the shop which had been partitioned off for the cook. 

They did not sleep in the house again until September. 

While they prepared for bed that night, they talked over the horse problem. Things were sufficiently in hand now so that the hunt could begin any time. Pop was convinced that none of his neighbors would risk the loss of face involved in selling him a horse at its real value and that it would be better to try the auction rings in Denver or Greeley. "But are places like that reliable?" Phil asked. "Sure they're reliable-like a coyote in a hen-yard. 

That's why you c'n buy cheaper there. Nobody trusts 'em. If you're stone deef, like I am, 'n got both eyes open, you may not do s' bad, always.” 

Next morning breakfast was delayed a little while

Bob and Larry were being convinced that the soap was not corrosive and positively would not remove the flesh from their necks (though they lost their case, there was some evidence on their side when it was all over); while Pop and Phil adjusted themselves to the new rule that stove-wood, in a decent household, is not dumped on the floor but piled in a box beside the stove; while 

Mr. Quigley, the plumber, went back to shave. But, when at last everyone sat down to berries and cream,

followed by bacon fried just right, and fried eggs, and fine brown pancakes, and the best coffee he had had in months, Phil was sure that he and Pop had done the right thing.

It was Pop's idea that this would be as good a day as any to look at horses, and, perhaps, if they could get to town in time, to do the actual buying; for, he explained, this was the regular day for the sale at one of the big rings. Everyone agreed enthusiastically, except Mrs. 

Brush. She announced that there would be no gallivanting anywhere until all hands had fallen to at neating up the yard, which resembled nothing so much as the day before creation. Phil silenced Pop with a glare across the table, explained that there might be no more large auctions for a week, and promised that the next day would bring perfect order in the yard.

And so they got to the sales yard in time for an hour's inspection before the auction should begin. It was not too much, for all the small board enclosures in the low stone building and several pens in the lot outside were full.

There was a crowd already, ranchers, truck farmers, cowboys, and dealers; going in and out of the pens with ostentatious indifference; pausing here to pull apart a horse's lips for a look at his teeth, there to feel the swelling on a knee and decide whether it were something chronic or the result of a bump in shipping. Swaggering employees of the trading company, with big black sombreros and fancy boots, sat on the top rails, twisting their long whips and sizing up the attendance in a lopsided drawl: "Just seen ol' Hoke Smith down from Greeley."

DUDE RANCHER 

"Yeh. Musta got wind o' them Belgians from over 

Rifle. Won't do him no good, though." "No?" "Naw. The old man's got a side deal on with Gallagher. Belgians won't even be put up, prob'ly, 'less

Hoke or the owner hollers. If they do, I shag 'em in the ring 'n Ham knocks 'em down ta Gallagher's first bid 'fore Hoke c'n say yes, no, or maybe. They won't look so good in the ring as what they do now. You watch.”

Apparently Pop's boasted deafness could be put on or off, for he seemed to catch well enough the occasional greeting from some old-timer. "Why-why, if it ain't 

Paul Wakefield! Thought you was dead years ago. Ya oughta been, ya ol' rattlesnake. . . . Hey, Gord, look what's blowed in. Tell the boys t' hide their horses quick. What ya figgerin' to steal today, Paul?" "Not a thing, Bruce. I give up tradin' last time you stung me. Jest thought these fellers 'd like to see the sights. Well, I hope they skin you, Bruce. Hi, Gord; thought you was in jail."

Pop pushed the staring Bob and Larry ahead and took Phil by the arm. "If you see anything you like, don't let them fellers see you lookin' at it. They'll bid it up jest to plague me 'n maybe git a little cut. Now use your eyes fast. Most this stuff's jest scrub work stock in off the range. Er else unbroke, half-starved mustangs. 

No good. Hafta see it all sold, though, 'cause you can't never tell what's comin' up next.-Here, here's some better stuff. What do you see?"

Phil climbed over the boards behind Pop. “Well, they're all pretty fair, Pop. That little black with the saddle scald-looks as if she might be old, though."

"Yep, she's all right. Been rode plenty. Gentle, too, see? An' look, she's got a good mouth. Age ain't ahurtin' her none. Mark her down; number 187. An' get this buckskin too, 184. Prob'ly from the same place as the little mare. Be good company fer each other. Both real horses but trim, right size fer the boys." 

Then Phil saw the bay filly.

She came from behind an enormous workhorse that had hidden her from view, and something turned over inside Phil as she walked confidently up to sniff his fingers. Except for the workhorse, she was taller by a full hand than anything else in the corral. Her sure but springy walk as she advanced on clean, long legs, told of centuries of breeding that had not given way to the one-eighth or one-sixteenth dilution of cold blood. Phil gloried in the chiseled bone of her head; the wide-set eyes; the broad jawbones between which he rested his fist with ease; the nervous, quivering nostrils, showing bright red on the inside. Beginning under her silky forelock, a narrow strip ran down her nose, over her muzzle, and ended in a flesh-mark on the upper lip. "Hey, Pop, come over here quick," Phil called, as he ran his hand ecstatically down the smooth muscle of her long neck and onto the deep, sloping shoulder. “Look, Pop, look! Did you ever see such a bay in your life? 

She makes these other bays old khaki. And she's got withers would hold a saddle. Look at that loin; three fingers from her last rib to her hip!" 

Pop laughed kindly. "She's awful pretty, Phil, but what's she good for? She's too big, fer one thing; fall all over herself; and with a middle like that, 'n them long legs 'n silly little feet she's got, she'd never stand

DUDE RANCHER 

up in this country. I thought you got over your thoroughbred idees. Come over an' look at this little paint 

Larry's got his eye on."

Phil's head was over his shoulder as he went to look at the "paint," and he bumped into the belly of a sour old gelding, who grunted and stepped aside with an air of annoyance. Though he should have foreseen Pop's attitude toward the filly, he was a bit hurt, and it delighted him to point out that the "paint" had a rather obvious ringbone. "So he has, fer a fact," Pop chortled, and winked at 

Larry. "I shoulda seed that, now, shouldn't I? Must be 

I'm gittin' old."

The four of them had almost made the rounds of the pens and had agreed on a few more that should be noted, when a bell rang at the front of the building. 

From all sides men and boys began to push toward the sales ring, and soon Pop and Phil, with the boys between them, were seated high in the tier of benches that fronted the little arena.

Into the auctioneer's stand on the opposite side climbed "Col." Hambleton Bliss; beside him perched the clerk of the auction and an assistant to identify the successful bidders. Into the arena itself strode the proprietor of the sales company, Mr. A. Wallace Cherington, bearing a slim black wand. He was clad in a tight, fawn-colored gabardine that showed off his square shoulders and tapering waist. On his head was a little gray Stetson, with the brim turned down over one fishy blue eye. And on his feet were intricately worked cowboy boots, whose long heels added to his extravagant height. He was followed by three or four squat flunkeys,

wearing overalls and carrying long drovers' whips.

Even before the sale began, Colonel Bliss looked apoplectic. He peeled the coat off his huge arms, and the sleeves turned inside out. He undid the top button of his shirt, and the collar flew apart, displaying a continuation of his barrel chest where his neck should have been. He put two pudgy thumbs inside his suspenders and fixed his bulging eyes on the skylight. When some inner sense told him that the hall was full, he reached for his gavel without looking and pounded for silence. 

The lower part of his face shifted over under his left ear; and when his voice burst through the ragged hole that appeared there, Phil stopped worrying about the danger of apoplexy, for with no effort the deep snarl filled the hall. "Laaadeeeees an' gemmun. Wegottalotta horses to sell here today; so please stop talkin' an' make your biddin' snappy. All animals sold as is; no guarantee unless the seller is here an' wants to stand back of 'em. 

Poztively every animal will be sold to the last bidder, regardless o' the amount o' the bid. Terms strickly cash. 

Call for your purchases an' take 'em within three hours o' the close o' the sale. Six bits a day will be charged for the keep of any left after that time. "All right, Tony, shoot 'em in, shoot 'em in. We ain't got all day."

XVI. HORSE AUCTION 

AT one end of the arena was a pair of solid timber gates, higher than a man's head. Now they swung in and admitted a youth leading by a rope halter one of the most bewildered bits of horseflesh that Phil had ever seen. Behind came five or six more men, evidently professional traders, who took positions against the wall in easy defiance of the large painted sign: "All persons will keep out of sales ring, this means you." The horse was a young washy bay, coarse, rather woolly, and barely broken to lead. He rolled his eyes wildly and would have pulled back, but the sharp crack of a whip on his quarters made him rear and lunge forward, giving the semblance of animation that was desired. His start carried him and the dangling youth to the other end of the arena. Here another whip, exploding in his face, swung him around and sent him back. The boy stopped him in front of the high stand, trembling in terror of the next slash. But Colonel Bliss now began the sale. “All right, men, here's the furst thing we got; nice big colt, nice 'n gentle, good size, make a good harvest horse fer some o' you fellas this fall. Three-year-old, ain't he, Tony?-Yep, three-year-old. What's that? 

What's that, Mister? Wire cut on the off fore? Take a look, will you, Wally. That's right; just like I could see from here: blemish, that's all. Don't hurt him a speck; sound as a noo penny. What's that?-Nope. Just halterbroke, like you can see. 150

HORSE AUCTION 

151 "All right, men, what am I bid? Start it off, somebody; start it off. Wegottalotta horses t' sell t' day. Well, say sump'n'!" "Ten bucks," growled the proprietor of the company. "All right, men, Wally starts it off at ten bucks. 

Funny guy, ain't he? All right, ten I'm bid. Who'll make it twenty? Ten-ten-ten-ten-ten-ten-twenty-twentytwenty-twenty. Who'll make it fifteen?" The Colonel droned on and on and finally knocked the colt down for $26 to an anxious young farmer.-"All right, Tony, shoot in the next. Oh, boy! Now here's a real horse."

For more than an hour Larry and Bob sat hynotized by the Colonel's song, but at last Larry drew a deep sigh and turned to Phil. "Gosh," he breathed. "How can he ever talk so fast? I can't even think the words that fast, but I can understand everything he says. Is he a good auctioneer, Phil?” "He seems so to me," Phil answered. "He's a worker, anyway. But I don't see how the company can spend so much of his time over fifty cents or a dollar." “All right, Phil," Pop spoke behind his hand. “You jest get your watch on this one. See how long it takes."

It took just under a minute, and Phil admired the Colonel even more. Then he turned his attention to the audience, to see where the bids were coming from. 

He saw no sign. Even when he followed the Colonel's nods, he could not tell whose bid was being recognized. 

After a while he turned to Pop. "How do you buy a horse in this place, Pop? I haven't spotted half a dozen bids all morning.' 

"Well, you better watch out er you might own a

DUDE RANCHER 

horse without knowin' it. All you do is bend one finger, er screw up your nose, er wink an eye. That's too easy seed, though. You ain't supposed to let no one know you're biddin'. You see, if you know horses, other fellers that don't 'll watch what you're abiddin' on 'n take it away from you, er anyhow run it up to twice what you could've got it for. An' if you look like a greenhorn, somebody'll take the chance o' runnin' it up anyhow, prob'ly the owner. I've seed plenty fellers buy a horse when all they thought they was doin' was knock a fly off their nose. Course, they don't have to take him if they speak up then; but a feller looks kinda foolish, lettin' ever'body see he slipped up on his signals." "When are you going to bid, Pop?" asked Bob. "I been abiddin' some a'ready. Here, I'll put my hand on your knee. Next time I bid, I'll squeeze, 'n then you'll know. They's lots o' horses here today, 'n they'll go cheaper by 'n by. Glad them two we like special ain't showed up yet."

On the way to the sale Phil had half intended to buy himself a horse. But with such a welter in the pens outside, he had not succeeded in focusing on anything suitable. And, of course, he ought to have Pop's advice. 

Still, it would be more fun, risky as it was, to choose on his own responsibility, if something good should appear. In one pocket he had a few dollars in small change; in another was $75 in crisp bills. The letter from his father enclosing the check had explained that it was a delayed birthday present, that it had nothing to do with current difficulties in meeting bills, that every rancher ought to have his own horse. So far, Phil had seen nothing he liked much, and now, weighing

HORSE AUCTION 

153 the risks, he decided not to buy at all. Next time, he'd use his eyes better in the pens.

The boys were fidgeting with the monotony of the proceedings, and Phil had begun to regard the offerings so listlessly that he doubted whether he would recognize a sensational horse if it came into the ring. But apparently the management knew that others would be feeling the same way, for a fifteen minute recess was called, and the benches promptly emptied. 

Outside, in the alley leading to the arena, among the bunch that would be sent in next, Phil saw a sharp pair of ears sticking up above the heads of the other horses, and again he felt a twinge inside him. I wonder, oh, I wonder, and he squeezed the roll of bills in his pocket. 

But then he shook his head: remember what Pop saidand anyway she'll sell for a thousand.

He sadly turned his back and walked away, stopped, and turned again. “Larry,” he said, “you stay out here with Pop and Bob. I'm going down that alley for a minute." He crowded among the bodies of horses, paying no attention to the looks of the handlers, made his way to the filly, and again ran his hand down from the withers, along the polished back to the beautiful straight croup. 

A moment later he was excitedly talking to the auctioneer, who was standing among a group of cronies at the office window. "Look, Colonel, if I should bid on a horse, see? I want to keep it quiet. Can you just recognize my bid without my being identified? See, I have some money here. Philip Marshall's my name.”

The Colonel looked at Phil and winked understandingly. "O.K., son," he drawled, "I getcha. I won't say

a thing. Take it down, Jerry: Marshall. And take a look at him, Jerry, so 's you'll remember."

When the bell rang, Phil, breathing fast, took Larry by the arm and held him back until they were hopelessly separated from Pop and Bob and the seats were nearly full. Then he and Larry hurried to places high up at the near end.

The Colonel mounted to his pulpit, delivered a sad little speech on the grief he suffered in having to deal with the brand of thieves who had been stealing horses from under his nose all morning, and called on Tony to shoot 'em in. The handlers pushed the next bunch along the alley to the gate; and from where he sat Phil had a good view of the filly, dilating her nostrils and showing the whites of her eyes in alarm at the crowding. 

The new bunch included eight or ten better than average saddle horses, and the crowd had come to life a little after the rest; so that the brisker bidding drove the prices up, and the sales took somewhat longer. 

When the filly was brought in, sixth of her lot, Phil's muscles stiffened in spite of himself and he turned to 

Larry. "Isn't she wonderful, Larry!" he gloated. Then the whip cracked at the filly's flank. She snorted, plunged high in the air, throwing the lean youth against the wall; and, before anyone knew what was happening, she had galloped the length of the arena, cleared the five-foot exit gate at the other end and was fleeing down the alley to the "sold" pens. There were a few whistles of admiration from the crowd, but mostly laughter and shouts.

Mechanically Phil did as he was told; but he was late leaving town. A tire was flat when he got to the truck, and then the spare blew out through a big patch, and he had to buy a new tube and casing. When he reached the turn-in, he could tell from the tracks where a truck had backed up to the bank, that Pop had unloaded and gone on to the ranch. Phil turned in, interested now to see how the little horses would look in homelike surroundings.

XVII. TAKING CHANCES 

AND now it was time to gather all the loose ends, little things that must be finished before the plant could run smoothly. The pipe had been laid out on the ground from the spring to the house but not connected; a concrete gathering tank had to be built at the spring. Catches were needed on all the new windows. Mrs. Brush issued an ultimatum about fly screens. Cans of paint and floor oil were piled in the corner. And Phil had to make exasperating trips to town, sometimes just to get a box of screws that had not been foreseen, or to buy another one and one-half inch union so that the pipe could be connected to the lead left by the plumber. In all the work of finishing the improvements, the boys were eager to take part, and before long they were really useful with hammers and wrenches and shovels. But there were occasions when they were only in the way, or when their interest flagged. Moreover, the rush of last-minute details and the forced irregularity of his schedule made it impossible for Phil to get the school work started. 

All these things brought him squarely against a problem that brings wretched days and nights to any dude rancher who is given to worrying. 

To Larry and Bob, of course, life meant riding. For the first day or two Phil or Pop had to drop whatever he was doing, saddle one of the old mares, and go along to see that the boys handled their horses properly and 162

TAKING CHANCES 

163 kept out of trouble. But the buckskin, Ham, and the little black mare, Wally-named for the auctioneer and the proprietor of the sales ring-were as reliable and sensible as a horse can be, the boys showed reasonably sound judgment, and there were many occasions when it was difficult or impossible for Phil or Pop to get away. go "Hey, Phil," Larry asked the third morning, "can I down to the box and get the mail?" "You may, but it will have to be later. The stove is out of commission until Pop and I get these pipes attached to the hot water tank. I'll try to work it so that 

I can ride down with you before lunch.” "Aw, gee, I don't see why I can't do it alone. Don't you think I can ride well enough for that?" "Surely, Larry, but that's not the question. Probably nothing would happen, and if it did, it could happen just as well when I was along. But, if there were any trouble and no grown-up was with you, you see, your parents might never forgive us.”

The next day it was three heifers that had appeared outside the corral. "Oh, Larry, Bob," Phil called, "here's something that needs to be done right away, and you can help. Shoo those heifers back along the fence and through the gate. You know, the one straight up the creek a half a mile. Somebody must have left it open. If you should find I'm wrong when you get there, walk along the fence and find out where it's down. Try to prop up the wire somehow, and we'll go out and mend it this afternoon.-The walk'll do you good. And notice whether their salt block is gone. That may be what brought them down."

DUDE RANCHER 

"They'll never git 'em in afoot," Pop observed. "Them heifers is full o' grass 'n high spirits. They're out t' see the world, 'n they'll run rings around the kids."

Sure enough, the boys were back in fifteen minutes, blowing and angry, red in the face from running. “I want to borrow your gun, Pop," Bob exploded. "Every time we get them started along the fence, they cut around us and head back. I can't run any more. I bet 

Wally'd show 'em. And there are two more that didn't come down here-they're in the oats."

Phil started to rise and go to the trouble but stopped himself and muttered, "What do you think, Pop?" 

Pop smiled a little and nodded. "All right, boys," Phil said. "They've got to be put back in pasture, and we're going to let you try it. Go and saddle Wally and Ham. Bring them down here to the house first and let me see how you've done it. 

You've driven cattle enough with me, Bob, to know what you're supposed to do. Try to keep out of the oats. When you are through, come back here and let me see you both alive."

After they had passed inspection, the boys were off, whooping with delight, and as Phil watched them from a window, he noticed that the drive was being conducted rather at the speed of the movies than the range. "What about it, Pop?" he asked when the boys were out of sight. "They'll learn more and develop twice as fast if they're trusted to do jobs like that alone. But do we dare?" "You gotta dare, Phil. Them kids has to grow up.

They're here to git the experience all ranch kids has

TAKING CHANCES 

165 from the time they're that high-stuff kids miss back 

East. Fer one thing, that means bein' on their own with a horse. You can't be babyin' 'em all the time 'n expect 'em to git what they're here for." "No-o-o, but is it safe, do you think? Where would we stand if a boy broke his neck and we weren't there?" "Safe? Course it ain't safe. No more 'n climbin' trees or playin' baseball, or ridin' in a automobile, or crossin' the street-or anythin' else kids is doin' every day. If you live with horses all your life, I s'pose you'll maybe git kilt by a horse some day; jest like if you swim er drive a automobile er work on the railroad er in a factory. But in all them things it ain't most generally the greenhorn gits in trouble but th' expert, the feller that thinks he knows it all 'n tries t' make a horse er a machine er his own carcass do somethin' it can't do. You gotta take chances, Phil, no matter what you're up to. "You take ol' Jim Hanning, died up to Mineral couple years back. When he's a young feller, he's workin' down in one o' the mines, an' it just suits him. Only they was two bad cave-ins once in two days. 'Most got him both times 'n scared him t' death. "He's through, he says; too dangerous. So he comes up, gits him a job carryin' the mail-letter carrier jest been shot accidental in a bank holdup- Well, sir, he's been totin' letters 'round three, four days, just gittin' so 's he knows the customers' names, 'n he's walkin' 'long the north side of a big two-story house. Warm day it is, winter, 'n 'bout two tons o' snow lets go on the roof, slides off the edge, 'n mashes him into the sidewalk. That'd been all right, maybe, only the crash 'n noise starts up a team tied right there, 'n they rare up

DUDE RANCHER 

on the walk where Jim is 'n fetch the ore wagon 'crost his belly. Somebody hollers down t' the hospital, 'n a couple fellers comes arunnin' up with one o' these here stretchers, sweeps Jim onto it, 'n starts down hill t' the hospital. "Well, sir, it's slick on the hill, mud 'n slush; 'n the front feller slips to his knees. Nacherly pours Jim out the stretcher, 'n he starts rollin'-steep bank 'longside the path. So he goes bouncin' down the hillside, scorin' billiards off the rocks, 'n fetches up in the crik 't the bottom. Ain't much ice there, only 'bout two, three inches; so he goes through 'n don't show up 'til he comes out t' open water in the rapids 'n snags hisself on a log. "When the boys gits him t' land agin, he's belchin' mine tailins s' fast he can't even cuss. But he spends a year in the hospital while they're atinkerin' him up, 'n when he gits out, he's right back 't the mine. Couldn't hardly git him t' come out nights. Never had no more cave-ins, neither, all the years he's down there. But after he gits him a pile saved up, so's he don't have to work no more, he rents him a house with a deep cellar, takes a miner's lamp 'n a chair 'n a bed down there; and that's where we'd always go whenever we wanted him to play poker." "You mean he lived and died down there in that cellar?" "Oh, no, he never died down there. And so the boys were allowed to ride out by themselves, on condition that they always go together and announce where they were going. Phil didn't worry acutely, but he always carried a mental image of a saddled and riderless horse that might someday limp up to the corral.

Life settled down into a routine at last. The boys worked with Phil on their studies for three or four hours every morning except Saturday and Sunday. On these days they took long rides or worked in the woods or the hay with Pop and Phil. Weekday afternoons they

DUDE RANCHER 

often spent with Pop at whatever he was doing, while Phil began the education of the filly. Pandora he named her and called her Pan for short.

The morning after her arrival at the ranch, he had taken time off to lead her around a bit and had cautiously mounted her bareback. She made no violent objection, only stiffened and trembled, so that he was sure she had never been ridden. Of this he was glad, for it meant his first chance to begin a horse's training. 

Phil's English saddle had arrived from home, and almost every day he rode Pandora out at a walk, with the boys on Ham and Wally. Often he jogged her easily along the edge of one of the plowed fields, so that she might learn to balance the unfamiliar weight and accommodate her motion to it. To save her mouth, he always used a snaffle bit.

He had a real scare one day when he made the mistake of letting her canter too early. She became excited and broke into an extended run which was more headlong than anything he had ever known. After a mile she was satisfied and slowed down without hurting herself or Phil, but he realized that the first lessons at a canter would better be given in the corral, where she could not get started and would have to hold her gait down to make the turns. This worked, and before long 

Phil could canter her around the fence on a loose rein. 

Pop and all the neighbors were amused or contemptuous about Phil's flat saddle, which they described as a pancake or a postage-stamp. "But it's more comfortable for just riding around," Phil would say. “Let me put it on your horse and you try it." "No, thanks," was the invariable reply, "I want some-

TAKING CHANCES 

169 thin' I c'n set my knees into. I'd sooner go bareback than onto that slick little thing." 

Phil could see the virtue of the stock-saddle with its horn and high cantle, for round-up work or bucking; but he had none of that to do. And it seemed pointless to add twenty-five pounds to the weight on Pan's young back.

He had made an early resolve never to let anyone else ride her until she was thoroughly trained. But the thrill of her power and reach was so great, so different from anything he could get from one of Joe's horses, for instance, that he had to communicate it to someone and persuaded Pop to try her, English saddle and all. 

Pop walked her out for a few paces, then, to Phil's horror, broke her into a good, round gallop. But as he watched, he felt reassured, for she did not stretch beyond the gallop. Pop swung her sharp around at last, galloped back, and slid her to a stop on her haunches in front of Phil. It was an exhibition of horsemanship that aroused both admiration and dismay. Phil hated to think of the strain on her immature tendons, but he marveled that Pop had been able to manage her so easily at speed and with a snaffle bit. "Fair sort o' pony," Pop panted, and Phil could tell from the gleam in his eye that he meant a good deal more. “I mean fer smooth, open country, where she don't have to know nothin' er shift her footin' fast. 

Strong, too. I bet she could sure skedaddle if a feller'd let her." "But how did you hold her down so easily and bring her around?" Phil asked. "She'd bolt with me if I ever let her out like that."

DUDE RANCHER 

"Don't b'lieve she would. She stopped real nice, I thought, fer a green one. Didn't pull a mite."

And Phil realized that he still had something to learn about "hands." "How'd you like the saddle?" Phil's eyes were laughing. "Saddle's all right, Phil; you shoulda told me. Course it'd be no good fer cow work, but I like it fine." This was the biggest reversal of form so far, and Phil treasured the admission as a hopeful sign.

The school work gave no particular difficulties. Both boys were bright enough, needing only a great deal of drill and some prodding to pay attention, so that Phil was confident in looking ahead to the fall examinations.

Late in July he heard from the mother whose son had been in doubt about the College Board examinations. He had passed and was accepted, but his mark in French was so low that he wanted a month's review before continuing the subject and would arrive the first of August. This upset Phil's plans for the exploring he had promised himself, but it meant something in the treasury and might be an indication that the idea was going to catch hold. 

The boy, Art Sherwood, a cousin of one of Phil's classmates, came from Baltimore. He was large and plumply handsome, evidently felt that he had "been around," and was prepared to look down his nose a little at the small outfit and simple arrangements. But when he realized that here was no rigid aristocracy of teacher and student such as he had known in preparatory school, no attitude of Western superiority toward

TAKING CHANCES 

171 the life he had known, no question of his fitness for the new life, he fell into the spirit of the place and enjoyed everything that went on. "Good training for football," he muttered to excuse to himself his participation in actual work. And his complete ignorance of riding helped bring him down to a feeling of friendly equality with the other boys and of boyish respect for Pop and Phil. For his use Pop rented an indestructible old mustang from Sandburg, giving a load of poles in return.

It seemed to Phil that the summer passed even more rapidly than the one before. All the boys were tanned and strong and active, and Phil knew that they could plan for themselves and carry out things they never would have attempted two months before. Pop was usually in a good mood and had taken a much larger part in the group life than Phil had dared to hope. 

The only thing that seemed always to make him bitter was the loss and misuse of tools. It had the same effect on Phil, too, but he had not yet figured a way around this, if the boys were to do the things he was sure they should be doing. Mrs. Brush's rhetoric had not softened much; but everyone could see that it was part of her stern good will. Both Phil and Pop realized that she had held them up to standards that would have been neglected in a purely male community. 

And then, the first of September, after Art Sherwood's month was over and he had gone back for a few weeks at Northeast Harbor before the opening of college, Phil received the telegram that he had been expecting:

Where'd we put 'em?" "They don't want luxury, Pop-they're not like Harold's mother. Eleanor means what she says about the tent. But we wouldn't have to do that: we could put another cot in the room we cut out of the shop for Art, and let them sleep there." 

Knowing something of Howard Oakes's work, Phil was hardly prepared for the slim, athletic figure that got out of the car and greeted him so simply. The only things that contrasted with the boyishness of his manner and looks and carriage were the searching hazel eyes and the wavy shock of gray hair. Harvard and New 

England were stamped all over him, but he was immediately at home with Pop and all the livestock, and particularly with Mrs. Brush. "You have done a splendid job with the boy, Marshall, both boys," he said, the first time he had a chance to talk with Phil alone. "I don't mean the studies so much, though I'm sure they're all right. I mean the

TAKING CHANCES 

173 self-reliance and the consciousness of other people that Bob shows for the first time. And, eh, I owe you for everything you can think of. When do we eat?”

During the Oakeses' visit Phil arranged one drive in the car up over the pass at 11,000 feet, through some of the big hay country, and back over another pass that was even higher. And after that he unhorsed both boys for two days and took Howard and Eleanor on an overnight ride into some of the great timber and grazing land forever beyond the reach of motor cars. He was careful to keep them on trails that he was sure he could unwind. Both were sensitive to the vastness and the silence and such smaller details as the tiny alpine flowers and the late columbine blooming near timberline. But, in the evening, back at the ranch, Howard 

Oakes spoke the thought that had been running through Phil's mind during the days of wandering. "It's all extravagantly beautiful," he said, “and I would not have missed those two trips for anything.

The dance of that glacial torrent bursting out of the little lake, plunging down over the shiny black rocks between those banks of green, and back of it that curtain of giant peaks and incredible sky: it's not a thing you'd soon forget. But really, you know, I'm sure this country right around you, with the timber and the cattle and the little roads and trails, would somehow get hold of you more than the other. It's a bit hard to say, but I probably feel that here it is even more beautiful and significant because man can find a place in the picture. Up there it's too hard on the ego." "That's right," Pop observed. "Nothin' like campin' to cut a feller down to size. It sure is elegant, like you

say; but 'bout the only kind c'n stand it week in week out is Mexican sheepherders. An' they're mostly daffy, I b'lieve." "Well, Phil," Eleanor said, "how do you like it? DUDE RANCHER 

"Wait a minute, everybody," Howard broke in. "That's always the trouble when a gang of dreamers try to think business: they assume that a lot of cash will turn the whole trick. It won't. More is needed than cash and optimism. From what you tell me, Phil, I imagine many of those dude ranches you saw were over-capitalized, that is, they had put in more than they could ever hope to pay interest on, and, spending it in big chunks in advance, had not used it where it was most needed." "That's right," Pop growled. "Look at Joe. First he sinks hisself 'n me too, almost, gettin' started on a thing he ain't fit fer 'n don't understand. An' now, now he's in deeper yet. Doin' a big business, I guess; but he's had to incorporate the plant 'n the ten acres it stands on, cut it loose from the rest o' the ranch (the which he's still got a big mortgage agin, not countin' a little matter between him an' me); an' to do it all, he's had to sign hisself up with them fellers 't Mineral 'n git them to put in their cash. He'll never pay out on it all." "That's a good example of what I mean, Phil,” Howard continued. “On a microscopic scale you had a dude ranch right here this summer. You had no capital, you say, and yet you stayed alive and fed and housed your customers. You haven't any cash to show for it; but all these improvements are worth twice what they cost you, because you did most of the work yourselves. And the difference between what they're worth and what you still owe on them represents your profit, that and 

Wally and Ham, plus whatever following you may have begun to build up. Are you much in debt for materials?"

TAKING CHANCES 

179 "No, not so much. Horses and food were cheaper than I thought they'd be, and we were able to apply all of Art Sherwood's ante on the bills." "All right. You see, you're ahead in experience and equipment and reputation, and you haven't let anyone else in for his cash. More important than that, if 

I judge you right, is that you know that you and Pop have done it all yourselves. "Now look at it the other way: suppose someone hands you $20,000 in a big lump. He wouldn't be doing it because he thought it was a sound investment, certainly; and you could never have the feeling that you have now. "No, Phil, here or somewhere else, the sensible thing for you to do is to go slowly, build only when you have to build, make it yourself; and then you'll own whatever you make. You'll have to go into debt at times, of course, because you can't expect one season's net to pay for much enlargement. But if you plan conservatively and don't lose your head, you'll keep your business your own. "Another thing: any business may fail through outside causes. Yours, being a luxury, is more dependent than some on the general prosperity and on the unreliable taste in luxuries. With a small plant that you'd built yourself as your growth demanded it, one that was paid for, you could get along somehow on your ranch until things came back. Then you could go ahead again. But with a big capitalization, calling for interest, you'd be out, with no plant, no job, and a big load of debt. "Later, say in ten years, if you've built soundly, so

DUDE RANCHER 

that people can see you know what you're doing, you can get all the backing you want for any sort of expansion you want to undertake. "I do think you're right, though, about not going to work on a big dude ranch. I wouldn't do it. If I were you, I'd go in with someone who knows ranching from the ground up. And I'd start in for myself before very long. That is counsel I could not give you if you were thinking of the steel business or newspaper work, or almost any of the things young men can start at nowadays." "Come on, Shylock," said Eleanor. "These farmers want to go to bed. Good night, Phil. Good night, Pop-

XVIII. PROMOTION PARTY 

"BUT I do like it, Bob," she repeated. "Naturally

I had to get used to the life, and I'm not fond of living in town, and, well, I—but right from the beginning I've enjoyed the work." She took off her hat, put it on the little suitcase, and pulled the center table a foot or so nearer the window.

Bob Saltell followed her with his eyes and almost got up when she took hold of the table, but settled back into the big easy chair as he saw it stop in position. Molly enjoyed the afternoon and gained confidence as it wore on. She found herself chatting easily and familiarly about the ranch, and she felt pleased when people assumed that she was connected with running it.
