DUDE RANCHER 

French place Phil remembered on Fiftieth Street. One thing they decided was that Molly would enjoy seeing the Whites again almost as much as Phil would; and so the visit to Passaic was put off until the following evening, when Molly would be free. This meant, of course, that Phil did not start home the next night; and, as the day after that was Saturday and Molly had no work at the office, it seemed a shame not to go further into the details about Grandfather and the ranch. 

And so Phil at last got the train Sunday night, for

Molly did have to be at work on Monday. He stayed on the train as far as Trenton, which was the first stop. 

From there he sent his bag on by express and started home the same way he had come East, hitch-hiking. The trip had been necessary, he felt, in the interest of the ranch; but funds were low, and train fare would have been an extravagance, added to all the expense of telegrams and long-distance calls and rushing around the 

East and that swell party Molly had engineered in New

York. It cost a little to get to Trenton; but it was simpler to catch rides once you were across New Jersey, and there was no sense in advertising to Molly how broke you were.

XIX.  

IT seemed to Phil that his trip East had been a success, would have to be repeated fairly often. But, back at the ranch, he began a season of worry. It was all very well to say, "Go slow. Build as you need. Don't expand too fast." He valued Howard Oakes's advice and tried to follow it. But he was planning on a larger group this summer, had to. You can't run an outfit for three customers. You have to build. Yes, but how much? What with? If we wait to see how many we're going to have, we'll never be ready for them in time. Have to turn 'em away! But suppose we build and no one comes? 

We'll be sunk. Absolutely. Not so bad for me: I'm young. But pretty bad for Pop, with everything he owns tied up. Oh, why don't they tell me ahead, whether they're coming or not?

Mornings, at the sawmill working on the logs Pop had skidded down, or afternoons, plowing and harrowing and seeding, he forgot administrative problems and enjoyed whatever he was doing. And Pop never seemed to worry. He took it for granted that Phil could see things through. He even urged him to go faster and farther, offered to sign a mortgage on his 480, suggested leasing the ranch land Joe wasn't using and stocking it with Herefords. "Might's well use the grass," he said, "now they's two of us t' see to things. We c'n make us a nice profit just growin' 'em out 'til fall. Sell 'em then, 'n we won't have 197

DUDE RANCHER 

to think o' hay fer winter. Joe's got nothin' in there, much, since he cut down his string o' ponies. It'll be back to brush 'n briers if it ain't grazed off. An' if we git yearlin's now, 'fore the spring grass comes, we c'n buy 'em cheap, carry 'em a month er so on that old hay we got, then turn 'em out in Joe's grass 'n fegit 'em, almost."

Phil liked the idea, and so Pop went over and made the arrangements with Joe, secured the use of the land at a low figure because, if he couldn't sell it, Joe was delighted to make his taxes. Then Pop bought the yearlings in the Denver yards. He had to pay more than he had expected; for it took a few days to get the loan through, and, meantime, there came a warm spell which encouraged all the cattlemen to hold their stock for the new grass. Prices went up everywhere. Still, the yearlings were a good buy: growing animals were sure to put on weight during the summer. For the next month there were forty new voices bawling around the corral.

Getting horses for the crowd that would be coming in July was not so simple; for even dude horses are individuals to a greater extent than yearling cattle. In the latter you want uniformity; while your horses have to be chosen to take account of the wide variety in human size and shape, the still greater difference in experience and ability. They must be safe to the point of soddenness for the beginners; safe but a little frisky for veterans who would be insulted if you gave them a "plug."

Pop grasped this and was for going to the auction ring every sale day until they had got eight or ten more on the order of Wally and Ham.

To Phil this seemed too sloppy. He still wanted to breed his own and was determined to fight it out with 

Pop. To breed horses, you ought to think in decades, he could see. Can Pop see it that way? Can I expect him to, at his age? Molly would see it! Molly's not here. 

Molly's got a good job in New York, with a great big lovely future. Molly gives me a pain! Shut up, Marshall! She'd be back if she belonged here; and if she doesn't, you've no business to-oh, shut up! No business? You fool; no chance, you mean! 

Well, horses aren't like rabbits. You breed a mare this spring, say, and maybe you get a foal next spring. 

If you do, and if it is any good, you have a start. But you can't really tell whether it is any good for a year or so, and you don't get much use of it until it is four or five. And is there any way of telling ahead whether you are going to breed something that will be good? Select the best mare you can find, probably, and breed her to the best stallion you know of. Best for what? Size? color? gaits? disposition? Better try to breed a useful dude horse as cheap as you can, at first. But dude horses sell for little or nothing, in the raw, before they're made; and a good riding horse might be worth a thousand back East. If you kept him, your dudes would know the difference, I'll bet. Would it cost much more to produce one of that kind than a lop-eared cayuse? 

Phil was prepared for a battle on this matter of raising horses and so was a little surprised when he won his point the first time it was discussed. Pop even added some reasons of his own, such as the pleasure the youngsters and most grown-ups would have in seeing the foals running beside their dams.

DUDE RANCHER 

"Can't see no better way to blow in the profits," he laughed. "An' it is more fun to use one you raised yourself, like old Blackie 'n Bess."

But, to Pop, who would have done what other ranchers did, that is, breed any mare you had to the nearest mustang stallion, Phil's concern for type seemed academic. Finally, however, when he realized that it was serious to Phil, he encouraged him to study the various breeds, and did not urge his conviction that Phil would swing back in the end to the old reliable cowpony type.

Naturally, Phil had long known something about horses in a general way; and, since he had been West, he had done what reading he could to fill in the blanks. 

But now he got down to cases and drew from the library in Denver all the books he could find that might help him. He found a good deal that he wanted, but there was one trait common to almost all the writers that irritated and confused him. Every horse expert, apparently, was strongly prejudiced in favor of one breed or type, just as Pop favored the native mustang and was blind to the merits of the others. There was no agreement. 

So Phil made a list of all the more familiar breeds and tried to weigh the advantages of each for himself and the needs of the ranch. Mustang. Morgan. Thoroughbred. Arab. Standardbred. Saddlebred. Quarter

Horse. The others and the various pony breeds he left out as being too rare or too specialized. And he filled a sheet with notations: 

Mustang. Ranchers are strong for them, but why do they have to be so small and so lacking in uniformity

of conformation? Why can't any of them move the way 

Pan does?

Morgan. Good, general purpose animal. Strong. Intelligent. Rather good-looking but heavy in the mane and tail and too thick in the neck. Probably a good bet if there are any around. 

Thoroughbred. Speed and looks, of course, and size.

But Pop says they're all crazy. And too fragile. Bred just for racing.

Arab. The men who write about them say they are perfect, of course. And nobody else does any more than mention them. Besides, where are there any?

Standardbred. Developed for trotting races and hence probably one-sided. Dad's mare Lou is sensible, though, and not a bad ride; and I'll bet she'd have jumped if he'd let me try her. Still, not so good in rough country. 

Saddlebred. Developed for pleasure riding. Easy gaits, good looks, size enough. A good bet if not too expensive. 

Quarter Horse. Seems to be about the same thing as they mean by "Steeldust." Must be the cowhorse extraordinary: has a good deal of thoroughbred blood somewhere; can go like blazes for a quarter of a mile; much prized for general ranch work. Have to look into this. 

Phil looked into them all and was discouraged. Can't buy one set to ride and another for broodmares; have to ride them all. And purebred mares of any type would cost, he found, much more than a rancher would care to risk under an inexperienced dude. Then, with spring at hand, he had to think of something besides horses. 

Those cabins and that shower-room were not going to build themselves. But still, the horse problem was im-

DUDE RANCHER 

portant; and if he could buy soon, the cost would be less than it would be later on. So he hunted up Colonel 

Hambleton Bliss at the auction yard and asked his advice. "Son," the Colonel said, "I'm like you, only more so.

I like horses, all kinds; and I don't forget the big babies, Percherons, Clydes, Belgians. I'm a amachoor, I guess.

But you take any o' these here experts, they all got one breed and can't see no other. A guy gets breedin' horses for himself, he's sure to go daffy one way another. You'll see. But what you want to go foolin' 'round with fancy stuff for? Why not stick to the good ol' broncs?”

Phil tried to explain some of the things that were bothering him. "Mmmm," the Colonel said, "tell you what you do. 

You go see Rutherford, Major Rutherford, down to 

Colorado Springs. He's in charge o' the Remount there. 

Gets around to every place they raise horses. Ought to know where you can find the right thing, if anybody does. And he might give you some ideas, too." "What's the Remount?" Phil asked. "Cavalry post. Buys replacements for the army. Puts out studs with ranchers. You better call him 'fore you go down; might be out around the country." 

Phil telephoned the Remount office and arranged to see Major Rutherford a few days later, when he would be back from an inspection trip. He forgot horses, then, and spent the time trying to build up the supply of firewood, which had dwindled to a point where Pop was threatening to use the kitchen chairs. Evenings he put in mailing out ranch circulars, larger this season, and containing three photographic cuts.

Major Rutherford was a busy man, but when he saw what Phil was about, he became interested and friendly at once and led him out to the stables, a series of long, low frame structures, divided into box-stalls, each with its door to the outside. "That's the trouble with horse-breeding in this country," he said. "Cattle, sheep, dogs, even pigeons-breeders keep records, study their breeds, select for type. But with horses it's been hit or miss, any old stock. Breed what you have around and sell your best fillies because they'll bring in a little cash. Particularly out here in the 

West, where everybody used to think the native cowpony was unbeatable. "Why, at the time of the last war," he went on, “the cavalry buyers for France and England, and later the United States, assumed that they would find in the West an inexhaustible reserve of good remounts. It just wasn't there. Careless or promiscuous breeding always produces degeneracy, reversion from every quality that a few careful breeders have built up over hundreds of years. It works just as fast in horses as in any other animal, and it's particularly bad out here, where stock has to be grown under range conditions, rather than stabled and pushed along the way it is in Kentucky, for instance." "The cattlemen don't think their stock is degenerate," Phil remarked. "No, most of them don't think anything about it, really," the Major explained. “They just have it, and because it's all they have, they think it must be good. 

And they get lots of encouragement from all the stories

DUDE RANCHER 

and songs about the noble, intelligent, tireless mustang." "But can't he do all he has to do?" Phil asked. "Sure he can, but not half so well as he could if he had been bred with some end in view. There are enough ranchers who have made a study of it to prove that. The men who worship the mustang say that he has been bred selectively, because he's had to survive desert conditions, heat and cold, poor grass and not much of it, long stretches without water. And in a way they're right. 

He has developed endurance and wiriness and intelligence. He's sure-footed. But he has also grown small and straight-shouldered and slow-what we call the mustang, that is. But you could almost say there is no real mustang." "I don't understand," Phil said. "What I mean is that the mustang is supposed to be a straight descendant of the Spanish horses that came up from Mexico before this country was settled. If there are any of those left, they are running wild in some remote arroyo in Arizona, only a few of them and too small to be of much use. The so-called mustangs you see on all the ranches are a haphazard mixture of the original strain with every kind of stuff the ranchers brought with them from the East, plantation horses, Morgans, trotters, scrub work stock-more of that than anything else, probably, if you can judge from the long feather and heavy neck that are so common." "Then you don't think we'd better go in for mustangs," Phil said, and he mentioned the table of breeds he had made. "Got it with you?" the Major asked, and when Phil

took it out of his pocket and handed it over, he read it with care, then turned to Phil and smiled. "I see you favor the Morgan, the saddlebred, and the quarter horse," he said, "and you dismiss the thoroughbred, the

Arab, and the standardbred. Well, I hope this won't hurt your feelings, but you couldn't be more wrong. 

You've got it turned just backwards."

Hmm, thought Phil, another fanatic; must have his own secret breed up his sleeve. But he laughed and asked for an explanation. "Well," and the Major laughed, too, "let's take 'em in order. I overstated the case about the Morgan: he's a fine animal, lots of quality. A real American breed, too. But I suppose I look at it as an army buyer; and straight Morgan, especially in this country, is pretty sure to breed below the size we require. Let me explain before we go ahead, that you are going to be selling your homebreds someday, and you want to command the widest market you can. Now, the Morgan is fine for lots of things; but for polo, for hunting, for city mounted police, for the Remount, he hasn't much chance. We have a government Morgan farm, though, and we're experimenting. "Then take the saddlebred. You will find army men mostly prejudiced against him. First because he runs to fancy gaits that we don't want, that mean a lot of lost motion. His action is too high to be efficient. Second, because he has been bred for the showring, where looks count and bottom is forgotten. We think he is beautiful and soft. But you'd better talk to someone else than an army man about the saddlebred. We can't see him at all. 

Then the quarter horse, or maybe the steeldust. (The

DUDE RANCHER 

professionals will try to tell you that the "steeldust" is one of five different strains of "quarter horse.") I don't think there is such a breed; but, granting there is, none of the individuals I have seen is built to carry a saddle. 

He has no withers, but a beefy hunk of shoulder where the withers should be. As you note, he can go like blazes for a short sprint; but then he folds. Train him for polo, and, if the saddle stays on, which it won't, he's fine for the first half of the first chukker. His legs are too short for a good walk, and with shoulders like that he pounds at the trot. Forget him. "Now take the breeds you pass over. Take the Arab first, because he's the foundation of all the other light breeds we have today. The Arab is as near perfection as a horse can be. He has everything: endurance, intelligence, looks, smooth action, speed enough to leave anything but a thoroughbred in any race over a quarter mile. Everything, that is, but size. And, pound for pound, he can do more work at any job you give him than any other horse in the world. But," and the Major sighed, "you're right in passing him up. There just aren't any Arabs, not a thousand in the whole country. 

We have a few stallions in the Remount, but they're not being bred to Arab mares. The Arab illustrates, incidentally, what might have been done with the mustang, if the ranchers had been willing to sit up nights for five hundred years, planning matings for the very best of their horses, instead of turning them out all together and leaving it to Cupid. "Then take the thoroughbred. He's largely Arab in his makeup, but he has been bred two hundred years for speed over a distance, and this has brought size. Size and

stamina and courage and intelligence. The thoroughbred is not bred for looks, like the saddlebred, but for performance. Performance above everything else, so that you find him in all shapes and sizes. But he's tested, isn't given the chance to breed on unless he has the qualities that, transmitted to his offspring, will make it a more useful animal. That's why it is something more than a joke when we say that horse-racing is conducted for 'the improvement of the breed.' Almost all the remounts we buy are half-thoroughbred or better; and you will find thoroughbred blood, up to 100 per cent, in polo ponies and hunters, and even cowhorses, in a few places. "And the standardbred. He's tested, too; on the trotting track. Probably beats the thoroughbred for ruggedness and soundness. But, at best, he isn't such a good ride as the thoroughbred. I'd take him ahead of all those others, though; every time." "Then you think we should raise thoroughbreds," 

Phil said with some surprise. "No, I don't." Major Rutherford stopped in front of one of the box stalls and pointed in through the open upper half of the door. "Here's my horse," he said. “Like to see him?” 

He entered the stall, snapped a shank onto the big gelding's halter, led him out into the sunlight, handed the shank to Phil, and took off the blanket. Phil saw a bony, muscular animal, mouse-color where his winter coat was clipped short, but showing a bright bay in the unclipped parts, the legs and the place where the saddle rested. "What an animal!" Phil exclaimed. "Sixteen-one,

DUDE RANCHER 

isn't he? And half of it above the elbow! Real withers, too, and a big barrel." "Walk him out," the Major said, and the horse started behind Phil with a long, low-swinging stride that covered ground faster than Phil cared to walk. “That's the most important gait you can have," he said when Phil stopped again in front of the stall. "It takes a real leg and some thoroughbred blood to get it, and you're not sure of it, even then." "You said 'no' when I asked you whether we shouldn't raise thoroughbreds," Phil took up the thread again, as they walked back toward the office. "Why not?" "Because it's racing makes the thoroughbred what he is. Breed him away from the track a few generations, and you don't know what you've got. Weeds probably, and there's nothing worse than a thoroughbred weed: leggy, waisty, herring-gutted, anaemic. All the temperament but none of the talent. He's a disgrace to the breed. No, to be given a chance in the stud, a horse should not only be a good individual himself, and preferably a good performer; he should come of parents and grandparents who could go out on the track year after year and lick the best that anybody could bring against them. I assume that you're not going to raise horses for the big tracks. And, anyway you couldn't grow them out enough to race as two-year-olds, not in your country. And you'd have to if you wanted to stand any chance of breaking even on the cost of producing them. "No, Mr. Marshall, for the thoroughbred we have to thank the extravagance and devotion of men who didn't mind throwing away millions to produce him. The only

ones who come near balancing their books are a handful of breeders in Kentucky and a few other states, who do nothing else and sell their crop as yearlings. In general, it's a costly sport, not a business." "Which of the breeds do you think we ought to try, then?" "None of those you've mentioned, but another, the half-bred." "Is that a breed?" Phil was dazed. “Yes, in a way it is. It has a stud-book and a registry of its own, managed by the American Remount Association. Most simply stated, a half-bred is any horse whose sire was a thoroughbred and whose dam was not. Any mare you have, bred to a thoroughbred stallion, would produce a horse eligible to registration in the half-bred book. Of course, if the mare was no good, it's likely the foal would be a poor thing, too; but we had to begin somewhere. Later, after there are enough registered half-bred mares, their offspring will be the only ones eligible. The book will be 'closed,' in other words. 

Then you'll have a breed with all the credentials of the thoroughbred on the father's side, and with dams almost as well bred but chosen for their general usefulness rather than for their racing record or promise." "But somebody must have to buy and keep thoroughbred sires, to make the half-bred possible," Phil objected. "Yes, Uncle Sam buys 'em, and the farmer keeps 'em," the Major answered. “That's a big part of our work.

After the last war, when, as I told you, they couldn't find a decent supply of remounts for the cavalry, the army and a few horsemen woke Congress up to what

DUDE RANCHER 

had been going on. A bill was passed, with a small appropriation. We began buying young stallions such as we could get for seven or eight hundred dollars because an injury had sent them from the track, or more often because their bloodlines were not fashionable enough to suit millionaire breeders. Some top horses have been in the Remount; Sir Barton, for instance; and Pillory, right here in Colorado. And another Remount horse,

Mentor, may have saved the important line of Hanover from extinction, through his great son, Wise Counsellor. "We have about 700 stallions now, thoroughbreds all but a few; and we assign them to farmers and ranchers who have enough decent mares to make it worth while and whom we consider good enough horsemen to be trusted." "How much does it cost them?" "Just the keep of the stallion, and they can charge a ten-dollar stud fee to outsiders, to lighten the expense.' "Who owns the foals?" Phil was getting an idea. "The man who owns the mare. The army does not even take an option. You see, it will give us a reserve of good cavalry mounts that we can buy as we need them. 

But it isn't just a military idea. Horsemen generally, and others who can use their eyes, have come to see that the standard of light horses all over the country needs to be improved. They believe that this is a public concern and that this sort of government support is the cheapest way of doing the job." "Could I get one of your stallions?" Phil asked eagerly. 

The Major smiled. "Not now. You haven't the mares, and if you do get them, we'll want to be pretty sure they

are good enough. Also, you ought to be able to breed at least twenty, your own and other people's, to make it worth while. Let's see, isn't there a stallion near you?" "A man on the ranch next to us, Snagler's his name, has one, not a thoroughbred, though.' "What's the name of the horse?" "Prince of Cimarron." "Never heard of him. What breed?” "Uh-h, Pal-Palomino." 

"Ouch! Another one of those! That isn't a breed, though the boys who go in for them would like to have you think it is. Of course, there again, as an army man 

I'm prejudiced against the off color. And I'm sure that in breeding for color, they lose sight of things that are really important. But you might do well to forget everything I say and try palominos. I hear there's a big sale for them to movie actors and fat sheriffs who have to ride in a parade once in a while. "But I'll give you the names of our Remount agents in the state, and if you have the feeling for a horse that 

I think you have, you'll make arrangements with one of them to breed any mares you think are good enough. 

Of course, you can breed any old scrub mare if you want to; but you won't live long enough to build her descendants up to anything you'll be proud to see around your ranch."

While Major Rutherford worked at his desk, Phil sat beside the window and looked through the list of Remount stallions. "Where's Meeker?” he asked. "What?-Oh, that's about the farthest one you could pick. 300 miles west of you, I guess; Jap Wyman. But say, there's a man you ought to see when you can! He's

DUDE RANCHER 

been at it forty years; knows as much about horses as anyone you'll find; and he'll be glad to talk with you when he learns you're interested. He has practically made over the horses in that part of the world. Great country, too. You ought to see it."

And so Phil went back to the ranch with another breed to ponder. But, while the Major had a bias, like all the others, what he said seemed to make sense. "Maybe Pop will listen better if I use the word 'halfbred' pretty often," Phil thought. "Only I'll have to watch out for the temptation to breed any mare we have. And someday I'm going to qualify for one of those government stallions. "I bet Pan's baby would suit the Major!"

XX. JUSTIFICATION DEAR FRANK: 

RFD, Mineral Spring, Colo. 

May sixth 

When you said last winter that you read my long letters and even liked them, I was pleased and meant to write you before this. But, for a simple cattle rancher, the days from February to June are full enough, if the weather is mostly clear, as it has been this spring; and when a man is also a dude rancher, it is even worse. 

These two springs we've had building and fixin' up to keep us on the jump. But at this moment it is snowing hard, adding to the sixteen inches that piled up yesterday and the day before, and there is not much we can do outside, so that I am free to write. 

You might think we'd be glad to see the snow, after an open, dry winter that Pop says bodes no good for the pasture in late summer and fall. And we are glad, for it means that the grass will come along fast now and that our oats, which showed only a few tentative shoots through the dusty earth, will germinate and grow fast. On the other hand, we are in a bad way to face a big snow and shall be hard hit if it piles up much more or if it takes long to thaw off the south slopes. You see, we went in for a little gamble on some yearling Herefords, bought them thin late in March, used a surplus of old oat hay to carry them through until they could go on pasture, leased Joe Wakefield's 213

DUDE RANCHER 

unused land (we'd like to buy it later if we can), and turned them out there with our own about three weeks ago. The idea was to let them grow during the summer and sell them for a profit in October. Now almost all our extra hay is gone, and the whole bunch is bawling around the corral in rage at the starvation diet we've been forced to put them on. When the snow started to pile up yesterday morning, Pop and I rode out to bring them back to the home pasture, just in case, and it is lucky we did. There were three we couldn't find, and Pop says it is ten to one they are done for. 

If we could get the truck through to the road and out to Denver, we could haul in a little hay and cotton-seed cake to carry the stock through the next few days after our hay is gone. I think that is out, though, because of the snow that is in our road now and the drifts that will be there if the wind blows before the sun melts a crust. We'll try it anyhow, of course, as soon as the storm lets up.-I've got so that I almost like burrowing through a drift to get at a tire-chain that has jumped off and tangled in the spring and axle. 

Our speculation in cattle represents part of the note that Pop and I signed at the bank in Mineral. But there is nothing we can do about it now. Pop is whistling rather dreadfully in the kitchen while he uses up our last gallon of paint, and I am sitting here in broad day, writing to you. I honestly think that neither one of us is worrying much, and that is another lucky thing.

One difficulty I shall find in writing to you from now on is that you are a member of the family. For some reason it is easier to write at length to people who have no connection with you by blood or law. I didn't mind not

JUSTIFICATION 

215 being able to come on for the ceremony, because big weddings, like funerals, are just a little too barbarous, even for me. But I was sorry that I couldn't manage a real wedding present for you and Nat. I'll have to owe you, I guess-perhaps you'll take a custom-designed colt. If you will, you can put in specifications for him right now, and I'll plan to deliver in six or eight years.

Anyway, I'm truly glad for you and Nat, and confident that you both have enough native meanness to prevent your becoming too stodgy too soon. 

Cooped up here with Pop since last February and loving every minute of it, I've thought a lot about this dude ranching "business" and particularly about some of the things you and a few others have said that it is unreal, for instance, an escape from the facts of modern life. 

I have a quarrel with that word "escape." It slipped over with some others from the vocabulary of the psychiatrist and is now used pretty loosely by almost anybody. Usually it seems to mean anything someone has done that looks to you pleasant but not very sensible. 

Or it means that the escapist is refusing to accept the world he has to live in. The President uses murder mysteries to "escape" from the cares of state, though; and that is all right, because it, presumably, doesn't affect his actions the next day. But what gets me about all you people who throw the word around is your admission: there is something you'd like to escape from, but you don't intend to do anything about it. I didn't miss the touch of envy in the disapproving smile I got from some people I saw in New York for the first time since I cut loose.

DUDE RANCHER 

This is what it boils down to, as I see it: Life in the city is the only one possible these days for most people. 

But there are some things about it that are bad, absolutely bad, not just relatively; and everybody knows that is true. That accounts for little matters like parks and movies and fiction thrillers. And it accounts for the moose-head over the fireplace in the thirty-fifth floor apartment, where the broker stands and thrusts out his chest and talks in a little deeper voice. It accounts for vacations, of course, and hence for dude ranches. Escape in that sense, the temporary one, seems to be universal and necessary; so there is no use trying to decide whether it is good or bad. 

A bad kind of escape, I suppose, would be one that led a man to give up and go in for egg-farming in New Jersey, though he couldn't tell a hen from a rabbit; or to teach Renaissance history because he was in love with the past but just couldn't bear the dirty present; or to become a communist organizer because he was essentially snob enough to accept the rich at their own valuation; or to start a dude ranch because he thought it would be a nice, easy, free life out in the open-away from care and grime and monotony. Or to start it because he distrusted mechanics and longed for the horseand-buggy days. 

That last is my vulnerable point. If I've gone in for this just because I find myself sentimentally liking to do things the way they were done a generation or two ago, I'll go woodsy one of these days and start a cult. And anyone who commits himself to dude ranching without knowing that he is fitted for it, could escape more promptly and effectively by cutting his throat.

JUSTIFICATION 

217 

What pleases me now, after almost two years, is that

I think I am fitted for it. I've got the superficial things like health and bodily strength and a liking for all sorts of people and animals. But, more important, and this 

I couldn't know until I'd tried it, the steaming earth in spring speaks to me; I have a patience with the processes of nature that you would consider a criminal torpor; I don't place a dollars-and-cents valuation on the hours I work, because I enjoy the work itself; and if I have a note rather than a balance at the bank, and if our books suggest the crack of doom, I list among our assets the meat on my ribs and the reserve of it out in the corral and on the hillsides. And, as I said, I don't worry.

But I don't want to brag too loudly nor to deprive you of the luxury of calling me an escapist. I admit I have what is probably an unrealistic admiration for the way they did things in the country in another day. It might conceivably ruin a pair like Pop and me. I even wrote some verses about it this morning. (Effect of spring, I guess.) They are the first I've tried since college, and they probably stink, like all the others. But, so that you may never lack proof of my retarded development:

LAMENT FOR ANYBODY'S GRANDFATHER 

(maestoso, ma non troppo) 

For sixty years he faced the gray attack 

Of dulness pelting him in steady showers. 

Twenty miles to the County Seat and back, It took his young mare then two mortal hours:

Nothing to see but crops and birds and flowers,

DUDE RANCHER 

Or, just three miles from town, the judge's face, Waiting beside his hedge to test the powers 

Of his new gelding in a smashing race 

Down maple shade to the court-house hitching space. (And Grand-dad said she'd real speed, Speed and heart a plenty. 

But down and back is pie for me, In sixteen flat, by G. M. C.!) 

When court adjourned or litigants agreed, Unless the trout were rising in the brook,

Or harrowed fields were rutting for the seed, The mayor and Grand-dad and that pious crook, Opposing counsel, crossed the street and took 

Adjacent hotel porch-chairs facing out

On micro-cosmos and the tattered book Of little things they found to talk about, Like politics and virtue and the drought. (They lit their ignes fatuos 

By striking mind on mind. 

But Reader's Digest sparks for me -And Lippmann, Swing, and N. B. C.) 

If driving sleet on Sunday kept him in, You saw the utter bleakness of his mind: 

Nothing to do but play his violin 

Or read or cut my father's hair or wind 

His fly-rod, mend an ailing watch or grind 

His axes, make new shelves for Grandma's stock 

Of jellies and the fees he took “in kind”; 

Then evening chores, a glance at the weather-cock, And chess or Ovid, if the judge should knock. (Thus recklessly he squandered life 

In doing things himself. 

Now all but sleep is done for me 

By gadgets run with sure A. C.)

JUSTIFICATION 

219 Well, that's that, and now you have me, and I don't care. So far I've made my "escape" stick and got my living by it. That ought to impress you birds who can't see anything but a balance sheet. And I'm counting for the future on the "urge to get away" that brings people out here for what they all call “a taste of real life."

Only I'm going to run my ranch the way that seems most real to me. That means there aren't going to be a lot of bucking horses and rodeo stunts and roping beef because a pack of Easterners think that's the way beef is raised. We're going to produce all we can of what we need, because it's good economics, but even more because our dudes like a place where they can see production going on and where their kids can take part in it. 

We're going to do things the ways that seem most suitable, whether they happen to be in the Wild-West tradition or not: Western saddles for any roping that has to be done; flat saddles or Western for ordinary riding, depending on what each person likes; and by all means flat saddles or none for youngsters learning to ride and for adults who are not sure they know all there is to know.

The thought of youngsters calls up another idea that I've had for some time and that I may have mentioned to you before. Kids who go to camp every summer, who have their time scheduled hour by hour so that they may get the most for their money, particularly those who go away to school and have their hours programmed much the same way in winter, miss out on something they need. That is the feeling of something coming from them, something they can do that makes for the fulness of the life of their whole community.

DUDE RANCHER 

Something necessary, not for their sake as individuals, but for the sake of the group of which they are a part. 

In the bygone days that I am fond of mooning over, on farms and in town, kids had value; and I am sure that it meant something to them that they can't get from classes in self-expression and talks on citizenship. The way things work nowadays, a boy or girl is an economic drag on his family until he's twenty or over. There is no help for that, and families certainly don't grudge the cost. But the knowledge must get back to the youngsters in one way and another; and I think it may help to account for the sense of futility and the callous selfishness that are pretty common among adolescents. 

I've tried giving them a chance to be useful in the community life during the last two summers, and you should have seen them eat it up. A sense of responsibility sprouted overnight; they identified themselves with everything that was going on; they wanted to understand what we were trying to do and why we did it that way. They were as much concerned with a sick calf or a broken window pane as I was. In short, the experience of citizenship did better than a class in it or a laboratory project for dissecting the idea. 

If that was an "escape" for them (and it well may be, for it is not the sort of life they can go on with), I still believe that it was an experience they ought to have, and one that will count later on when they have to settle down into a groove and plug away at one thing.

And now that I'm fairly launched on a psycho-analysis of society, I may as well say that I think plugging away at one thing is a chief cause of much of the nervousness and dissatisfaction that I see in adults. Doing

JUSTIFICATION 

221 one thing might not be so bad, if you could see the results of your work; but no man on an automobile assembly line can take much satisfaction in the exquisite tightness of that left mudguard on some roadster down in Tulsa. And, pardon me, I don't see how a broker can see much of himself in the first radio tube that comes out of the factory whose stock he helped market. 

Here's an example: summer before last, over at the other ranch, there was a family named White. Mr. 

White is a very successful manufacturer of plumbing fixtures, and naturally his work is all executive, has been for years. But he liked to do things, and he came to life when I encouraged him to take part in some of the ranch work. This spring, in connection with our plans for a new shower-room and some bathrooms for one or two cabins that we plan to put up, I wrote and asked his advice about equipment and methods, and particularly about the advisability of our trying to do the work ourselves. The response was amazing. “Boy," he wrote, "we certainly can do it ourselves! For me it's the chance I've been waiting for, to get my hands on a wrench again, sweat a joint. You're going to have the swellest plumbing in three counties. And Sam (that's his son) is coming out to work with his old man and spend the rest of the summer with you." He intimated that Mrs. White would have summers after this to preen 

Sam for the Social Register but that this year there was going to be a little precautionary sabotage. So the two 

Whites will be here in about two weeks; and I hope the weather permits us to get things cleared away for them by that time, because Mr. White will be at work on a

DUDE RANCHER 

tree-house or something if he can't go right ahead with the plumbing.

So you see that our feeling about youngsters (Mr. 

White would be flattered to have me include him) is going to play a part in our policy for the ranch. And it isn't just that I want to build Utopia and tamper rashly with the youth: it's good economics. You can take care of more kids in a given space than you can adults, and kids are more apt to come back summer after summer if you do something that they like and that their parents consider worth while.

But I started to deliver a few wise words about settling down to do one thing all your life. There isn't much use in hashing it over, though, because it must be. But of this I'm sure: the farmer or rancher is lucky because he has to do many different things; and, as a dude rancher, I'm even luckier because I have to learn all of those and dozens that he never heard of.

We're going to breed horses, for instance. 

Pop and I have a quarrel about "What is a horse?" that dates from the beginning of our acquaintance. It isn't settled yet, but I'm making progress, and I'm going to win. So is he, when he finds out. First thing I had to do was to get him into the idea of raising horses at all. He said it didn't pay; you could buy them cheaper than you could raise them; they took range that ought to be supporting cattle.

But it turned out that he was "easy licked" on that question because he is as fond of them as I am; and fortunately he doesn't have to settle every question by asking whether it pays or not. To both of us that is irrelevant if we enjoy it enough and it doesn't bust us.

JUSTIFICATION 

223 And I'm not even convinced that in the very long run we can't make it pay. Suppose you had to sell your surplus and your culls at a loss. Wouldn't it "pay," perhaps, if your crowd went home and talked of your ranch as the place that had the best doggone horses they'd ever seen? A cavalry major I consulted tells me we're in a position to make it pay if anyone can because we have a use for lots of horses in our dude ranch, and it won't be as if we were simply raising them for the market.

I took my own mare, two other good-sized ones we just picked up, and a little one we bought last summer -left them all with a rancher down south of Denver to be bred to a strapping big son of Whisk Broom. The major said it would be folly to breed the little one because she'd give us a scrub; but I argued with myself that we'll need a good many ponies for the smaller kids. 

And a garden! I outraged Pop's class pride as a rancher by rooting the willows out of a black patch of earth near the creek and planting vegetables. Work for 

Mexicans, he calls it, and at first he just turned up his nose; but I've caught him a few times lately yanking weeds out of the carrots and peas.-No, this snow will do them good. 

And chickens. I made over one of the sheds into a brooder house and got a lot of babies for broilers this summer and for replacements in our laying squad next year. This, I'm afraid, will turn out to be a boner. It looks as if we should be able to buy them ready for the pan cheaper than we can raise them. And there are coyotes. I want to turn the chicks loose to work on the worms and bugs and the oats that get tossed down into

DUDE RANCHER 

the mangers; but the coyotes sit by the hour across the gulley, waiting for me to open the gate. Pop picks one off with his rifle every now and then, but I am a hopeless shot and don't scare anything but the livestock. I am working on a plan to get all the coyotes into the pen and turn the birds out, but some of the details are holding me up. 

May tenth.

I couldn't get this out to the mail box the day I wrote it. Snow piled up, and cattle kept us busy. Sun has been busy for the last two days, and tomorrow we're going to drive the cattle, very gently, back to pasture. They look bad, and Pop says it will take them a month or more to gain back what they lost. Horses are all getting fat. 

Love to Nat, Yours,

PHIL.

XXI. DYNAMITE 

YOU start southwest as if you were going to Mineral

Spring, but about three miles up the cañon you will notice, carved out of the rock, a little road that branches south and winds steeply across the face of the cliffs, to disappear high up on the shoulder of the big gulch that enters the cañon at this point. It is such a little road, and the view over the unwalled edge is so vertiginous that most of the curious motorists from Denver and the out-of-state tourists inspecting the mountains reconsider a quarter of a mile up. It is broad enough there to turn around, for the road crosses the throat of small Black Gulch before starting up the mountain again. They stop, usually, turn back and hold their breath until they come down to the highway again.

Once a farmer from Kansas, coasting down with his family in their pickup, put on the brakes after a heavy rain, and you can still see the frame of their car sticking partly out of the washed sand at the bottom. 

But the ranchers who live back up the little road think nothing of it, and their friends know that, once you have got out of the cañon, there is a broad tableland, good for hay in damp summers, and the best place within miles for the Fourth of July celebration. They regard the mile and a half of spectacular cliff-side as a sort of insurance against too many nosy outsiders.

Frank Hanning was among the first to arrive. He got the old khaki blanket and the lunch basket from the 225

DUDE RANCHER 

back of his pickup, whistled to the little collie to jump out over the end-gate, and settled Mildred and the baby on the blanket, in the shade of a large yellow pine. "This is the best one in the whole field," he said, "'cause the ground's smooth on the whole north side. 

You always got a good place to move to when the sun catches up with you. Most these trees got rocks or cactus or both 'round 'em. I been comin' to this one since I was the size o' Baby, I guess.-So long, Mil; I'll be back soon 's I can get your ma from Mineral. My folks'll be along 'fore I get back, and they'll come straight to this tree."

Frank wanted to be sure that Ma came down to the celebration today. He knew that she could be depended upon to take the right amount of responsibility for 

Baby, so that Mildred could circulate a little and see her friends and his. Mil had been pretty tied down lately, missed the celebration altogether a year ago, just before the baby came, and hadn't got around much since. His own mother would be glad to take the responsibility, of course, but she was just a little too possessive. Better make her see the baby had two grandmothers. And his parents, the ranching Hannings, might as well get to know the mining Hannings from 

Mineral. Ranchers aren't the only people in the world.

Before he started off in his car, he walked across the broad field of sparse grama grass to a spot under a clump of trees, where he could see two men and several horses. 

Patches was there, his entry for the big race. He had ridden him over the day before and left him with Len Baldwin, because it would be better for him not to make the trip on the day he was to race and because

Mil didn't like to drive with the baby rattling around alone on the seat. "Hi, Len, Bill," he greeted the two young ranchers. Dynamite lunged back against the halter, found it held, lunged forward, and reared, pawing with his front feet.

A few days before the Fourth, Phil had driven over to consult Pike Moore, chairman of the committee. "Sure! Glad to have the whole bunch," Pike had responded warmly. "The more, the merrier. It'll give us a chance to see is them Eastern kids any good at footracin' 'n such. Bring any balls 'n bats you got. Might git up a game.'

Since it would be a job, Phil knew, to get them all organized and ready, he set breakfast half an hour ahead. Putting up the lunch for sixteen was no stunt for Minnie Brush, who had not missed a picnic on “The

Flat" for twenty years and who knew how to get the most out of the helper Phil had engaged for the summer. But some of the crowd wanted to ride over, and it still took a long time to get them saddled and ready. 

Others, like Mrs. Fiske from Baltimore, whose Helen and Alan were twelve and nine, would better ride in the truck.

DYNAMITE 

231 There had been activity for an hour before most of the crowd got up. Molly was in the kitchen wrapping sandwiches as they came off the assembly line, and storing the coffee and the watermelon and the oranges in clean gunny sacks. Phil fed the horses that were to be used for the long ride, threw two big forkfuls of hay into the back of the truck to serve as upholstery, and a sack of oats for the horses, then laid out all the saddles and pads and sent Bobby Oakes to instruct the riders about taking their ponchos or raincoats. Pop went down the line, checking for loose shoes or stones jammed between shoe and frog. 

After breakfast Mrs. Fiske, who had a headache and was feeling the altitude after only three days at the ranch, decided she was not up to the trip; but Mr.

White teased her until she agreed to ride in front with 

Phil and Miss Holmes from Cambridge, while he climbed in behind to keep order among the youngsters. 

Bob Oakes and Sam were riding, of course, as was 

Harold Austin, who liked to consider himself a veteran on the basis of his overnight experience of the year before. Molly let these three get themselves in order, but she had to help Bill Henderson from New York and the two girls from Chicago. Though they had been at the ranch two weeks and so were permitted to ride their horses to the picnic, they still were baffled a little by the intricacies of throat-latches and cinches. Then, while Phil held Pan's head, Molly climbed into the saddle. "It's pretty noble of you to let me ride her, Phil," she said. "I'm so excited and out of training that I'll probably fall off."

DUDE RANCHER 

"No, you won't," Phil laughed. "She'll probably shy a little at the cars on the main road, but it's just play." 

Molly, riding behind the troop, looked back and waved just before they disappeared among the fresh green of aspens lining the narrow road. Pop and Phil waved back, then went to finish loading the truck. Besides Phil and Miss Holmes and Mr. White and the

Fiske family there were two fourteen-year-old boys, rather sulky because Phil had decided that they were not acclimated enough for the ride, after only four days at the ranch, and Minnie Brush, who sat in one front corner, guarding the food. 

Pop insisted on staying home with Minnie's helper. "Can't ever'body go larkin' off; so, much as I hanker fer the heat an' dust an' chatter an' ants, I'm gonna stay put; take it easy. White's got some little jobs laid out'll keep me out o' mischief 'til you git back."

In spite of Phil's effort at an early start, his crowd reached the arena after the morning competition had begun, but not too late for Sam White to win the balloon-blowing and Harold Austin to make a creditable second in the boys' hundred-yard dash. The dudes got no distinction in events like the sack-race and the potato-race; but Phil, paired off with Bill Miles, was first by three seconds in the log-sawing; and Miss 

Holmes astonished everyone by driving a big spike to its head in a heavy timber with fewer strokes than any other woman.

One event for which everyone stopped and gathered around, laughing, was the rolling pin contest. A strawstuffed dummy, wearing overalls, coat, and hat, was set up at one end of an open space. All the ladies who

DYNAMITE 

233 could be persuaded to enter gathered at the other end and took turns hurling the rolling pin. Direct hits were greeted with a mixture of boos from the men and cheers from the women, while a wide miss by any unmarried girl was hooted as a shoddy device to snare a husband. 

Minnie Brush took first by a wide margin, but Molly was well up among the also-rans. 

Buck Snagler, the only representative present from 

Pinnacle Park, the somewhat untopographical new name of Joe Wakefield's lodge, dominated the numberone horseshoe pit, of course; but he was glad that Hap, in the two years since he had seen him, had improved enough to make it look like a real game.

Whenever one event was over, there were several minutes of confusion, while ranchers and their families milled around, hailing friends, or crowded at the refreshment stand for beer or pop. The committee, meanwhile, with volunteers and conscripts, would be setting the stage for the next contest.

When everything was ready, Pike Moore would grasp the bell he needed to supplement the thunder of his voice in such a crowd, ring for order, thrust out his red beard, and bellow the continuation of the program. "All right, ever'body, pay attention, please, or we'll never get through. The next event is bareback wrastling. Git your horses quick, ever'body's gonna enter. 

Come on, Slim Miller, git away from them girls an' git in this. You c'n see them seven nights a week, but it's only once a year you c'n always win this here beeyutiful e-lectric flashlight put up by, let's see, put up by 

Hodkins Hardware. All right, git on your horses, an' when I ring the bell, go to it. Every man for himself

DUDE RANCHER 

an' no gangin' up. An' you keep them long toes o' yours out from between the horse's front legs, too, Slim; I seen you last year."

In bareback wrastling you simply try to stay on your horse as long as you can, while you ride around and throw opponents on the ground. Great strength in hands and wrists is important, but luck and caution count heavily, too, and experience. Phil, who had wisely chosen the most sluggish and “under-slung" of his recently acquired dude horses, managed to stay on until the field had dwindled to three, including Slim Miller and Frank Hanning. Phil laughed when he felt himself sliding off backwards in the lobster grip of Frank's huge hands. He hit the ground hard, called out a challenge for next year, and watched Frank turn to tackle Slim. 

Slim's wiriness and feline quickness had made him the winner for the last three years; but this time he was over-confident and careless, so that Frank was able to close with him and lift him bodily from his mount, though he had both arms around the horse's neck and was wriggling like a hooked trout. While Slim dusted himself off and rejoined the girls, Frank collected his prize from Pike and went over to his family.

Eating was done in family groups, not from clannishness, but because it was easier for your friends to find you if they wanted to say hello. Ethel and Bill Miles, both strained and touchy, were glad of Molly's invitation to eat with the dudes. Almost every large pine around the edges of the long cleared space cast its mottled shade on a jungle of sliced ham and cold fried chicken and creamy yellow potato salad and big “party” layer cakes already cut into heroic sectors. From each

DYNAMITE 

235 small encampment a child would leave once in a while and come back with his arms full of frosty bottles of pop and beer, for there was no water on the flat. The air was filled with laughter, scoldings, yelps of smacked infants, jokes and news shouted across from group to group. Food trades were frequent. "Oh, Mrs. Nevins, I want you should taste this cake. 

It's a new recipe I got from the farm magazine.” "I heard you say you're out o' chicken, Lou. Here, we brought heaps too much." “A doughnut, sonny? Two hours was just enough for food, gossip, cleaning up, relaxing a little-and for the sun to pass its hottest

DUDE RANCHER 

peak. Pike Moore downed a last beer at one gulp, looked at his watch, drew a wrist across his whiskers, and roared for attention. There were to be horse events of all kinds: a children's race for boys and girls under fourteen; a slow race, in which riders switched horses by lot and the last horse across the line was the winner; musical chairs; then the main horse race; last, perhaps, a baseball game, if anyone thought there was time before going home to milk and return for the dance in the schoolhouse.

When Phil found that the children's race was a matter of some hundred yards over pretty fair ground and that the contestants were really below the age limit, he let his boys enter their horses.

There was a lineup of fifty or more, a pause while the starter pushed one or two embryo "gyppers❞ back of the mark, the crack of a blank cartridge, and they were off. The flailing of arms and legs was in a much faster tempo than the movement of the ponies; but still the thunder of hoofs was exciting to everybody. The winners crossed the finish line while the main bunch was far back and a few stragglers had hardly started. 

Phil was glad to see the representatives of the ranch finish unhurt in the anonymous middle, whooping just as delightedly as if they had won. In fact, except for a certain practical envy of the prizes awarded to the first two and the last riders, all the entrants seemed to feel that they had done satisfactorily. 

Musical chairs was a fine game, too; but Phil did not enter, because it called for training in a horse such as no dude mount is likely to have: sudden stops on the haunches, a quick getaway, skill in "riding off." Here

DYNAMITE 

237 the old-fashioned cowboy on his mustang was ahead of all competitors.

He did not enter the slow race, either, nor let the others, for he realized that a victory would be a poor recommendation for any horse he expected to hand out to one of his ranchers. And it seemed a sort of backhanded treatment of loyal but sedentary horseflesh. 

At last Pike called the ten-minute warning for the main race, and there was an eager buzzing and shifting of the crowd as they found places along both sides of the scant half-mile, running from east to west, with its dog-leg to the left about two-thirds of the way to the finish. There were several slight ups and downs, one or two boulders, a number of bushes, and innumerable small stones. But it was the best place on the flat. 

Starters and judges got into position, and men were posted on the course as buoys or lighthouses beside the worst rocks. "Don't you wish you were in this, Molly?" Phil whispered as they squeezed in on the south side near the start. "Mmmmm!" Molly breathed out hard with excitement. "You know, I almost won it once, Phil, before 

Dad got rid of all our best horses." 

Though Pike had said ten minutes, it was more like half an hour before all the saddles were on and girths and stirrups tested. Then there was the shoving about for a good position; arguments and hot retorts, sidebets and challenges. After everything seemed ready, you could see stragglers coming over from the grove, yelling, "Hey, wait a minute." Buck was right on time, there in the middle, conspicuous on the cream-colored

DUDE RANCHER 

Prince, dressed in a new doeskin jacket with fringes, the silver of his saddle gleaming in the sun. The Prince stood calm and confident, and Buck himself was all business for once, with his rawhide quirt and sharp spurs; no loud talk and no showing off.

Among the last to line up was Bill Miles with Dynamite. He had tried to mount near his hitching tree and nearly lost hold of his horse in trying. Then he started to lead him over but had to call some friends to get behind and shoo the quaking beast along. With two men at the bridle, Bill was hoisted up and led forward to the line, where a space opened for him somehow and he entered, just three to the left of the Prince of Cimarron.

Still they were not off. Though most of the horses stood quietly enough, here and there a green one, upset by the large crowd, would start ahead and try to break away. He would have to be led back through, turned around and pointed in again.

Dynamite was far the worst: he reared high, plunged forward with his front hoofs striking, his eyes wild, foam in his mouth. He refused to go back through and was led all the way around the end. The second time up to the line, Bill deliberately squeezed in beside 

Buck: just as well to keep next to the man you have to beat. Buck seemed satisfied, too, but only gave an amused and elaborately patient smile at the sweating 

Dynamite and the straining Bill.

There was a moment of quiet, and they expected the gun. But no, a little gray down the line squealed and started kicking his neighbor, and three horses were out of position. There were murmurs, "Let's go"-"If

DYNAMITE 

239 they can't hold their horses, let 'em drop out!" But you don't do it that way. The race itself may take less than a minute, but you have to spend whatever time is necessary to give every man a chance at the break. 

And now they were getting straightened out down the line. Dynamite's tattered nerves, however, had again stood all they could take. He drew in a raucous sob, seemed to crouch, and went into the air. There was a swift intake of breath by the crowd as he came down. One hoof narrowly missed Buck's head, knocked off his sombrero, and the heel of the shoe tore a blond gash in the burnished leather of the saddle-roll.

This was too much for Buck's composure. Pale and glowering, he reached across with his right hand and brought his quirt down sharply twice on Dynamite's head. Again Dynamite went up, but this time backwards. He sat down hard on his tail, rolled to his side, got up quickly and stood trembling but made no move to escape.

Bill, on the ground, made no move at all. 

The men on horses spurred quickly ahead to get clear of him. Others flocked around, not knowing quite what to do. Ethel ran sobbing from the crowd and pushed through to kneel at Bill's side. But it was nothing, really; just a sprained wrist that had folded under him, wind knocked out, and a bloody scratch in the scalp. He rolled over on his back presently, sat up, and before he had the breath to speak, smiled at 

Ethel to reassure her. Then he got his voice back and said, "Someone . . . help. me on again, will you?" He rose to his feet unassisted. 

Ethel seized him and shook hard. "Bill, Bill! You're

DUDE RANCHER 

not going to get on that horse again today! Everything's all right, Bill, and I don't care. Do you hear me?" 

Bill smiled wanly, started to speak; but the color left his face, and he settled in the dirt, holding his bad wrist.

While friends were helping him up again and leading him into the shade, Molly came to life. "Phil,” she said and took him strongly by the arm, "I'm going to ride that horse."

Phil was petrified for a moment, but then he burst out, "Molly, no! You're crazy. I won't let you!” "You won't stop me! I can't tell you why now, but 

I've got to! Bring him around here and help me up.' "Molly, I don't care why nor what you say: as long as I can stand here and fight, you are not going to get on that horse." 

Molly stiffened and bit her lip. There were needles in her eyes, but they met something the same in Phil's. "All right," she said in a choking voice, "you ride him then." "I'm going to," Phil answered quietly, “if Bill will let me. Can't race a man's horse without his consent." 

A sudden inspiration came to Molly. "And I'm going to ride Pan," she blurted. There were tears of anger in her eyes. “And I'm going to ride rings around you!" "Ride anything you like," Phil snapped and went over to Bill.

There was a general feeling of relief because Bill was not badly hurt and because Molly had not carried through her plan, so that the lineup formed again slowly and casually.

DYNAMITE 

241 Phil obtained the judges' permission to substitute his own flat saddle, which he got from under the tarpaulin in the truck. Then Bob Oakes came back from the grove leading Pan, and the only saddle for Molly was the one Phil had removed from Dynamite. After another delay, while the stirrups were being laced shorter to fit her, she and Phil, not looking at each other, found places in the line, and the judges galloped down to the finish again. 

Buck was sitting calmly on the calm Prince, but scowling now and running a thumb up and down the tear in the saddle. Pan was bewildered but walked obediently enough into a space two to the right of Buck, and Phil, with his stirrups up to the bottoms of the skirts, was coaxing Dynamite into a place five or six horses to his left. Riders eased away from him on both sides, so that he had little trouble getting into line. Most of the devil seemed to be gone out of the horse; too much, perhaps, Phil thought. 

The starter was wide-awake, didn't wait for a perfect alignment. The instant he saw that Phil was going to get in, he pulled the trigger.

Dynamite's real and probably much more aristocratically allusive name had been lost to man when the oat-dealer, one of several, had neglected to secure the certificate, before he sold him for a feed bill. Now, if his jumpy nerves were not blocking his long horsememory, Dynamite may have been roaming back to the blue-grass pasture and the white board fence many, many springs ago when he was a yearling in Kentucky. 

A racy yearling, alert and full of life; but a little

DUDE RANCHER 

flat in the ribs, a little thin in the waist, too much neck and not enough bone. And so, when the extra-specials were drafted for the August sale at Saratoga, two more months with the lesser fry on the farm. The Lexington sale, then, $400; to an Italian from Wilmington, with a reputation on the minor tracks for doing profitable things with cheap yearlings. The breaking that fall: more oats, less hay, no pasture; gallops in the early morning under a sleepy exercise boy; the bell ringing for practice starts from the stall-gate; breezing on the track; time trials and the nods of the trainer holding his stop-watch; the sharp pain of the firing-iron and a month's layoff for the swollen knee, immature bones' response to early pounding. At it again and ready for the track as a two-year-old in February. The starter's bell. Fourth in the first race but closing fast; second three days later; then two wins and a move up in the betting. Many more races and enough win, place, and show, money for hay and oats and shipping. In December another bad knee after the thirty-eighth start.

Claiming races as a three-year-old: $2500, $1800, $1500, $1000; won two at that and claimed by an owner-trainer from Michigan. Entered at $1250, $1000, $800; claimed again. As a five- and six-year-old always wearing blinkers and bandages, the jockey always dredging with whip and spurs for that last buried ounce of speed. At seven relinquished for a feed bill and sold to the bush tracks, Nebraska, Missouri, Colorado. The hypodermic now, when the owner is set to win; and boxed by the jockey when a win is out of order. Bad ankles at ten and sold outright for $200. Chaffy oats and musty hay, or none. But

DYNAMITE 

243 back to the races at eleven and twelve and thirteen-the rodeos now with a sprint every day for a week; then into the truck and across the state to the fair. Sour and stale and mean; ache in the joints. Last place in the first two starts at fifteen; the needle again, a blind crash into the fence and the jockey out on the ground. 

Off to the auction: $35. The dusty ranch in the plains. 

No sense under the heavy saddle. Broke the cultivator.

Away from there with the gin-smelling bum, wages for a month in the bankrupt beet-field. And now up here, breathless, in this strange mob.

At the crack of the pistol there was a convulsive jabbing of spurs, a convulsive burst from the line, a sudden thunder of hoofs, a cloud of dust. With a large field and a short race the start is important, may be decisive. You'd better get among the leaders early and stay there. Buck and Frank Hanning and Len Baldwin and two or three others were clear of the pack in the first few jumps and fighting for the lead. Pan broke greenly for Molly, was almost left, but started behind the close-packed bunch and began to lengthen her stride.

Dynamite was left, flat-footed, his legs spread apart and white in his eyes. Pike Moore laughed and rang his bell above the derisive laughter of the crowd. The sound was not much like the starter's bell in the old days at the better tracks; but perhaps because he was already confused, perhaps because this saddle felt comfortable and the weight was more in the right place, Dynamite caught the idea at last and unwound. One great jump, and he was in his stride, long legs swinging through in a perfect rhythm, long neck stretched.

DUDE RANCHER 

Looking ahead, Phil knew that he was out of the race; but this was like moving in a dream, and he let the metronome keep it up, long legs swinging through, not swinging so very fast but covering twenty-five feet to a stride, and the stragglers up ahead coming nearer and nearer. 

When he knew that he should be about clear, Buck looked first to the sides and then behind him. There was old Patches dropping back a little, nothing else to worry about; and Buck took in on the reins. No use spending the Prince all at once and maybe having some plug come on at the end when the Prince's wind was gone. More fun, too, to play with Hanning a while. 

Molly had caught and passed the slowest horses before the bunch was well strung out, so that there was still a solid block ahead of her. She looked for a place to squeeze through, but Pan was not aggressive, seemed content to slacken her stride and run at the flanks of the others. And there, over their heads, Molly saw the Prince increasing his lead. "Around, then," she growled aloud and swung to the right. Pan picked up speed again on the new tack and seemed to like the clear running; but, going so wide, she had to travel farther, was carried clear to the edge of the course and hurdled a two-foot chunk of granite, whose human lighthouse disappeared with a yell. "Half way to the bend. I've got to cut in before we get there," and Molly swung in to the left. "Look out, Chet, I'm coming by," and she brushed the neck of the game gray pony as she forced him in.

The next time Buck looked around, Patches was through, three lengths behind and fading. "Easy,

DYNAMITE 

245 

Prince. It's all yours, boy." He took in again on the reins and sat back in the saddle. The Prince loved to run and did it easily, when he was out in front. Looking to the left, Buck saw the crowd, following with admiring eyes the creamy mane and flowing tail of the leader. Then he saw the faces turn and heard a mounting roar. He snapped his head around to the right and there, six lengths back, coming like a hurricane, was a big bay with Molly's burning face against the neck.

If Buck had never slackened speed in the first place, he would have won with something to spare; and, even now, if he had been less devious by nature, he would have had plenty of time to coax the Prince up to his full drive again. But he was coming into the dog-leg, the bay was on the outside of the bend; and it was more natural for Buck to keep straight past the beginning of the turn and carry his challenger wide. But he underestimated Pan's speed and had to jab home the spurs to keep from being passed on the outside. Pan's head was at his thigh now, and the Prince was shortening stride but fighting for every inch. Both horses were too wide, almost off the course. Buck had stopped looking ahead, and Molly's hard eye was measuring the 

Prince's ears. Neither saw the stump.

By the time Phil began to catch the laggards, the field was well strung out, and Dynamite knew the ropes. His scruples were fewer, too, than Phil's, and he knifed between struggling horses, throwing them out of balance but keeping his own. Into the dog-leg he charged, and Phil never knew how he cut through the dozen runners crowding for the short side of the turn. 

He opened his eyes as the pebbles stopped flying into

DUDE RANCHER 

his face, closed them again as he sliced into the thin strip of daylight between a buckskin and a roan. A rising surge of speed in the clear beyond, only two runners ahead, away off at the north, a blur of the judge's face at the southern marker. "Third, anyway, Dynamite, old boy," Phil whispered as he fought the lathering horse to a stop a quarter of a mile beyond the finish. "And I thought Pan could run!"

When Phil turned, he saw the crowd gathered at the north side of the course. Just as he trotted up, the ring broke, and Mr. White came through the opening with 

Molly in his arms. Her eyes were closed, one side of her face was a paste of blood and dirt, and one leg swung irrelevantly, in spite of his efforts to hold it still. "I can't remember anything in the book about beer for dressing wounds," Doc Livesey, the veterinary, was working over the blue furrow along Molly's cheekbone. But it's probably better 'n water, at that." He wadded the dish-towel, threw it aside, and reached for the clean one that Mrs. Hanning, Sr., held out to him. "But she won't like the smell, when she comes out of it. Now, Marshall, ready with that gentian violet. Stop shaking, will you! This don't amount to anything; scar, maybe, 'cause I'm not going to try a suture. It's that leg I'm worried about; don't have much call to set bones in my work. You sure took a chance, Mister, moving her like that.-Here, Buck, you take the bottle now. Slop some of it into those cuts, if you've got all the gravel out. "O.K., now let's see what we got here." Molly's dungarees from the hip. "Mmmm, nasty! Two of 'em, I believe, one above and one half way below the knee. Like to get 'em set if I can, before she comes to. Knock in head may be a handy thing when you got no ether."

Molly winced once while he was manipulating the bones, opened her eyes for a moment, closed them and lay quiet. "Now, gi'me those slats. Nice job o'' smoothing, Frank. You've sure got nice towels, Mrs. Hanning; shame to rip 'em. Oh, hello, Joe. Glad you got here. 

No, not too bad, I believe. May have to reset 'em when they see the X-ray, but this'll keep 'em still for now. 

No, if I was you, I'd let her lay right there 'til we can get an ambulance from Denver. Have to bend the leg too much in your roadster. You go 'phone; then beat it down the cañon and show 'em the way in here." 

He stood, picked up his kit, and turned to Phil. "Now let's look at the mare. No, you come along; you can't do any good here. Get that rope from my car. -That's right, Buck; you lay down; take it easy. I'm sorry about your horse, but he didn't suffer any."

They walked over and stopped by Pan. One man had her by the bridle, and Hap was kneeling in a puddle of blood, holding his hand in the great tear inside her front leg. "All right, Hap," Doc said to him, “hold your finger in there just a minute more, if you can, 'til I get my clips." 

Several men quickly adjusted the throwing hitch and rolled Pan over on her side, while Hap followed her

DUDE RANCHER 

movements as she went down and they trussed her tight. "All right, now you can take your finger out." The blood came in spurts. "Mmmm. Artery, of course." Doc plunged into the wound, attached a clip, made the ligature, removed the clip, and took several stitches in the skin, all in less than three minutes. "Now, let her up." "Well?" Phil asked. "Can't say. No bones broke, anyway. I didn't take time to look at the muscles and tendons. She may travel sound, and she may not. You won't know for a year. 

She's bred, you say? Well, that's good. You wouldn't want to be using her much anyway." 

When they got back to the tree, Molly was taking her part in the discussion. "Oh, Phil," she called when she saw him, and there were tears in her eyes, “did I kill her?" "No," Phil laughed, "you probably saved her from overuse during the next few months. And Doc says she'll be all right next year." 

Pike Moore took Phil by the arm. "Here's your prize for winnin' the horse race, slip for twenty-five gallons o' gas, next time you're up to Mineral.” 

Phil's jaw dropped. “Sure, you won," the judge explained. "We all seen it the same way. Molly 'n Buck went so wide you come up to the line a length ahead. Looked the other way to you, 'cause they wasn't pointin' right. You first and them tied for second. What? Sure, they hit the stump exactly at the line an' the same time. We couldn't give neither one of 'em a nostril. Shoulda been watchin' where they was goin', though. Killed
