249 one horse and mighta made a hash o' both of them." "No, Dad," Molly insisted, "Buck didn't do anything dirty. It was just dumb of him to pay any attention to me. He could have won. But when he tried to keep me on the outside, I should have pulled up just enough to cut behind him. Then I'd have won. Poor 

Pan, I let her down." "Hey, Phil," Bill Miles called from his tree. "Come over here. You didn't think that gas was yours, did you? Me and Ethel have decided you c'n keep old 

Dynamite, though, if you're fool enough to take him." "Oh, Phil, that's wonderful," Molly laughed. “Now, when Pan is well again-ouch!-I'll show you she can beat that bag of bones." “Any time, kid, any time,” Phil replied.

XXII.  

WHAT a swell summer to look back on! In spite of dust and no rain, and the pasture used up without much hope that any more will grow for winter. In spite of that note up at the bank, coming due in three weeks. Have to go up and see Dan Thompson; get him to extend it; maybe lend us some more. Good prospects for next summer. Thompson ought to consider that.

I wish Mrs. Fiske didn't have to let their bill slide. 

Perhaps things will straighten out for them this fall. 

Tough. I hope Western can wait for what we owe them on the building stuff. Mrs. Brush won't let us pay her; wants to lend us two hundred. Gosh! What a swell summer! It's having Molly around, of course. Even before they let her out of bed, she had things humming. And now she's getting so spry on her crutchestoo spry; be throwing them away and going back East first thing you know.-Wish she'd break the other leg.— 

Don't be a lout, Marshall!

Phil forked the last puny stalks of half-developed hay through the loft window and went to lunch. "Phil," Pop mumbled around a mouthful of tough boiled beef, "you been wantin' to git to see that, whatyou-call-it, Wyman place, over west. Time you went. 

Nothin' you have to do here, now all your dudes is gone. You're played out; need a change. You'll like them there roadside hotdogs, too, better 'n this prison grub you got us cut down to." With thumb and finger 250

he extracted a length of sinew. "I've et coyotes was tenderer." "But, Pop, I don't want to go away while Molly's still here. She'll be-" "I know, I know. Self-sacrificin', ain't you? Well, I tell her she'd oughta go 'long, keep you from doin' somethin' dumb. Minnie c'n stay on three, four days, cook fer me 'n straighten the place up for winter. That is, if you don't think it's scanlous.-Ouch, Minnie! You got the hardest knuckles! I didn't see you sneakin' up behind. Here, gi'me some o' that cabbage, now you're here." "Would you, Molly?” Phil looked at her eagerly. "Grandfather seems to have it all set," she answered and reached for the big mound of homemade butter. "Let's see," Pop went on, working out the details, "this Wyman you say's between Craig 'n Meeker. Must be three hundred, three hundred and fifty miles. You ain't never met Wyman; so you can't figger on campin' on him overnight. Kinda long drive anyhow, time you'll git started.-Tell you what you do, Molly: Remember your aunt Dottie Bryan, er your great-aunt, I guess she is? Only time you ever see her was when your grand-ma died 'n she come over. Well, she's got a place outside Steamboat Springs; sort o' dairy, I b'lieve it is. 

Doin' purty good, last I hear.-She don't like me; never did. Figgers I got a loose tongue an' no sense o' what's fittin'. An' then, she never forgive me fer marryin' your grand-ma. Thought she had first claim on her. "But she hadda take the three hundred dollars your grand-ma 'n me coughed up so's she could git started. 

She don't like me any better 'count o' that, neither;

DUDE RANCHER 

but it's a good reason fer her to put you up, you 'n Phil, when you're atravelin' through-be glad to, anyhow, I suppose, 'count o' your grand-ma. "Well, you stop at Mineral on your way. Give her a long-distance call who you are an' what you're up to; an' I'll bet you c'n spend the night with her. Not much of a drive, next day, over to Wyman's; an' when you've talked all the nonsense you c'n think of with him, you c'n come back to Aunt Dottie's agin. "And you, Phil, if you see she takes a likin' to Molly, an' if she lets you stay in the house, you'd better brace a chair agin' your door at night, 'cause ol' Dot'll likely be stalkin' you with an ax."

The faithful pickup wheezed and boiled over the pass but cooled off coming down the other side and purred smoothly through the meadow-land of Middle Park. Aspen groves were yellow flames licking up to the snow that was already lower on the peaks. In the broad valley, beside the gravel road, willows were changing, too, and the wild rosebushes were crimson beside the rocks. Haystacks dotted the mown meadows. "It's the first time I've been over here, Phil, and isn't it wonderful? So open, after the thick timber and narrow gulches at home." Molly loosened her overcoat that she had buttoned for the cold of the pass. "Sure is. Wish we had a few stacks of that hay. We might hold those yearlings over if we had. I came through here once last summer with- Let's see, it's three o'clock.-We won't get to Miss Bryan's in time for supper unless we step on it, or unless the road is straighter than what's behind us.-I'm glad she was cordial.-Pop had me scared."

"Oh, most of his difficulties with people are his own fault." Molly laughed. “And, of course, he knows it.

Aunt Dot was as sweet as she could be-said she was glad we called from so far off, because it will give her time to go to work on a brace of chickens that need frying." 

The day was so fine and the road west of the range so smooth that they rolled on through Granby and 

Hot Sulphur Springs, then over Rabbit Ears Pass, just in time to see the rich valley, already below the last rays of sun that reached the heights. It was only a few miles down off the pass and along the valley floor to Steamboat Springs. After the fried chickens had been demolished, and the strawberry shortcake that followed them, there was just time before dark for Phil and 

Molly to inspect the new barn and the prize herd bull.

Then they took Miss Bryan to the second movie and came home to sit up late, talking about the old days in Hammond County. 

Though Miss Bryan was clearly taken with Molly,

Phil completely forgot to brace the chair against his door. 

The next morning they drove west down the Yampa River, some of the way through wild, sagebrush country, to Craig. Here they turned south on the 

Meeker road and had to watch sharply for the little turn-off east to the Wyman ranch, along the Morapos road.

The country was really big now, great pale bluffs and mesas; tall sage on the highland; strips of meadow in the bottoms, following the streams; gnarled scrub-

DUDE RANCHER 

oak on both sides of the road; buzzards wheeling against the opal sky. "What a country in winter and spring!" Phil whistled his surprise at the bigness and loneliness. "Wonder how they ever get out. Look at the consistency of that road surface: hard and smooth now, but it must be grease after a thaw or a rain; and no bottom, I bet, if it gets soaked."

They crossed a sandy cottonwood-bottom, turned right along the face of the hill, then wound east up a smaller valley, climbing to two or three hundred feet above the watercourse. "There it is," Molly said, pointing to a group of buildings down in the meadow. "But, no, they said there'd be a sign. Probably part of the ranch, though." 

A mile or so further on they turned off over a small kill that momentarily screened the lowland, swung around the shoulder and down a steep grade to the buildings. 

The house was long and low, and Molly thought how comfortably cool it looked in the shade of the big trees. 

A low fence protected the generous yard and lawn from cattle. Phil and Molly clambered down from the pickup in the open space between the barn and the house and went through the gate, beyond which a stoneflagged path led to the porch. 

A white-haired lady with kind and knowing blue eyes answered Phil's knock and smiled good morning. "Yes, I'm Mrs. Wyman. Won't you come in? It's hot this morning, isn't it?"

They entered the large kitchen and dining room. "Yes, Mr. Wyman's here. He's not much better,

though. I don't know whether— What did you want to see him about?” "I-I'm sorry," Phil stammered. "I didn't know he was sick. Miss Wakefield and I were, uh, driving through, and we'd heard Mr. Wyman had some horses-' "Oh, if it's horses, I know he'll see you. Won't you take off your hat, Miss Wakefield? Yes, just hang them there." She opened the door leading to the next room and put her head through. “Oh, Jap. Here's two young people want to see you about some horses. All right?”

She turned back to the visitors and took off her apron. "He says come right in," and she led the way. "Jap's been pretty sick. You'll excuse him not getting up." 

Near one corner of the rather dark room, slowly rocking in a big chair, not turning his head but looking slantwise at them from a pair of cavernous eyes, sat a man who reminded Phil at once of Abraham Lincolnnot so tall, surely, but carved from dark granite. His thick hair and jutting brows were black; his shoulders and jaw were large and square; his nose strong and prominent. His square, bony hands shook a little on the arms of his chair. Nothing to guess his age by. "Pleased to meet you," he enunciated slowly in a voice that was still smooth and pleasant. "Sit down, won't you? There, in front of me. Just lay your crutches on the floor, Miss-I didn't get the name." "This is Molly Wakefield, Mr. Wyman," Phil said, "and I'm Philip Marshall. We, that is, I live on a ranch over on the other side of the Divide. Major Rutherford

DUDE RANCHER 

told me last spring that I ought to come over and see your horses." "Yes, we've got a few still. Don't know as there's anything you'd want, though. I'm cutting down since I been sick. What kind of horses did you want to buy?" "Oh, I don't see how we could buy any, right now, anyway," Phil said apologetically. "I wanted to ask you a few questions, if—" "Better see the horses first. Don't matter if you want to buy or not. Like to have you see 'em. Like to see 'em myself, coming in off the hill. We'll get the man to ride out and round up some." He looked at Phil. "Or maybe you'd like to ride out with him?” "Oh, yes!" Phil said. "How about you, Molly? Oh, I keep forgetting." 

Molly laughed. "I'm glad. But I'll be riding again in six months. You run along." "Yes," Mrs. Wyman looked at the clock, "you'll be late for dinner if you don't hurry. The young lady can stay and talk with me. You can do without her that long." "Oh, but we didn't-" Molly began. "Nothing else you can do," Mrs. Wyman went on. "It's too late for you to get to Craig or Meeker, either one. You'll just have to put up with what we have," and she shooed Phil out the door in the company of the husky, red-faced hired man. "Yes, sir," the hired man said after they had saddled and were riding up to the pasture, "Jap sure loves horses. Raised all we got and a good part o' what other fellas has around here. Take that one you're ridin': he's five-year-old by Cruzad out of a mare by old Judge

Denney. He's seven-eighths thoroughbred. You might not think it, blocky as he is; but Jap tries to breed 'em with a good middle. Got a turn o' speed, too. 

Cruzad never raced, but Judge Denney held the world's record for two miles fourteen years: three-twenty-six. 

We ain't got Cruzad no more. Had to change 'cause his daughters was gettin' to breedin' age. Secret 

Greetings name o' the horse we got now. Big horse. 

Show him to you when we git back. Now, all them yearlin's, two-year-olds you see are by him." 

Phil discovered that Cruzad's son did have a turn o' speed, and needed it, for the youngsters were full of life and in a mood to run. Even the old broodmares pretended to be alarmed and swung out of sight around the hill, with their gawky foals charging along behind them, arching their tails self-consciously. 

When at last they gave up the game, left their scruboak maze, and headed down the path to the corrals, Phil saw that the sound of their approach had been too much for Mr. Wyman. He was standing with Molly in the doorway, shielding his eyes with his hand. He even walked out, with such evident pain that Phil felt guilty, and led the way to Secret Greetings's paddock. 

The massive brown stallion trotted amiably up to the fence. "Go on in. Get acquainted," Jap said. "He's got a good disposition, like most of the Ultimus line."

Phil climbed over the rails and with his hands confirmed the impression his eyes had given. "Sixteenone?" he guessed and looked at Mr. Wyman, who nodded. "Heavy, too, and lots of bone. Twelve hundred pounds?"

DUDE RANCHER 

Mr. Wyman nodded again. “Might go a little better than that. Twelve-fifty, I guess, in the spring. Like his yearlings? I think they're pretty fair." 

Phil and Molly agreed that what they "had to put up with" for dinner was about the best meal they had ever tasted: fried home-cured ham and rich, smooth gravy; biscuits reached from the oven, with three kinds of homemade jam; Mrs. Wyman's pickled beets and cucumbers and onions; a cole slaw that filled Molly with such reverence that she did not ask how the dressing was made; last of all, apple pie and good strong coffee. "If you'd only let me know you were coming," Mrs. 

Wyman said, "I'd like to have got you a decent meal.” "Wow!" Phil commented with more relevance than dignity, and Molly apologized for his manners. "You said the 'Ultimus line,' Mr. Wyman," Phil was on his third cup of coffee, "Does that mean he's a son of a horse called Ultimus?" "No, it means in the line of male descent. His sire's sire was Ultimus. This horse is a son of Luke McLuke by Ultimus; good horse and great broodmare sire. Ultimus was full of great blood; never raced but he sired more performers and producers than any other stallion 

I can think of, right off. Ever study bloodlines?" "No, not really, I-" "You will, if you get in this game. I been at it forty years. No sense to it, I guess, out here a thousand miles from a track, but it gets you. I let some fellows I thought I could trust take some thoroughbreds o' mine once to race. They ruined the horses and robbed me.

Bad business. Since then all the racing we've done is around here, our own little fairs and such. That's fun enough for us.” "Do you raise cattle, too?" Phil asked. "Oh, sure." Jap tried to keep the amusement out of his voice. "What else would we have the horses for?

You don't see the cattle, 'cause they're still up on the government reserve, White River National Forest. 

Ranch runs right up against it." "Do you mean, then, that you use these three-quarterand seven-eighths-breds for all your work with the cattle?" "Sure." Jap showed that he was puzzled. 

Now we're getting to the point, Phil thought, and then he said aloud, "That's one of the things I wanted to ask you, Mr. Wyman. Do you find them satisfactory, cool enough, I mean, and intelligent, and hardy enough? People tell me they don't make such good cowhorses. Too big and too hot." "Listen!" A heavy finger was tapping Phil's knee. "I've tried 'em all. Y'understand? Forty years. And for a horse that has everything-speed, sense, balance, gait, strength-I'll take a three-quarter- or seven-eighths-bred that's been raised on a ranch. Yes, sir, every time. 

There's only one thing can beat 'em." "What's that?"

The old rancher straightened up in his chair and turned slightly, so that he was looking out toward the hills. “That, young man, is a pure, straight, registered thoroughbred, raised the same way. No mustang can touch him for any job a cowhorse has to do!" 

Phil expressed his astonishment, but persisted. "How

DUDE RANCHER 

about the climate, though, the snow and the long winters and the cold? The rough country?" "Listen!" This time the finger was tapping the corner of the table. "A range-raised thoroughbred can outlast, outstarve, outrustle the toughest mustang that was ever made--and grow a better, thicker coat o' wool when he needs it." "But Major Rutherford said-” "I know; know it all by heart. 'They got to be tested, or they'll go to seed.' Well, I don't suppose ours would show up so good on the big tracks, maybe; but when you been raisin' 'em and watchin' 'em and usin' 'em as long as I have, you ought to know pretty well how to test 'em and breed for what you want." "I've got an old thoroughbred gelding," Phil said. "At least, I think he must be a thoroughbred from the firing scars on his knees and ankles, and the way he runs. He's no good, though; crazy as a cottontail.” "Oh, Phil," Molly broke in, "you know you adore 

Dynamite as much as you do Pan." She turned to Jap. "He loves him more after every spill; but the horse really is getting more quiet since we've had him.”

If Phil had had his way, this would have been only a beginning; but Molly could see that Mr. Wyman was very tired and that his spirits were sustained only by his great enthusiasm. "We must go, Phil," she said, when she found a chance. "Oh, yes, of course." Phil came out of his trance, rose, and brought her crutches. “Oh, you don't want to go now," Mrs. Wyman objected. "You'd better spend the night and get an early start in the morning. We've plenty of room."

But Molly insisted, and they were off again. "Be sure to stop in and see us any time you're near,” 

Mrs. Wyman said at the gate. "You know, I think it does Jap good to talk with anybody loves horses like he does. But he is tired." "That," Phil breathed when they were out on the road again, “is a visit I'll never forget. Did you see how relieved he was when he learned we weren't going to buy any of his horses?-Mmm, Secret Greetings by 

Luke McWhat's-his-Name by Ultimus. I wonder how far back you can go. And don't you suppose any of them had mothers? Mmmm. I'll have to find out a lot about all that." "You've got it bad, haven't you, Phil?" "Mmmm! Guess I have. Don't know about that thoroughbred-cowpony heresy, but his opinion is certainly worth something. Swell, swell people! I hope he's better the next time we come over." Phil flushed but did not correct himself, just let it go at that. 

Before they had reached Steamboat Springs again, he spoke hesitantly, “Look, Molly, let's not go home by the same road tomorrow. I've been studying the map and found another way. It's a good deal longer, butuh-it seems to me there's something to be said for that." He came to a stop beside the highway and took the map from his pocket. "Look. I bet we can make it in a day. See this road leading southwest from 

Kremmling over to Wolcott, then up the Eagle River- 

Leadville-Dillon-Loveland Pass-and home." "But if we're going that far out of the way," Molly put her finger on Wolcott, "we ought to go downstream from here to Glenwood Springs and see that part of the

DUDE RANCHER 

cañon of the Colorado. I hear it's wonderful! We could get up about three and be in Glenwood in time for breakfast."

Phil's temperature rose with pleasure at this coöperation greater than he had hoped for. "H-m-m-m, let's see, twenty-four, plus thirty-one, plus-it's only about 

135 or 140 miles from Steamboat." 

When, after a quick breakfast, they turned back homeward from Glenwood Springs, it was already nine o'clock. But only here and there did the morning sun hit the road, for the perpendicular cliffs rose hundreds of feet from the turbulent river. In every break in the rocks of the south wall moss and evergreens and dark ferns hid in perpetual shade. Water dripped from the cracks. But on the north side the sun hit the wall near the top and was reflected down in a hot glare of modulated terra cotta. And the sky between the light and dark edges was a bottomless blue.

Molly and Phil sat hypnotized and perhaps a little chilled, until, after several miles, the cañon suddenly broadened into a warm upland valley. Then Molly shifted into the corner of her seat to straighten the bad leg and to get a better look at Phil's profile, a good deal leaner and more coppery than when she had first seen it. "A penny," she said at last. "Huh?" Phil grunted, coming out of his trance. He looked at her and smiled, suddenly conscious of the long silence. "I was offering a penny for your thoughts. There were waves of something or other passing over your brow, and your lips were moving. Were you building

castles? or just that new games-room you've been talking about?" "Neither one," Phil replied. "Fact is, I was thinking about Hap." "Oh-what about him?” "I was thinking we ought to nab him. We're going to need more staff next summer, and I bet I could get him to come now. If I don't, he'll probably be off to

Wyoming again. He does quickly and well the things I do poorly or not at all. He'd be good with the summer people, and think of what his ax could do this winter!- 

It may take the next fifty years, but I'm going to turn that place into something that will make your eyes pop.' 

"Confident, aren't you, Phil?" "Certainly I'm confident. Have to act that way, anyhow." "Successful, too?" 

Here he caught her tone of banter. “Oh, sure. I have everything: youth; beauty; a big note at the bank; two big bills for supplies (which I hope don't come to the banker's notice until I've hit him for an extension); a share in forty emaciated yearlings that have to be dumped on the market because everybody's grass is shot and he has to do the same; a precarious fingernail-hold on a peewee dude ranch that lacks almost everything it requires to run as a dude ranch should; three pairs of overalls that would get me arrested if I weren't living in the wilderness; and prospects of a winter on corn-meal mush, if we can only get some corn-meal. Am I successful?" "Well, are you?"

DUDE RANCHER 

"Yes." "Of course you are. You love what you are doing. 

You look ahead. You don't get discouraged because your plans never come through on schedule. You feel that you belong. Dad used to say that it would take the full time of fourteen men to keep the ranch looking shipshape. The result was that no one did even the little that he could have done. Things stayed the same or grew worse." "Your father seems better satisfied, to me, since he cut out all pretense of ranching. Maybe a tourist hotel is just the thing for him." "No, it's not that. I don't know what it is. Maybe he's got the idea of a gold mine out of his system at last, stopped counting on it, now that the land is practically out of his control and he realizes he can't redeem it. Mother told me, when I stopped with her in 

June, that Dad had been almost hinting that she come back. I wouldn't be surprised if she did, someday. I think it was Dad's crippling obsession that sent her away in the first place, that and Buck." "What about Buck? You'd think your father would have booted him out long ago." "For a long time it puzzled me. Then I decided it was just that Buck had an arrogance and swagger that Dad envied a little and that he thought were part of the right atmosphere. Now that I'm sure Dad is fed up with him, it's hard to understand, again. I shouldn't say this, I suppose, but I have a strange feeling that, as if, as if Buck had some sort of hold over him, perhaps something 'on' him, as they say." "I'll bet you're romancing about that last. I don't

think Buck would have the imagination to work out such a thing, not the tenacity to keep it under his hat for years. "He had imagination enough to think big things about the old gold yarn, anyway, and he kept that under his hat, or thought he did. Grandfather used to see him out chipping away at the rocks; told me all about it. Usually he just laughed, but once he splattered a rifle-ball on the rock above Buck's head. He told me Buck bolted down the slope like a scared jack rabbit, didn't even think of his horse tied to the tree, sent a hired man out to get him later. There isn't much Grandfather has missed, prowling every inch of the country all his life.” "What do you think of the gold story, Molly?" 

Molly looked quizzically at Phil for an instant, saw that he was only curious, and answered simply, “I don't think there's anything in it. I'm sure of it, in fact, because, if there had been, Grandfather would have gone after it. Not perhaps in the old days when Dad was trying to build up his dude ranch-Grandfather's heart was never in that. But now, certainly, when you and he so desperately need cash to get things started right." "You think his heart is in what we're doing now?" "Of course. Don't you?" "Yes. And just because I do, I'm sure you're wrong in your reasons for deciding the gold is all a myth, though I devoutly hope you're right in the fact. As a woman, Molly, you probably can't understand this one little thing about Pop: he loves the country, the outof-doors, every little unspoiled gulch, every pine and

DUDE RANCHER 

boulder-not just all out-of-doors, but this that he has made and that has made him. "Two years ago, I would have had no interest in a gold development scheme for itself, because it wasn't my line. I might not have minded cashing in in one place and going somewhere else to use the cash; but now I'm like Pop. I'd want to stand guard with a cannon to see that no one, for any reason whatever, came in and started tearing up the hills. You ought to read 

Lewis Mumford's chapter on mining in Technics and

Civilization." "Why did you say 'as a woman'? Don't you think women love the land, too?" "Sure they do; almost as much as a man, sometimes.

But a woman is more practical, more realistic than a man. Pop's reasons and mine simply aren't good reasons. I know that, and it makes no difference. With you, if it ever came to a test, you would view it differently. You proved it just now when you said that

Pop's failure to act showed that there was nothing to act for. . . . Suppose someone were to offer Pop a fantastic amount for the ranch tomorrow, say half a million dollars. You think he ought to sell out, don't you?" "Why, yes, he ought to; but—” "That 'but' is half the secret of being a dude rancher and more than half the secret of Pop and me. Still, I realize it proves a fundamental childishness, that probably affects us in other things, to our damage. We're the sort of business men, I imagine, that should have a guardian appointed." "You've got Minnie Brush."

"Yes, isn't she wonderful? But her loyalty is so savage she thinks everyone is trying to do us. She'd have us in a continual feud with all the people who sell us anything. I don't dare leave the invoices with the groceries any more. And all our guests are strong for her, once they catch on."

Molly sat back rather sadly and watched the advance of fall, a week or two for each thousand feet they rose. 

Phil was sad, too, thinking of the winter that would soon close down; of the short few miles back to the ranch and the end of the outing; of Molly's inevitable departure to her place of importance and a future with that man Lloyd. If only the old car would break down this side of the pass! But it churned to the top with only one or two stops for cooling, and there they were at 12,000 feet, the only living beings in a waste of timeless rock. Already the peaks wore a light dusting of new snow, and even the pale sunlight glancing from their riven planes was cold and hard. 

Phil stopped at the summit. "Mind if I pause and look around?" he asked. "I think Congress ought to meet up here, just one day a session. Can you imagine 

Senator Flatus A. Burble orating at that?" He opened the door on his side. "Would you get cold sitting while 

I went out to the edge there and peeked over?” "If there's going to be any peeking over of edges, I'm coming, too," Molly said. It seemed natural to lift her from the running board to the ground and to give her a supporting arm across the road to the brink. 

The stiff west breeze drove a scud of cloud across the sun, and sharp horizontal snowflakes stung the skin. "Brr!" they both said at once, laughed, and started

DUDE RANCHER 

back for the world.

Rolling down off the pass, Phil was silent once more, and Molly sat with her coat turned up around her ears. 

But down among the pines his speech returned. "Molly," he said impulsively in a voice that he knew 

I was thin and cracked. "What, Phil?" 

He coughed, "Molly"-that was a little more like his own voice-"I don't want you to go back East." There was no response. "Molly," he repeated, "I don't want you to go back East." It sounded like a small boy complaining about the rain on Saturday. "Why not, silly?” ""Why not?' 'Why not?' As if you didn't know!" His words came in a rush now. "Because I love you, see? 

You're going away, and I haven't a chance against that career of yours. Even if I had, I wouldn't have any right to talk like this, with nothing but corn-meal mush ahead. But I love you, and I want to be with you always, and . . ." He stepped on the gas, and the pickup swayed wildly around a pair of sharp curves- "I'm glad I got it off my chest.-Now: do you think that a partner's overcall in your suit is rightly forcing?

Or do you think there are cases where it may be disregarded, to mislead an overventuresome opponent, for instance?" "Don't be a fool, Phil. You know what I think of bridge. But there's one thing about me you don't seem to realize." "What's that?” “Why, I think corn-meal mush is quite a career, not the white, but the yellow, you know."

"What!" There was a screech of brakes, and one worn tire gave way under the sudden stress. The old pickup slithered to the edge of the road and stopped at a crazy angle.

Later, when they were humming down the good road of the cañon west of Mineral Spring, Molly said, “Phil, don't you think it would be safer if you drove with both arms?" "Seems inappropriately sane and conservative," Phil replied. "Still, I don't suppose you'd be able to stir the lumps out of the mush so well if I ran you into a tree and smashed you up again.” 

So they drove very slowly, not to risk another blowout, and talked of the things they'd do to the ranch. 

Maybe not next year or year after, but sometime. And 

Phil realized how much better "we" was than "I" in the grammar of planning. There ought to be tennis courts and some place to swim. A little track for schooling horses and training kids. Polo field, too, later, maybe, if we can find a spot flat enough. Electric system pretty soon: the combination of dudes and kerosene lamps is about the biggest fire hazard you can think of. Should we advertise? Join the dude ranchers' association? Don't we need a big storehouse to keep supplies cool? A refrigerating plant? Wonder who holds the title to that 800 acres Dad sold to Jackson and he let go for taxes. That would give us two miles more of creek bottom, fair pasture, fine timber. "And I'm with you, too, Phil, about the garden. We ought to have a much larger one. Why, even this summer those things you planted did beautifully up until the middle of July, when the drought got to be too

DUDE RANCHER 

much for them. With good rain, we could have all the vegetables we'd need and lots to put up for winter. 

And I'm going to have flowers around the house, and a lawn." "Yes, and I'll build you a fence around it to keep the calves out, and I'll transplant young cottonwoods for shade-a real yard. Won't it look funny, a defiant half-note in the symphony of green! No, that doesn't make sense: I'm so excited I'm getting maudlin. I can't believe it has all happened.—But say, who's going to tell Pop? Not much of a life for a woman, he says. I wonder whether he'll want to scalp me." "He'd better not try! But let's not tell him for a few days. I'll pick a time.” "How about your mother?" "She knows. I wrote her last month confirming the explanation I had to give in June, when she found I'd bought only a one-way ticket." "What!" "You didn't think it was all your idea, did you? I had it long ago. And I knew pretty well about you before you left me in New York last winter. If you hadn't told me before we got back to the ranch today, I'd probably have told you, and I'd have hated you for it, too, you old turtle." 

Pop was no fun, didn't wait to be told. "What are you two doing out there on the porch?" he shouted severely some time after their late supper. "Looking at the moon, Pop," Phil replied truthfully. "Well, it's about time! If that there trip hadn'ta worked, like Minnie said it would, I was fixin' to rub your noses together, soon 's you got back. But I seen

I didn't have to, when you come in lookin' like a couple o' sick calves. G'night!”

Molly rose and hobbled across the porch to look in. "I think you are a nasty, conceited, meddlesome old man; and I can't see how Phil and I are going to stand you around our ranch!" 

And she really had the last word, because Pop's "Teehee" was lost in the bang of the door.

THE END
